Its main product is Cerner Millennium, which is an EHR platform, allowing the clinicians or hospitals to view patient health data in real-time. Cerner Health Facts® EMR database comprises de-identified EHR data from over 700 participating Cerner client hospitals and clinics in the United States, including patients’ demographics, encounters, diagnoses, procedures, lab results, medications, vital signs, and other clinical observations. The version of the Cerner database that we acquired contains medical records for 69 million unique patients from 2000–2018, which is a good resource to conduct big data analysis for research and scientific discoveries.


3.3 EHR/EMR Database Design and Structure: Database Queries

Most EHR/EMR database systems are based on relational database design. Oracle and Microsoft SQL server are among the most commonly used Database Management Systems (DBMS) employed by most EHR/EMR vendors. When healthcare data is acquired or purchased from vendors, the data format is usually in text files (or csv files). Similarly, publicly available and free healthcare data often exists in text file formats. For example, when we purchased the 2018 version of the Cerner Health Facts database, we received the EHR data in csv files. Furthermore, some vendors, including Cerner, provide design details of their database structure through data dictionaries and database schema diagrams. The data dictionary document that we received from Cerner explains the data sets and the data elements within the Health Facts, and the database schema defines the database tables, fields and relationships. Using the details of the database design, one can recreate a database system to host and manage healthcare data for research purposes.

3.3.1 Database Construction

In general, a database system development process can be divided into three stages: database design, implementation, and database maintenance. Database design starts with a conceptual data model and produces a specification of a logical schema. The output of this stage is a detailed relational specification, the logical schema, of all the tables and constraints needed to satisfy the description of the data in the conceptual data model. Implementation includes the construction of a database according to the specification of a logical schema. This will include the specification of an appropriate storage schema, security enforcement, external schema and so on. Finally, database maintenance, including operational maintenance, is to ensure that performance of the database system is maintained at an acceptable level, which requires effective communications among the team members to determine and implement changes as needed.

The design and construction of an EHR database system for research purposes should follow the description of the schema document received from the vendor. A traditional relational DB design is most common, but a distributed DB design is also an option for efficient data management.

3.3.2 Traditional Relational Database System

Database systems based on the relational data model are most commonly used in healthcare data management. In a relational model, data is organized in tables, linked with relationships, and controlled by constraints. Famous examples of relational database management systems include Oracle, Microsoft SQL server and PostgreSQL.

The steps for creating your own database system to manage healthcare data include,

  • Setup database environment (database server configuration)

  • Create schema and tables

  • Populate tables with data

  • Operate database server (run server process)

3.3.3 Distributed Database System

As database sizes keep rapidly increasing, in order to improve the performance of data manipulation, online analytical processing (OLAP) and online transaction processing (OLTP), the Apache Hadoop was developed as a framework for running applications on large clusters from commodity hardware. Hadoop provides a distributed file system (HDFS) that stores data on compute nodes, and implements a computational paradigm named Map/Reduce, where the application is divided into several small fragments of work, each may be executed or re-executed on any node in the cluster. The Apache HBase, established on Hadoop HDFS, provides the capability to read/write big data randomly in real-time. As an open-source, distributed non-relational database management system, HBase modeled after Google’s Bigtable: A Distributed Storage System for Structured Data, developed by Chang et al. (Fay et al., 2008).

Unlike traditional SQL databases (e.g., Oracle, Microsoft SQL, MYSQL, PostgreSQL), the NoSQL databases (e.g., HBase, MongoDB, Cassandra DB) provide more flexible solution on unstructured data (e.g., photos, videos, documents and articles). They are generally document-oriented. In this way, the unstructured data can be stored in a single document that can be easily found, but it is not necessarily categorized into fields in relational databases.

For the Cerner EHR database, we employed the HBase framework mainly for performance concern, since many tables are very large with billions of records. In an HBase Cluster, the query and analysis performance are significantly enhanced. The comparison shows ~7x speed improvement on a two-node cluster.


3.4 Data Extraction

3.4.1 Define Inclusion/Exclusion Criteria for Data Extraction

EHR/EMR data present a wealth of valuable information for researchers. Tapping into this potential necessitates collaboration between domain experts (i.e., clinicians, healthcare experts and physicians) and data analysts as a starting point in EHR research. This collaboration should stimulate and produce some interesting topics to study, which is not trivial. Most of the time we, as statisticians and data scientists, do not have a clear scientific question in mind. On the other hand, domain experts may have enough scientific knowledge to identify important questions or hypotheses in a biomedical or clinical field, but they may not know whether the data are good enough or analytic methods are available to address the questions or hypotheses. It is essential for domain experts and statisticians/data scientists to closely work together to identify good and important questions and hypotheses. This process sometimes is not easy and may require multiple iterations until a good question or hypothesis can be identified. More discussions on this point can also be found in Chapter 1 (Section 1.2).

Once the questions or hypotheses are determined and the objective of the project is clear, initial data extraction and data exploration can begin. In order to extract the appropriate data for the targeted questions, we need to clearly define inclusion/exclusion criteria for data extraction. The initial inclusion/exclusion criteria for data extraction can be simple but must be refined later after further data exploration and data cleaning. If the identified question or hypothesis is regarding a disease, all the diagnosis codes (e.g., ICD-9 codes) related to this disease need to be identified so that all the relevant data from all the patients with these diagnosis codes can be extracted from the EHR/EMR database. Sometimes this may not be trivial since there are many disease diagnosis codes possibly related to the disease and it may not be easy to identify all the diagnosis codes or ICD-9 codes by keyword search. Some domain knowledge related to the disease is needed to identify all synonyms of the disease. In this case, some natural language processing (NLP) approaches can also be used for this purpose. Sometimes a patient is likely a confirmed case of disease by reviewing his/her records of medications, treatment procedures or lab test results, but the disease diagnosis code or ICD-9 code of the disease did not appear in the patient’s record due to missing data or other errors. In this case, more patients related to this particular disease can be possibly identified through medication, procedure and lab test tables. However, it needs domain knowledge to identify the medications, procedures and lab test results, which are almost uniquely related to this disease. Finally, it is necessary to use rigorous procedures and approaches to confirm the cases of disease diagnosis via formal disease phenotyping, which will be discussed in the next subsection.

Some other inclusion/exclusion criteria may also involve patient demographics such as age, gender or race, or the data from other EHR/EMR tables such as medication, procedure, lab test, clinical event, vital sign or microbiology tables. Some of these data or variables are quite accurate and complete, some others may have high uncertainty, high missing or error rate. It is quite challenging to accurately identify the targeted cohort of patients that meet all inclusion/exclusion criteria.

3.4.2 Phenotyping: Cohort Identification

One of the fundamental steps when extracting data from an EHR database is to define the cohort of patients. In particular, if the cohort is defined based on disease diagnosis, it is known as electronic “phenotyping”. Phenotyping has important applications across different study designs (such as cross-sectional studies, case-control studies, and experimental studies) to identify cohorts of patients with characteristics of interest. Moreover, phenotyping is also used in the field of bioinformatics. This application of linking genomic data with EHR-derived phenotypes is called phenomics and discussed in detail in the human phenome project (Denny 2013; Freimer and Sabatti, 2003).

Nevertheless, due to input errors, biased billing codes, missingness, and many other factors, correctly identifying interested study cohort is not trivial. Depending on the EHR data types, electronic phenotyping algorithms can range from the basic rule-based methods on structured data to advanced methods using unsupervised models on unstructured data (Table 3.2). Basically, the general methods of phenotyping include rule-based methods, machine learning (e.g., natural language processing (NLP), supervised learning methods, latent variable methods, unsupervised learning methods), and hybrid approaches combining different strategies (e.g., rule-based and machine learning).

  1. Rule-Based Methods
    When we start data extraction, the most straightforward approach is to utilize the International Classification of Diseases (ICD) billing codes of the target disease to obtain data from the EHR database system. Since 1999, researchers have been using ICD-9 codes to identify patients with acute myocardial infarction (Petersen et al. 1999). But ICD codes are for billing purposes and they may not reflect the true status of more complicated chronic diseases which may lead to many unexpected misclassification errors. Hence, we need to consider more variables such as lab results, diagnosis codes, and medications when developing rules or definitions to identify the targeted cohort of patients. In reality, the process of developing phenotyping rules usually involves one or more clinicians specifying inclusion and exclusion criteria based on a structured EHR database that has clear diagnosis codes. Developing phenotype criteria is a tedious process that can take 6–8 months to complete (Kirby et al. 2016). Due to their complicated nature, it is important to share phenotypes publicly for validation and implementation by other healthcare organizations and research institutes. That was the reason for the creation of PheKB (Phenotype Knowledgebase). This is a repository of phenotypes that includes 44 open-sourced phenotypes of different diseases. A majority of phenotypes on PheKB comes from the eMERGE (Electronic Medical Records and Genomics) network. This network is a collaboration of multiple medical centers that focus on developing EHR phenotype definitions for genomic research (Gottesman et al. 2013). eMERGE is responsible for a large catalog of phenotypes, including type 2 diabetes, cardiovascular disease, and sclerosis (Kho et al. 2012, Ritchie et al. 2010). For example, to identify patients with type 2 diabetes (T2DM), investigators at Northwestern University developed an eMERGE definition for type 2 diabetes (excluding T1DM cases) by taking into account an HbA1c above 6.5%, at least one T2DM diagnosis code and at least one hypoglycemic medication. This definition produces a 98% positive predictive value for the identification of T2DM patients compared with clinician review after validating at three different sites (Kho et al. 2012). Therefore, the eMERGE phenotype is considered a reliable algorithm for T2DM classification in many case-control/cohort studies.

  2. Machine Learning Methods
    Due to the high missing rate of EHR data in many clinical settings, the rule-based approach may have low sensitivity. Furthermore, the rule-based approach is not generalizable to other diseases. For example, the definition of diabetes identification cannot be used on hyperthyroidism. With the enormous number of diseases and syndromes, the method of rule-based phenotyping is not efficient although it is more reliable with high specificity.

    In order to achieve high-throughput phenotyping, a growing number of studies have used machine learning methods to learn the pattern from the data and label the patients automatically. Since 2013 researchers started focusing more on adopting standardized terminologies in concept extraction and on developing phenotyping systems using unsupervised machine learning and deep learning methods. NLP analysis of unstructured clinical notes is another direction to determine the disease cohort. According to Martin-Sanchez et al., 80% of data captured in EHRs recorded are unstructured data (clinical notes, pathology reports, and discharge summaries). Clinical notes include rich information including diagnosis, medication, etc.. NLP is a useful technique for electronic phenotyping and often integrated with the rule-based method or machine learning methods. Xu et al. (2010) developed a new natural language processing system (MedEx), which helps in extracting medication information from clinical notes. Soysal et al. (2017) created CLAMP (Clinical Language Annotation, Modeling, and Processing Toolkit) as a multipurpose NLP tool. Open Health NLP Consortium (OHNLP) is a public resource where researchers can find these NLP tools including the ones we mentioned earlier. Using these NLP tools, Carroll et al. (2011) combined NLP-derived mentions of concepts and medications with structured data to create a support vector machine (SVM) classifier for rheumatoid arthritis. There are many other use cases of machine learning methods for phenotyping. Hubbard et al. (2019) introduced a Bayesian latent phenotyping approach that deals with imperfect and missing data when there are no gold-standard data available. Huang et al. (2004) used a process called FSSMC (feature selection via supervised model construction) to reduce 410 structured variables down to 47 variables, and then rank these features to determine the features that had the highest performance in identifying diabetes patients. Glicksberg et al. (2018) compared AUC performance using different machine learning methods to phenotype Hepatorenal Syndrome. Beaulieu-Jones and Greene (2016) applied the embedding method to define the disease cohort, and employed denoising autoencoders to achieve the goal of phenotyping.

    Besides standard ML models, deep learning was also adopted for the purpose of phenotyping. The advantage of deep learning methods is that it does not require any feature selection and can learn the patterns inside a big EHR dataset with a large feature space and a large number of patients. Lipton et al. (2015) used Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) for multilabel classification of 128 diagnoses. Lasko et al. (2013) performed a deep learning method on dirty longitudinal clinical data using Gaussian process regression, producing the final phenotyping model with AUC of 0.97. More use cases and reviews of deep learning methods in EHR can be found in Alzoubi et al. (2019).

    In the unsupervised learning approach, the unlabeled data are clustered into different groups of patients with unknown medical conditions using Limestone (a technique of nonnegative tensor factorization technique) (Ho et al. 2014). The authors of this method then asked medical experts to review and validate the conditions of top groups of phenotype candidates as explained in their study.

  3. Hybrid Methods
    One good advantage of machine learning and other statistical methods equipped with model selection techniques for phenotyping is that they are generalizable to many diseases. But the shortcoming is a lack of efficient validation of the classification results. Researchers often use the rule-based method as the gold standard for labeling and then build ML-based phenotyping models based on the labeled training dataset. Li et al. (2013) used labeled data derived from eMERGE definitions for T2DM patients and then trained the Apriori algorithm on the labeled data to develop a phenotyping algorithm called ARM (association rule mining). This algorithm is considered a hybrid approach (combining the rule-based methods with machine learning methods) and shows a better performance against the rule-based performance alone. Similarly, Carroll, Yu et al. (2015) used medical concepts from two public sites Medscape and Wikipedia as the gold standards to select features from EHR. The proposed features are filtered by keeping only the features mentioned in existing clinical notes of EHRs using the NLP. Eventually, the authors added ICD-9 codes and additional notes into the feature space to build the final logistic regression model.

Table 3.2 Phenotyping Methods and Data Types

Method Structured Data Unstructured Data
Rule-based ✓
Machine learning
NLP ✓
Machine learning ✓ ✓
Deep learning ✓ ✓
Unsupervised learning ✓ ✓
Hybrid ✓ ✓

Whether the phenotyping is rule-based, machine learning-based or hybrid approach, the final identification of the disease diagnosis should be validated by clinical expert reviewers. For further automation to reduce the manual review process from medical experts, Henderson et al. (2017) developed PheKnow–Cloud that allows researchers to automate the validation process of the output phenotypes using unsupervised learning of medical literature. Electronic phenotyping is an emerging field and an essential step for EHR research. Therefore, in the upcoming years, it is expected to see many more phenotyping methods and tools beyond the ones discussed in this section.

If the targeted cohort is defined not only by a disease diagnosis, the inclusion/exclusion criteria may also involve other variables in the EHR database such as patient demographics (age, gender or race), medications, procedures, lab tests, clinical events, vital signs or microbiology tests, similar approaches for disease phenotyping can be employed. However, uncertainties and data errors (including missing data issues) need to be carefully considered. The literature in this is sparse and more future work on this topic is warranted.


3.5 Data Extraction Report

Once a cohort of patients are identified and relevant data are then extracted from the EHR database, it is essential to generate summary statistics with tables, plots and figures. A summary report is an efficient way to facilitate collaboration and communication among research collaborators. For that reason, a summary report is highly recommended. Below we share with the readers a summary report template to highlight the main components that a report should include.

Purpose of this report:

Serve to provide an initial communication with clinical or epidemiological domain-specific experts to identify scientific questions or hypotheses

Data extraction criteria:

Clearly specify initial inclusion/exclusion criteria that were used to extract the data based on which this report is generated.

Basic summary statistics:

Tables and figures with detailed captions

  1. Disease types or sub-types: patient distributions and by calendar year

  2. Race distribution and disease prevalence distribution

  3. Gender distribution and disease prevalence distribution

  4. Age distribution and disease prevalence distribution

  5. Spatial region distributions and disease prevalence distributions:

    • Number of patients

    • Number of hospitals

  6. Hospital distribution

  7. Number of encounters: distribution per patient and by calendar year

  8. Time length of data intervals (from the first time of any event to the last time of any event for a patient): distribution per patient

  9. Mortality rate distributions

  10. The number of following counts and distributions by patients and by calendar year

    • Disease diagnosis (distinct ICD-9 and/or ICD-10 codes): comorbidity distributions

    • Medications

    • Procedures

    • Surgical procedures

    • Clinical events

    • Lab tests

    • Microbiology tests

  11. Patient type description distribution (inpatient or outpatient) and by calendar year

Project-Specific summary statistics:

Example 1: All SAH project—summary statistics for the following items

  1. CT-scan test

  2. Clipping and coiling treatments

  3. Vasopressor treatments

  4. Complications

    • Cerebral Vasospasm (CV)

    • Delayed cerebral ischemia (DCI)

Example 2: Diabetes

  1. Glucose level

  2. HbA1c level

Research Plan:

  • • Potential scientific questions or hypotheses if any

  • • Plan for next step


3.6 Illustration Example: Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (SAH) Project

3.6.1 EHR Database Design and Construction

Below we share our experience in designing and constructing a database system to host and manage Cerner EHR data. Although most healthcare database systems, including the Cerner EHR system, are based on Oracle or Microsoft SQL server, one can choose any existing database tools to build your database management system. We employed PostgreSQL, an object-relational database system that is free and open-source with the features of traditional proprietary database systems [distributed under a license similar to BSD and MIT]. The installation binary can be downloaded from https://www.postgresql.org/download/ for the operating system of your choice.

Here are the steps needed to construct a database system to manage and query healthcare data:

  • Setup database: You can use a Docker container to deploy PostgreSQL. Containerization is popular because containers are flexible, lightweight, portable, scalable and secure.

  • Create schema and tables: EHR/EMR vendors may provide DB schema in SQL files to build the definitions of tables. However, you may need to make a few changes to the statements to match your choice of DBMS. For example, Cerner EHR system uses Oracle, if you use PostgreSQL, then you need to replace NUMERIC(P,S) with DECIMAL(P,S).

  • Upload data files: Once the SQL files are prepared, you can run them from a client tool such as, pgAdmin or DataGrip. The client tool can also be used to load csv files to the database table. However, if the csv file is very large or when there are many large csv files waiting for import, creating a batch script to do the job will be more efficient.

  • Query the database: When a database query is executed, a procedure usually fetches all the records from the server process and then transfers them to the client process. If the query returns a huge amount of data, a proportionally large amount of memory will be allocated by the client. If the dataset is too large to be practically handled on the client-side, it is possible to create a server-side procedure. This will make it possible to transfer only a controlled amount of data to the client, so that a large dataset can be examined without keeping it entirely in memory.

3.6.2 SAH Cohort Identification and Data Extraction

To illustrate the process of cohort identification and data extraction from an EHR database, we share the following example from our project, subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) treatment analysis and prediction.

Our cohort of patients with SAH was first defined using the traditional rule-based approach: all related pieces of data from the Cerner EHR database (i.e., patient encounters, diagnoses, medications, etc.) that are specific to one disease are obtained by identifying ICD-9/10 codes. We adopt two ways to get the ICD-9/10 codes: 1) keyword search in the diagnosis description; and 2) find corresponding ICD-9/10 codes online or from clinicians. Once we determined the ICD codes of SAH, then all information tables can be derived by utilizing the relationships between different identifiers and the ICD codes. For example, we can first extract all the encounters that contain those ICD codes of SAH. Then use the encounter IDs in the extracted encounters to extract all the tables with regard to medications, procedures, clinical events, lab procedures and so on.

Although some literature discussed and listed ICD codes for SAH, in order to extract as many relevant ICD codes as possible for our project, we conducted keyword searches against the diagnosis description in the EHR database. Eventually, 192 unique ICD-9/10 codes related to SAH are collected. The unique ICD codes are then categorized into six code groups based on causes, symptoms and characteristics. See Table 3.3 below.

Table 3.3 Code Groups and Their Descriptions

ICD Codes Description
800.X-804.X Fracture of skull with subarachnoid, subdural, and extradural hemorrhage
430/I60.X Nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage
P52.5/P10.3/772.2 Baby-related subarachnoid hemorrhage
852.0–852.2 Subarachnoid hemorrhage following injury
S06.6X Traumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage
I69.0X Sequelae following nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage

After a discussion with clinicians, we classified ICD code group “800.X–804.X” and “852.0–852.2” as traumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage and deleted patients diagnosed with “P52.5/P10.3/772.2” because those codes are related to infants. So filtered ICD code groups and their descriptions are as follows (see Table 3.4).

Table 3.4 Combined ICD Code Groups and Their Descriptions

ICD Codes Description
800.X-804.X/S06.6X/852.0–852.2 Traumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage
430/I60.X Nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage
I69.0X Sequelae following nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage

However, traumatic SAH and nontraumatic SAH present different clinical symptoms and complications. To treat the two types of SAH as the same disease by doing subsequent analysis will generate biased results. We also are interested in patients who were admitted to hospitals with SAH, instead of sequelae following SAH. To further refine the cohort for analysis, we also dropped the patients with the sole diagnosis code of sequelae following nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage. One problem showed up after checking the historical SAH diagnosis for individual patients. A specific patient may have different combinations of SAH ICD code groups listed in Table 3.4. Here historical SAH diagnosis means SAH diagnosis records presented in the patient’s historical encounters. See Figure 3.1 for the number of patients falling into different combinations of SAH ICD code groups.

From Figure 3.1 we can see that most patients had only one type of SAH diagnosis. But there are still a significant number of patients with both traumatic SAH diagnosis and nontraumatic SAH diagnosis or other combinations of SAH diagnosis codes. If the targeted cohort is just one type of SAH, can we simply drop the patients with over two types of SAH diagnosis? We believe that simply dropping patients from the analysis will introduce bias or produce misleading results for cohort studies. To better address this problem, we may consider using the first SAH encounter data for all patients. See Figure 3.2 for the number of patients whose first SAH diagnosis fell into different combinations of SAH ICD code groups.

In this SAH project, we finally restricted our analysis of patients whose first SAH diagnosis was nontraumatic SAH. We treated patients with both a diagnosis code group of sequelae following nontraumatic SAH and a diagnosis code group of nontraumatic SAH as nontraumatic SAH patients. In this project, we also focused on adult SAH patients (>17 years old) and patients’ age was used to define the cohort. Finally, we obtained our initial cohort with a sample size of 29,889 nontraumatic SAH adult patients.

We extracted the data for adult patients who had at least one SAH diagnosis in the record, which was crude, since a patient with one SAH code in his/her medical record may not be a confirmed SAH patient. A gold standard for a confirmed SAH diagnosis is to review CT scan data that we were not able to access. Thus, it is possible that this initial cohort may contain patients who were not confirmed SAH patients or even those who were not adults (17 years or younger) due to errors. A formal phenotyping approach described in Section 3.4.2 above can be used to further refine the cohort. In order to do this, a training dataset needs to be available. As we discussed above, first a rule-based algorithm can be developed by collaborations with clinicians and SAH experts so that a training dataset can be established. Then a machine learning approach can be employed to confirm all the patients in the cohort.

3.6.3 Data Extraction Report

We can automate the generation of data extraction reports (see Section 3.5 above), which will make it reproducible. Examples of programming tools that one can use to write reproducible reports include R Markdown and Python Jupyter notebook. Below we share our own experience in producing the summary statistics report automatically.

The first step is to install nbconvert package and related environment settings under your operation system. You can refer to https://nbconvert.readthedocs.io/en/latest/ for more details. In order to make the figures and tables displayed nicely in the report, you can copy the following codes into the Jupyter notebook cell.

Once you finish the coding and get the outputs, you can input “jupyter nbconvert –to pdf –no-input ‘ipynb file location’” in the commend interface, like Anaconda Prompt or Server Bash. Since your collaborators might not be interested in the codes, set the configuration argument to “–no-input” is useful in hiding the codes from the report. Please make sure that your nbconvert is most updated. You can update the package by typing “conda update nbconvert” in your Anaconda prompt or Server Bash. nbconvert has many other functions. It can help convert the Jupyter notebook into other formats, such as html, slides and etc. Also, it can convert the Jupyter notebook into a report template that you have developed. Language Jinja will be utilized to write the template. Readers could learn more from https://www.datacamp.com/community/tutorials/jinja2-custom-export-templates-jupyter.

3.6.4 Potential Data Extraction Pitfalls and Errors with Solutions

We have experienced some problems and encountered some errors while working on our SAH project. We share some of these experiences here.

Errors found in the EHR database: It is unavoidable to contain errors in any database. For example,

  • One encounter may map to multiple patient identifiers, which is clearly an error.

  • One patient identifier may have multiple races or genders due to errors.

Mistakes we made in the process of data extraction include:

  • When extracting the first encounter of SAH patients based on the admission time, the admission time of some encounters may be the same due to errors. The first encounter cannot be distinguished. Similarly, the time stamps for medications, lab tests or procedures may contain errors or possibly missing, we should be cautious to extract the data specified by time stamps.

4

EHR Data Cleaning
               

Yashar Han Yuefan Vahed TalebiFengHuangMaroufy


4.1 Introduction

Data cleaning (also called data cleansing or scrubbing) is the process of detecting and fixing errors and inconsistencies in datasets to reduce heterogeneity and increase the quality of data. It is the second step, following data extraction, in the process of preparing data for statistical analysis and/or model building. Despite being labor-intensive and time-consuming, data cleaning is extremely important for any modeling and analysis based on EHR databases. However, it is sometimes overlooked by researchers, and in such cases, the inconsistencies and errors may have significant influence on the precision and accuracy of the results.

Cleaning EHR data is different from other cleaning methods for big data and small data studies. Unlike big data analysis based on EHR databases, in a traditional clinical research setting, such as experimental studies and clinical trials, the data collection is controlled, hence data cleaning, if required, is not a tedious task. On the other hand, cleaning the data from other big data management systems may not be as sensitive as EHR big data cleaning. For example, a movie recommendation algorithm (such as the algorithms used by online streaming services) has more flexibility to include or exclude outliers. The worst-case scenario for these algorithms is to recommend an unfavorable movie title to a viewer. In contrast, in EHR data analysis, the researchers do not have such freedom to drop or change the data. Excluding possible (but not probable) values from clinical research data may lead to biased results and, as a result, alters clinical decision making in a potentially harmful way.

As mentioned in the introduction chapter, until recent years, the use of EHR data as a source of scientific research has been overlooked, partly because the primary purpose of the EHR databases is not for research or clinical decision making. Instead, software developers optimize the database systems for legal and billing purposes with three main goals in mind: finances of health care management, user-friendliness for health care personnel, and usefulness to prepare legal documents. Moreover, since one database is used by hundreds of healthcare provider systems, the heterogeneity and the possibility of finding errors in the data are not negligible. Additionally, the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA law) requires the EHR data to be properly de-identified before making it available to researchers. This de-identification process makes detecting and fixing errors even more difficult. As a result, EHR data is not primarily research-friendly, making data cleaning and processing the most challenging steps in this type of research (Nobles et al. 2015).

In this chapter, we begin by reviewing existing methods and tools developed specifically for EHR data cleaning. We then list and discuss some common challenges and data cleaning issues in EHR databases and demonstrate methods on how to address them. Finally, we end the chapter with some general recommendations on data cleaning strategies tailored for EHR data.

The main goal of data cleaning is finding and correcting errors and reducing data heterogeneity. EHR includes a wide variety of data such that a single database can be used for multiple types of research. Therefore, it is helpful to develop a data cleaning procedure that is reproducible and, more importantly, repeatable for different datasets corresponding to different research projects. Developing generalized project-independent approaches for data cleaning can be expensive in terms of time and effort, but the payoff will be significant.


4.2 Review of Current Data Cleaning Methods and Tools

There are many published papers and packages on EHR data cleaning, most of which focus on datasets from a single or a limited number of health care facilities. In addition, some are quite limited and address a single task in data cleaning, such as unifying the measurement unit of some lab results. Also, we should emphasize that there is a narrow margin between data cleaning and data preparation. Some existing tools presented as data cleaning tools are in fact data preparation tools. For example, IntelliClean uses a knowledge-based system to create a measure of similarity between two records and merge them if their similarity is more than a predefined threshold (Lee et al. 2000). This approach is quite challenging when applied to a big EHR database as it is highly sensitive to the selected threshold. IntelliClean might be more reliable if applied in a project-specific manner to a narrower cohort and make use of domain knowledge. In this chapter, however, we address general data cleaning which can be applied to an entire database regardless of the goal and purpose of the process and the cohort of interest. The existing data cleaning tools can be divided into two main categories: general data wranglers, and data cleaning tools for specific EHR datasets.

4.2.1 Data Wranglers

These tools usually are the product of research groups working on data cleaning. One of the earliest examples of data wranglers is the “DataWrangler” originally developed by Stanford Visualization Group, then published by the Stanford/Berkeley Wrangler research project (Kandel et al. 2011). The free version of the tool was completed in 2012 and a commercialized version of the tool, called Trifacta data wrangler (Wrangler Editions 2019), was developed later.

Another example is Potter’s wheel, developed by UC Berkley (Apers et al. 2001). This tool has a Graphical User Interface, which minimizes coding and makes data manipulation easier for users without professional computational skills. This tool has a spreadsheet-like interface in which the user can define different types of transformations and then create a pipeline of these transformations. Potter’s wheel was optimized for working with large datasets. The fast visualization (even before loading the entire data), some type of data quality metric and automatic discrepancy detection, are the main features of this tool (Apers et al. 2001).

There are other data wrangler tools that are both commercially and publicly available, each with their own strengths and weaknesses (Adu-ManuSarpong et al. 2013). These tools were developed to handle a wide variety of different data types. Their main function is to reformat or transform the data. This kind of procedure is especially helpful in handling date/time fields and geolocation information. Both of these types of data can have a different or complex format and transforming them into a more uniform and convenient format is important. Furthermore, some tools offer data extraction functions as well. Detecting patterns and regular expressions and reformatting is another field that these tools are tailored to handle. Some more developed (and usually paid) tools can help in dealing with missing value issues and even clean the data by dropping outliers. Another strong property of these tools is their ability to handle and visualize the big size of data rapidly with their well written and optimized codes.

Although the generalizability of these tools is one of their main selling points, it is also their main drawback. Although working with EHR data has its own complexity, and wrangler tools would leave some inconsistencies in the data, it is still required to be dealt with specifically. Hence, data cleaning tools developed specifically for EHR data may be a better choice in most scenarios.

4.2.2 Data Cleaning Tools for Specific EHR Datasets

EHR database systems may have different designs and implementations. They may use different data dictionaries and their schema, as a relational database, can be different from one to another. For example, one database may use the World Health Organization ICD-10 code list with around 14,000 different codes and another may use the ICD-10-CM code list with more than 70,000 entries. Date may be entered in European style (dd/mm/yyyy) or USA format (mm/dd/yyyy). Different countries have different formats for their postal addresses; therefore, geolocation data can be another challenging task for data cleaning teams. Because of these discrepancies among EHR database systems, data cleaning tools are usually developed to perform on specific EHR database systems and may not be applied directly to other databases. However, a study for the methods and experience of research groups and their tools will always be beneficial and informative to researchers developing their own data cleaning tools.

One example of this type is “rEHR” package, developed by UK researchers using “r” statistical software. The UK has developed a near-universal deployment of EHRs in general practice performed by general practitioners for over 20 years (Springate et al. 2017). The following databases were used to create this universal EHR database:

  • The Clinical Practice Research Datalink (CPRD, previously known as the General Practice Research Database, GPRD)

  • The Health Improvement Network (THIN)

  • QResearch

  • The Doctors’ Independent Network (DIN-LINK)

  • Research One

This database is completely de-identified and made available for research (Springate et al. 2017).

rEHR package is a wrapper of SQL queries to interact with the underlying database easily and rapidly with some additional features. It can extract data from the database and convert the longitudinal data to a cohort dataset suitable for survival analysis. rEHR can create data that have matched control and case groups and can cut cohort data by time-varying covariates. An additional feature is the code’s ability to unify the measurement unit for HbA1c.

There are also some statistical methods for data cleaning in literature. For example, algorithms have been developed to detect and clean inaccurate adult height using median height during different stages of a patient’s life (Muthalagu et al. 2014). This approach is simple and easy to implement but drops surprising and dramatic information points from the patient’s history. For example, we neither expect a negative change in the patient’s height, nor a significant height gain after adolescence. However, we should keep in mind that, although with extremely small probability, there are exceptions to the above assumptions. For instance, if the patient undergoes a bilateral lower limb amputation surgery, the change in height will be negative, on the other hand, if the patient undergoes a spinal reconstructive surgery, he/she may gain height even after becoming an adult (Spencer et al. 2014). These situations are rare but, depending on the type of study and target population, ignoring them during the data cleaning process may remove some critical data points and add bias to the study.

4.2.3 Data Quality Assessment

One of the first challenges an investigator faces is if the available data is suitable and good enough for conducting the study of interest. The answer to this question is critical and the result usually becomes apparent during the initial steps of the data cleaning process. For example, we may be interested in studying a rare infectious disease using a Multi-central EHR database system. Initially, we may find that the database has enough records and subjects to conduct the study. However, in later data inspections, we may realize that majority of the records have been recorded improperly, and it includes an extremely high rate of missing data. These findings may force the research group to drop some blocks of records from the study which eventually may result in a significant decrease in the number of subjects available to continue the study. Kahn et al. proposed data quality metrics to assess the appropriateness of the available data (Kahn et al. 2012). They divided these metrics into five different categories:

  1. Attribute domain constraints: This metric measures the basic summary statistics of each of the desired attributes and assesses the distributions. If the clinical knowledge proposes a certain distribution with a definitive possible range of values for that attribute, we can estimate the level of error in the database for that specific attribute. For example, pulse oximetry values are usually above 95% and we know the possible range of values is from 0% to 100%. So if we find a large number of negative or more than 100% values for these attributes it can be indicative of poor data quality (high number of errors) (Elder et al. 2015).

  2. Relational integrity rules: This metric deals with the structure of the database from which we have extracted our data. For example, if we discover that the primary key for each patient (which is called Patient_sk [super_key] in Cerner) is not unique for some patients, we can conclude that the database has a structural problem, and a higher ratio of this discrepancy reveals a lower quality of the data.

  3. Historical data rules: This metric evaluates the quality of the time-varying attributes, such as the use of the same format for different time points.

  4. State-dependent rules: This metric assesses if the changes in the lifecycle of an object follow expected transitions. For example, if a patient is flagged as expired at one-time point, we shouldn’t have any records at later time points regarding his/her state of the living. An example is the measurement of blood pressure, recorded after the reported time of death. However, we should keep in mind that some results may become available and recorded in the database even after such occurrences such as toxicology reports.

  5. Attribute dependency rules: This metric measures the quality of dependent attributes. For example, if we observe pregnancy diagnosis for a patient with multiple male gender records in different encounters, we may question the quality of the data (Kahn et al. 2012).

This type of data quality assessment can guide us in addressing the following questions:

  • Is this specific research feasible using the available dataset?

  • How difficult and tedious will the data cleaning process be?

  • What areas should the data cleaning team prioritize?

Some studies used this framework to evaluate the data quality and clean their data mainly by dropping some of the records or attributes (Dziadkowiec et al. 2016), which although easy and straightforward, produced biases in the final results.


4.3 Common EHR Data Errors and Fixing Methods

In this section, we provide a brief review of possible errors and inconsistencies in EHR databases with some examples. In each case, we describe methods for reducing the inconsistencies and resolving the issues.

Most EHR databases have tables to store information about patients’ encounters, lab results, clinical events, diagnoses, medications, procedures, and surgical procedures. Among all of the information, only the encounter (which includes demographic variables), lab results, and clinical events (include vital signs) tables have quantitative results, hence they will be the focus of our data cleaning tool. However, the remaining tables, which mostly include records as diagnosis codes (e.g., ICD-9, ICD10), medication codes/names (e.g., NDC, generic names), procedure codes (e.g., CPT), are not free of issues. We also address those problems and provide simple procedures to deal with them. Furthermore, we will use examples from SAH and AIDS cohort projects based on the Cerner database (as mentioned in Chapter 2). Since we address general data errors that are not data/project-specific, the same methods (with some modifications) can be used for other databases. Finally, we describe our novel semi-automatic tool for dealing with major errors and inconsistencies in an EHR database.

4.3.1 List of Common Errors in an EHR Database

  • Demographics Table

    • Inconsistent gender.

    • Inconsistent ethnicity.

    • Non-unique patient key field.

    • Errors in recorded age.

  • Lab Table

    • Multiple units of measurement for the same lab.

    • Multiple date/time for a single record.

    • Some units of measurement are impossible.

    • Out of range errors.

    • Assigning the wrong Lab ID.

  • Clinical Events

    • Overlap with Lab Table.

    • Multiple clinical event codes for the same feature.

    • The same group of measurements has different time points.

    • Assigning the wrong clinical event ID.

  • Medication, Diagnosis, Procedure Tables

    • Multiple formats for the same medication.

    • Too many categories for diagnosis codes.

    • Multiple types of codes for procedures.

4.3.2 Demographics Table

Addressing concerns with the Demographic table is especially important because any data quality assessment is heavily dependent on this table. Furthermore, the demographics table is important in tracking down the errors and culprits of data cleaning in other tables.

Multiple Race and Gender

One of the issues that can be encountered is the inconsistency in the patient’s race and/or gender. This error is one of the commonly found errors in any EHR database. After the initial cleaning, the rates of gender and race inconsistencies are 0.3% and 22%, respectively, in the HIV-Cancer cohort. Besides mistakes made during data entry, we believe that the misreporting of transgender (estimated around 0.3% of the entire population in USA (Gates 2011)) and multiracial patients (estimated around 6.9% in USA (Parker et al. 2019)) could lead to these inconsistencies.

To understand the origin of the inconsistency, it is helpful to describe how the patient’s race and gender are recorded in the database. As presented in Figure 4.1, each patient has a unique Patient SK in our database (it should be unique but as we will discuss later there are some errors in this part). Each Patient SK has one or more associated Patient IDs and each Patient ID has one or more Encounter IDs. Patient’s gender and race are recorded as a feature of Patient ID. There may be more than one Patient ID for the same Patient SK. If the person demographics change with a new encounter, a new record is added to the Patient Table which includes a new Patient ID. Although rare, the same “person” may have different records for race/gender over time across encounters. Typically, when this occurs it is due to a blank or unknown value in one record and this field will be filled out in another patient record.

Our approach for fixing this error is:

  1. For each Patient SK, we select all the Patient IDs.

  2. We drop all of the NULL/null/Not Mapped values.

  3. If the patient has more than one value for gender or race, we get the majority vote for gender and race and set it as the correct patient gender and race.

  4. If the majority vote is inconclusive (i.e., an equal number for each category of gender/race) we select the first recorded gender and race as the correct patient’s gender and race.

Multiple Patient Keys for the Same Encounter ID

Another error that we may find is that multiple Patient SKs are linked to a single Encounter ID (it should be the other way around). If the ratio of this kind of error is high in any database, the database structure is unreliable and it will result in a significant reduction of the data quality. This type of error should be fixed before any further analysis/data cleaning. In our case study since we faced this error at only a 0.02% rate, we simply dropped them from our database.

Multiple Calculated Birth Date

In this example database, the patient age for each encounter is saved in multiple fields:

  1. Age in years

  2. Age in months

  3. Age in weeks

  4. Age in days

  5. Age in hours

In our case study we encountered three different scenarios for each recorded age (Figure 4.2):

  • First Scenario: All of the fields are blank (missing age)

  • Second Scenario: Only the age in years fields are populated (age in years)

  • Third Scenario: All of the age fields are populated (age in year/month/day)

Each patient may have multiple encounters. By using the date of admission for that encounter and age from either first or second scenario we can calculate exact or approximate birthdate respectively.

If we get multiple exact or approximate birthdate for a single patient, we use the following procedure to decide which one is the potential correct birth date:

  • Case 1: If all of the patient ages are compatible with the first scenario, the patient’s birth date is missing.

  • Case 2: If all of the patient ages are compatible with the second scenario, the patient’s birth date is decided by majority vote (Case 2a) or first recorded age (Case 2b), similar to the patient’s gender/race approach.

  • Case 3: If a patient has one (Case 3a) or more than one (Case 3b) estimated birth date compatible with the third scenario, the patient’s birth date is calculated using the first recorded birth date.

4.3.3 Lab Table

As mentioned earlier, big EHR databases are used by multiple health care facilities (e.g., hospitals and clinics) each of which may use different kits or equipment to evaluate a lab measurement (e.g., serum potassium level). Some equipment may have different normal reference ranges for the same lab measurement, and it is possible that the EHR system provider allowed each entity to use a customized data entry structure. These two factors led to the use of multiple formats to report the same lab measurement. In our example database, the Procedure ID column includes the numeric code for a lab procedure. The results are recorded according to the numeric Result Type ID field and may be stored using different formats (e.g., alphanumeric, numeric, text, etc.). For example, Table 4.1 shows the different formats used in our example database to report serum potassium level (encoded as Procedure ID&#160;=&#160;24), with multiple Result Type IDs for the same Procedure ID (namely 1, 7, 9, 12). Furthermore, each Result Type ID has another sub-level that consists of different units; hence, the serum potassium level was reported using 18 different formats, in one of the datasets.

Table 4.1 Different Report Formats for Serum Potassium Level in the Example Database

No. Procedure ID Procedure Name Result Type ID Result Type Description Normal Range Low Normal Range High Unit Unit Description
1 24 Potassium, Serum 1 Alpha 3.5 5.1
2 24 Potassium, Serum 1 Alpha 3.5 5 meq/L Milliequivalents per Liter
3 24 Potassium, Serum 1 Alpha 3.5 5.6 mmol/L Millimoles per Liter
4 24 Potassium, Serum 1 Alpha 3.8 4.8 Not Mapped Not Mapped
5 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric
6 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric 3.5 5 meq/dL Milliequivalents per Deciliter
7 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric 3.8 5.5 meq/L Milliequivalents per Liter
8 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric 1 3 min Minute
9 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric 3.5 5.3 mm/L Millimeters per Liter
10 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric mmol/L Millimoles per Liter
11 24 Potassium, Serum 7 Numeric 3.8 4.8 Not Mapped Not Mapped
12 24 Potassium, Serum 9 Text
13 24 Potassium, Serum 9 Text 0 0 meq/L Milliequivalents per Liter
14 24 Potassium, Serum 9 Text 3.5 5.1 mmol/L Millimoles per Liter
15 24 Potassium, Serum 9 Text ng/mL Nanograms per Milliliter
16 24 Potassium, Serum 12
17 24 Potassium, Serum 12 3.6 5 meq/L Milliequivalents per Liter
18 24 Potassium, Serum 12 3.5 5.1 mmol/L Millimoles per Liter

Another major issue with EHR data is inconsistency during data entry, which is also a significant cause for assigning different formats to similar fields. For example, the short description for “Millimeter per liter” in our example is “mm/L” (Table 4.1, row 9). We noticed some electrolyte concentration levels reported as “Millimeter per liter” (instead of common Millimoles per liter, Table 4.1 rows 3, 10, 14, 18). After careful examination of the values and the distribution, we determined that the operator selected “mm/L” by mistake. In this case, we simply adjusted the unit of measurement.

Another common mistake in data entry is mixing and confusing the lab IDs for count versus percentage lab results. This issue is more common in White Blood Cells (WBC) measurement. For example, Our database has different lab ID codes for Lymphocyte Percentage (as a percentage of the whole number of WBC) and Lymphocyte count. Due to operator misunderstanding or unawareness, sometimes the percentage of the lymphocytes reported in the lymphocyte count and the unit of measurement is selected as a percentage. Instead of simply deleting these values (increases missing and bias), we developed a mapping table to map this type of results to their appropriate lab ID. More specifically, we have developed a conversion map to consolidate the data and make the reporting format uniform. The strategy is discussed in the next section.

Developing Conversion Map

In this section, we explain our strategy for developing the conversion map and its structure. Throughout, we use the serum potassium level for example. Figure 4.3 shows the flow chart of the lab unit unification algorithm.

Table 4.2 presents an example of the conversion map. The first four columns are directly selected form our dataset extracted from EHR databases (including ID and units for potassium level), the rest of the columns are added in which we describe our data cleaning strategy. The description of each of these columns are as follows:

Table 4.2 A Section of the Conversion Map used for Unifying Serum Potassium Level

No. Procedure ID Result Type ID Unit Conversion Map ID Convert to Conversion Equation Lower Limit Upper Limit
1 24 1 24_1_ 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
2 24 1 meq/L 24_1_meq/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
3 24 1 mmol/L 24_1_mmol/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
4 24 1 Not Mapped 24_1_Not Mapped 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
5 24 7 24_7_ 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
6 24 7 meq/dL 24_7_meq/dL 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
7 24 7 meq/L 24_7_meq/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
8 24 7 min 24_7_min 24_7_ mmol/L omit 1 10
9 24 7 mm/L 24_7_mm/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
10 24 7 mmol/L 24_7_mmol/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
11 24 7 Not Mapped 24_7_Not Mapped 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
12 24 9 24_9_ 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
13 24 9 meq/L 24_9_meq/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
14 24 9 mmol/L 24_9_min 24_7_ mmol/L omit 1 10
15 24 9 ng/mL 24_9_mmol/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
16 24 12 24_9_ng/mL 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
17 24 12 meq/L 24_12_meq/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
18 24 12 mmol/L 24_12_mmol/L 24_7_ mmol/L x 1 10
Conversion Map ID

This column is a concatenation of “Procedure ID_Result type ID_Unit” sections of each format. This specific field has been developed for making simplifying the programming section of our tool and better interpretation of the results.

Convert To

This column shows the final format that the values will be converted to. The purpose of this field is to unify the data to the most frequent format used in the database for that specific lab result or a unit suitable for the specific lab result according to professional knowledge. For the serum potassium level example, we select 24_7_mmol/L as our unified version.

Conversion Equation

The data in the Conversion_equation field will be used to calculate the target value (a value compatible with convert_to characteristics). The code will capture the formula from this field, and use it for mathematical calculations.

The strategy for creating the conversion equation for each lab is:

  1. Check the distribution, lower limit, and upper limit of the current format and compare it to the convert_to format distribution, lower limit, and upper limit. If they look the same, the conversion_ equation will be x (i.e., f(x) = x and no mathematical manipulation needed). One important rule for the conversion map is to use x as the variable (same as x in f(x)). If we want to do mathematical computation on the data, we can use a standard equation like:

    $\left. f(x) = (((x - 4)/8)*5x) \right.\hat{}2.$

    In this phase of developing the tool, we compare distributions manually. It is also possible to develop an automatic method to evaluate the difference between the distributions and if it is not significant put x in the convertion_equation field.

  2. If the distributions are different, first we try to find a conversion_ equation according to the literature. If we fail to find a reliable method, we seek guidance from clinicians.

  3. If we are unable to find a suitable map for a record, our tool leaves the record in the current format. For instance, when the Unit was not known (Not mapped, unknown, null, etc.) we face conversion_map_IDs like 24_7_Not Mapped for which we could not determine the conversion equation.

  4. If a format looks completely wrong and did not look valid, the conversion_equation is set to omit. The code will delete the value and consider it as missing.

The Lower Limit and Upper Limit

According to literature in medical science journals, for some lab results, there are ranges of possible values that are compatible with life; that is, values beyond these ranges are not normal or even possible in a living person. To include the extreme cases, we can set an even wider range than the values reported in the literature. Our tool first calculates the value according to conversion_equation and then if the result is between upper and lower limits, it records the result; otherwise, it reports the value as missing. Table 4.3 shows the possible values of some labs according to literature (Clot-Silla et al. 2011) and our assumptive upper and lower limits. We should emphasize that this optional step is a part of data processing, but we include it in our data cleaning tool to promote faster and less computationally intense data processing.
