Lab Date and Time

Often there are multiple time/date-stamps for some events, recording the event times from order to administration (or cancelation) of a medication, a procedure, or a lab procedure. For example, each record in the lab table may have one or several of the following seven date/time-stamps:

  1. Lab Drawn Date Time

  2. Lab Received Date Time

  3. Lab Performed Date Time

  4. Lab Completed Date Time

  5. Lab Verified Date Time

  6. Lab Ordered Date Time

  7. Lab Cancelled Date Time

For longitudinal analyses, we need a consistent time stamp for each recorded event. Some timestamps are clinically more reasonable to use, and none of the fields above have complete information (i.e., some of them have missing dates). Therefore, we can define the following strategy to provide a consistent time/date stamp for each event.

  1. If a date/time recorded as 01/01/1900 in the Lab Table it should be considered as missing date/time, and lab value with a valid date/time in the Lab Cancelled Date Time field is dropped from the records.

  2. The first valid date-time value will be selected out of the first six (according to the order 1–6 above) entries and recorded as the lab records time stamp.

  3. If the user provides two dates/times (e.g., admission and discharge time) as lower (t0) and upper (te) limit for each patient encounter, the code will incorporate an extra validation step for selecting the correct timestamp. The details of the lab date time validation algorithm are presented in Figure 4.4. Again, this optional process is a part of data processing and our tool is flexible to include/exclude this step in the data cleaning process.

User Input Form and Report Generator

To facilitate the use of our data cleaning tool for scientists without professional programming skills we developed a user-friendly interface. Figure 4.5 presents an example of the user interface.

A study of descriptive statistics and basic graphic visualization of a dataset are primary steps to any statistical analysis for evaluating data quality and suggesting a suitable model for the data. However, based on our experience producing such a simple report can be extremely time-consuming even for researchers with a reasonable computational background. Therefore, we added a report generator function to our data cleaning tool to create an automatic generated report, although this step is optional and can be ignored. This tool can create a report in HTML, Microsoft Word, Acrobat pdf, or other formats. Figure 4.6 is a screenshot of the first page from the example report generated by this tool.

Output

Although each data cleaning team may have their own preferences, based on our experience we recommend following these strategies for organizing the output of the data cleaning report.

  1. Keep the unique identifiers in your output without any modifications, they are required to stay unchanged for merging data tables consistently.

  2. Do not drop any values unless you are 100% certain it is an error; for example, see the height data cleaning approach in Section 4.2.2. Data cleaning should be a general procedure for EHR big data. The project-specific data modification is called data processing and it will be discussed in the next chapter.

  3. At each step of data cleaning or modification of algorithms, save your results and outputs in different files to keep track of different versions of the files. Based on our experience this is a useful strategy in the event of unfortunate mistakes in one part of the process.

  4. When you clean and process data for different teams or data analysis users, it will be helpful to flag any remaining issues in the data which could not be addressed using the data cleaning process to inform the analyst about the reliability of each record.

Figure 4.7 presents an example of using flags for lab data. You may use other types of flags. The description of each flag presented in the figure follows:

  • Kept—there is a valid result and it has been unified and kept in the data.

  • Missing—the original data did not have a value.

  • Omitted—the field was wrong and dropped.

  • Out of range—the result was out of range of user-defined possible values and dropped.

  • Missing equation—there was no equation for this Conversion map ID in the conversion map. The Converted_Result field will not have a value.

  • Canceled—the lab Cancelled date/time field has a valid date (i.e., the lab has been canceled). The Converted_Result field will not have a value.

The final result will be a more uniform distribution for the lab values. As an example, Figure 4.8 shows the result of lab data cleaning on the D-dimer lab result. As you can see the range of lab results reduced dramatically and it looks more uniform.

4.3.4 Clinical Event Table

The clinical event table, particularly the vital signs subgroup, has a similar structure as the lab table and all the steps of the data cleaning tool can be applied to it. However, there are other inconsistencies in this table, mostly project-specific that require clinical expertise, which we will discuss in this section.

Variable Combining

Since our example database is used by many health care facilities, there are often various extensions of a label or description used for the same event. We use a combination of statistical techniques and clinical expertise to deal with this issue, and propose combining codes describing the same events. For example, there are 46 distinct event code IDs for the Blood Gas test, some with very similar descriptions, e.g., Base Excess, Base Excess Arterial, Base Excess Venous. For a cohort of SAH patients, Table 4.4 gives the number of patients with each of the three result records and Figure 4.9 shows that there is a small number of patients with two or all three of the records. In addition, Figure 4.10 does not show a significant difference between the average values of the three record labels. Therefore, after confirming with expert clinicians we may decide to combine the three labels into one variable.

Table 4.4 Summary of Three Test Components in the Blood Gas Test

Test Number of Records Number of Patients
Base Excess 3029 310
Base Excess, Arterial 7027 493
Base Excess, Venous 277 88

Information Recovery

Some clinical events produce multiple results at the same time with different descriptions and codes. However, not all results can be found in the database; some are missing. For example, the Blood Gas test produces multiple measurements (see Figure 4.11) including PO2 Arterial, pH Arterial, PCO2 Arterial, FiO2 Arterial, and CO2, but not all are recorded every time the test has been conducted.

This issue can be categorized as a “missing” problem in EHR data which can potentially be handled using simple imputation methods when there is a strong correlation among records. The details of how to face this kind of problem will be presented in Chapter 6.

A Case Study

The Braden scale, used for predicting pressure ulcer risk, is composed of six categorical results: sensory perception, skin moisture, activity, mobility, friction and shear, and nutrition. Friction and shear are rated on a scale of 1 to 3, and all other categories are rated on a scale of 1 to 4 (see Table 4.5), where higher scores represent a lower risk of developing the ulcer. Potentially all six records should be available for each patient at each measurement time point. However, the majority of patients have a total score for pressure risk or a combination of other records. As the only suitable solution available, here we decide to aggregate all the available records to recover the total pressure record for all the patients.

Table 4.5 Braden Scale for Predicting Pressure Sore Risk

Component Levels
Sensory perception

  1. Totally limited

  2. Very limited

  3. Slightly limited

  4. No impairment
Skin moisture

  1. Constantly moist

  2. Very moist

  3. Occasionally moist

  4. Dry
Activity

  1. Bedfast

  2. Chairfast

  3. Walks occasionally

  4. Walks frequently
Friction and shear

  1. Frequently sliding

  2. Feeble correction

  3. Independent correction
Mobility

  1. Completely immobile

  2. Very limited

  3. Slightly limited

  4. Full mobility
Nutrition

  1. Very poor

  2. Probably inadequate

  3. Adequate

  4. Excellent

Overlap of Different Tables

While comparing the clinical event and lab tables we may notice that there is a certain level of overlap between the two tables. This could be because some of the lab workups are performed in the clinical wards and the database records any data produced by these centers in the clinical event table, although in nature these are lab procedures. To overcome this issue, we can create a mapping table to map similar results from the clinical event table to the lab table after cleaning the overlap variables using our data cleaning tool.

Correction of Misinformation

We may find many cases of inconsistent labeling of events in the clinical event and lab tables which most likely occurred during the data entry process. For example, the two variables associated with coma scores are Pediatric Coma Score, for non-adults, and Glasgow Coma Score, for adults. However, occasionally adult patients are labeled with pediatric event codes, Pediatric Coma Score, instead of the Glasgow Coma Score. We can use the encounter table and history of patients' records to fix this issue.

4.3.5 Diagnosis and Medication Table

Other tables in Our database such as Diagnosis and Medication table are mostly codes and labels rather than values. Although the data cleaning approach described above (for lab and clinical event tables) is neither required nor applicable to them, these tables have their own specific inconsistencies and issues that need to be resolved. In the remainder of this chapter, we share the important issues we faced during data preparation for a couple of specific projects: Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (SAH) cohort and HIV-Cancer cohort studies.

For medication, we found that working with the generic names would be more robust and efficient compared to medication codes (NDC codes1). However, we realized (possibly among different care facilities) there are some inconsistencies in spelling, upper/lower case, and using multiple words (via “-”, “/”, etc.). We used a combination of screening and clinical intuition to fix these inconsistencies and unify the generic name for similar medications.

The major issues we face with the diagnosis table are the large number of diagnosis codes (ICD-9, ICD-10) and the fact that there are no specific dates associated with each record (all of the diagnosis codes recorded with the discharge date-time stamp in the database). When using statistical models, we may decide to use higher-level codes, i.e., the first 3 digits of ICD-9 codes. Because of the ICD-10’s better design, this approach is more appropriate in handling ICD-10 codes. Reducing the number of unique diagnosis codes is not an issue when using Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) models. In fact, we have compared the same model based on both code levels and noticed no significant differences. To understand the order of diagnosis, in terms of chronology or importance, we used the information provided in two columns “Present-at-admission” and “disease-priority”. The first gives us a way to cluster the diagnoses before a certain time, which is necessary when casual inference and propensity models are used.

4.3.6 Procedure Table

Introduction to the Procedure Code Data

Procedure codes are a sub-type of medical classification used to identify specific surgical, medical, or diagnostic interventions. They should be compatible with the diagnosis codes (ICD-9 or ICD-10) to prevent a denial of payment by insurance companies like BC/BS2 or Medicare. In our example database, data files use three types of procedure codes. We begin this section with a brief description of each of the classification code types common in EHR databases:

  • ICD-9 procedure codes

    • They are two numeric digits followed by a decimal, which is then followed by another one or two digits (in contrast with ICD-9 diagnosis codes which begins with 3 alphanumeric characters).

    • They are categorized according to the anatomical region or body system.

    • They have broader categories than CPT3 and HCPCS4. (There are considerably fewer ICD-9 procedure codes than there are CPT procedure codes).

    • They can only be used for the inpatient setting.

  • CPT codes

    • They are also known as the Level 1 HCPCS coding system.

    • CPT is a medical code set that is used to report medical, surgical, and diagnostic procedures and services to entities such as physicians, health insurance companies, and accreditation organizations.

    • They are used in conjunction with ICD-9-CM or ICD-10-CM. Numerical diagnostic coding during the electronic medical billing process.

    • They are made up of five numbers.

    • Unlike ICD, each number in a CPT code does not correspond to a particular procedure or technology.

  • HCPCS level II codes

    • Level II of the HCPCS is a standardized coding system that is used primarily to identify products, supplies, and services not included in the CPT codes, such as ambulance services and durable medical equipment when used outside a physician’s office.

    • Level II codes are also referred to as alpha-numeric codes because they consist of a single alphabetical letter followed by 4 numeric digits, while CPT codes are identified using 5 numeric digits.

Procedure Table Data Cleaning

The data cleaning team can try to convert some of the CPT or HCPCS Level II codes to equivalent ICD-9 procedure codes but usually, this cannot be easily done, i.e., the CPT codes and HCPCS level II codes do not fit in any of the ICD-9 procedure codes. Because of this, we can decide not to perform any data manipulation on the procedure table and the data cleaning for the procedure table may not be necessary.


4.4 Discussion

Here are the key points in this chapter:

Check the database structure. The first step of data cleaning is to ensure that the database has the correct relational database schema. This step is crucial if the researchers are working on a copy of the database and not the original functional database. The original database should have mainly correct database structure, otherwise, it cannot be operational, but the deployment of the copy of the data is prone to structural problems. If there is any issue with the database schema it should be fixed first, otherwise, any other data cleaning procedure will introduce more errors in the data or can be unnecessarily difficult.

Seek field expert’s guidance. As in any other research area, it is recommended to have professionals with sufficient knowledge of the field to be included in the data cleaning team. In EHR data, we recommend including team members with clinical knowledge and health care management background in the data cleaning field.

Do preliminary/exploratory analyses early in the study. As we discussed in the previous sections of this chapter, in any EHR based research project, the first two questions that should be answered are:

 

  1. Does the data have enough number of patients to address the main purpose of the study?

  2. Is the data of good quality for conducting the study and will it yield good results?

 

For this purpose, we propose to write a data cleaning summary statistics report. Creating this report in the early stages of the study will answer the above questions and will help the research team to decide if the data is good enough for the study. This is crucial to prevent wasting time on the data cleaning for a data set that is not useful for that specific study.

Data cleaning is (usually) a never-ending process. Data cleaning begins with identifying errors. Usually, you develop a strategy for addressing the errors for a specific cohort of patients and during the advanced phases of the study, you discover more errors. Furthermore, as you move on to other patient cohorts you will probably identify new errors, specific to that population. Be prepared to return to the data cleaning step frequently.

Document every step of data cleaning. Data cleaning is often an iterative procedure and may need to returning to if for modifying some steps. Document every step and every procedure. This will be extremely helpful when you need to come back and possibly cannot remember what you have done before. Also, EHR research teams are usually big and staff turnover is inevitable. By keeping detailed and well-organized documentation of the data cleaning procedure you will save time and prevent unnecessary challenges.

Be focused and efficient. Keep in mind that you do not need to clean the whole database. It is good if you are capable of that, but usually, it will take years and by the time you are done, the database will be updated with new errors. Your data cleaning procedure should be focused based on the current study. For example, you do not need to clean the data about the history of previous joint dislocation if the research is focused on Alzheimer’s disease. Furthermore, when you see some data records are seldom present for your cohort of patients, if it is clinically justified, you may want to drop those records. As an example, let us assume you want to study the effect of weight on the development of osteoarthritis, during the First Summary Statistics Report you realized only 2% of the patients have records about the history of fungal infection of the skin. Since fungal infection of the skin is not clinically related to the development of osteoarthritis and it is sparse in your data, it may be better to drop these records. Doing this kind of initial variable selection will reduce the computational and person/time costs. On the other hand, including that in the data will probably add more noise to your data and will make future model building and/or statistical interpretation more challenging.

There is no universal solution. Unfortunately, there is no universal solution for data cleaning. Each EHR database has unique errors and it is almost impossible to develop a universal tool to clean every type of EHR data. But strategies such as the methods we provided in this section can be implemented to handle the data cleaning effort.

The first data cleaning for EHR data will be time-consuming, but if the focus is on developing a more generalized method for data cleaning, the data cleaning step for future projects will be easier and faster. This happens because first, the data cleaning team members will become familiar with the database structure and common errors, and second if you have fixed a lot of errors in the first data cleaning then the next project will just add a few more errors. It is beneficial to look at data cleaning as a long-term investment. As the EHR research team conducts more projects, the data cleaning team will become more efficient and the time spent on the data cleaning step will be reduced. No EHR research can be done without data cleaning and spending time on developing a generalized, efficient, and accurate data cleaning framework is justified with the potential gains in the timely conducting of future projects.

As we have discussed, data cleaning rarely ends. The data cleaning teams often find new strategies to handle data cleaning more efficiently and accurately. Your data cleaning team can focus on applying the developed general methods on the whole database when it is applicable and computationally doable. Furthermore, each project brings new challenges. In the future, we may update this chapter to reflect these new errors and present newly developed strategies.

The authors of this chapter would like to thank Frances Brito, Yuxuan Gu, Michael Gonzalez, Xueying Wang, and Leqing Wu for their great work and effort in helping shape this chapter. None of this would have been possible without their unconditional support and help.

5

EHR Data Pre-Processing and Preparation
               

Duo Yu, Xueying Wang and Hulin Wu


5.1 Introduction

Data pre-processing and preparation are the steps after data cleaning and before statistical analyses, which are critical for statistical analysis and modeling. Given a statistical analysis method, a certain format of inputs is required, some forms of data pre-processing and preparation are necessary for different data analysis purposes. On the other hand, different data pre-processing and preparation methods may produce different datasets for final analysis, which may yield different analysis results. Thus, it is important to standardize and document the data pre-processing and preparation methods in order to ensure the reproducibility of the data analysis results.

As discussed in Chapter 1 (Section 1.2), the multiple steps from EHR data extraction to data analysis may need to be performed iteratively. In particular, the steps of data pre-processing and preparation may need to be iteratively done with the steps immediately before and after it, i.e., the data cleaning step and the data analysis step (Figure 5.1). In general, different data pre-processing and preparation methods may be used to wrangle the data from different perspectives and for different analysis purposes. We may also need to further perform more project-specific data cleaning using the methods discussed in Chapter 4. Different purposes of data analyses may require the data to be pre-processed or prepared differently, which are usually project-specific. For example, different analysis purposes for different projects may require to define or re-define the outcomes or predictors differently, and more specific data cleaning and processing may be necessary to better determine the outcome or predictor variables.

Data pre-processing and data processing are often used interchangeably. Here we will distinguish between the data pre-processing/processing and data preparation although these two steps are sometimes difficult to distinguish.

5.1.1 Definition of Data Pre-Processing/Processing

Before we perform formal data analyses to address a clinical or scientific question, we need to process the data, summarize it or extract useful features from it. Data pre-processing or processing may include the following tasks:

  • Summarize the data into new data variables or generate derived variables from the original data

  • Deal with data issues: e.g., missing data imputation or redundant data combination

  • Derive different features based on the repeated measurements of some variables

  • Extract features using dimension reduction or other feature engineering methods

The data pre-processing step is project-specific. Different projects may require processing the data in different ways. Usually, the data processing step may need to be iteratively performed with data preparation, and these two steps may not have a clear boundary in some cases. Ideally, we should extract as many features as possible from the raw data so that the signals and information buried in the raw data can be identified for downstream analyses.

5.1.2 Definition of Data Preparation

After data cleaning and data processing, we must prepare the data in different formats for different analysis purposes. The data preparation is purpose-driven, and different objectives for a project may require to prepare the data in different ways. Thus, even for a particular project, many versions of the datasets may need to be prepared for different analysis purposes. Data preparation may include, but are not limited to, the following tasks:

  • Project-specific data cleaning for data preparation: For example, if some encounters for a patient may overlap with each other or be very close in time, then we may need to combine or separate the overlapping encounters to meet the goals of a particular data analysis

  • Define, classify and label the patients for different outcomes, treatments or conditions

  • Define the encounters or time intervals to prepare the data for different analysis purposes

  • Prepare the data for different scenarios or cases, including for sensitivity analysis purposes

As aforementioned, data preparation and data processing need to be iteratively performed. Many versions of the prepared data may be created in this step. It is crucial to document or label the latest version of the prepared data and ensure that all the team members use the correct version of the prepared data for final data analyses, which is critical for the reproducibility of the analysis results.


5.2 Data Pre-Processing

5.2.1 Tidy Data Principles

Variable encoding is a basic data pre-processing method to transform the raw data into the “tidy” format to facilitate statistical analysis. There are three principles of tidy data (Wickham 2014):

  • Each variable has one column

  • Each observation has one row

  • Each cell has a value

For structured EHR data, different variables are stored in different tables. To create a tidy dataset, we need to merge the variables from those tables into one dataset based on their relationships. In Figure 5.2, a diagram is shown on how to create a tidy dataset base on the structured EHR data. Note that the encounter table in the EHR database is already in a tidy format, i.e., it meets all the above three principles of tidy data. However, many other tables, such as the medication table, may not be in the tidy data format, instead it may be the long data format, in which each observation has multiple rows. For example, in the top-left table in Figure 5.2, the treatment variable is in the long data format in the medication table. In this case, each treatment of a observation need to be transformed as a single variable and to be re-formatted as an individual column. Thus, a total of 4 treatments will generate 4 columns after transformation. After merging the medication and encounter tables by encounter IDs, each row of the tidy data represents all the treatment records for one encounter of one patient in EHR data; and each column represents a single variable (Figure 5.2 bottom) that will be included in the analysis model in the next step.

Variable Encoding

The main aim of variable encoding is to transform raw data into the format of values that is appropriate for statistical analysis. Traditionally, one may group the variables into three categories based on the clean data and corresponding codebook, i.e., continuous, binary and categorical variables. For example, generally we treat age as a continuous variable, sex is considered as a binary variable, and race will be treated as a categorical variable. Sometimes, a continuous variable can be transformed to a binary or categorical variable using discretization methods if needed.

An EHR dataset may contain records of different medications, diagnoses and procedures for individual patients. We can process the data in two ways: 1) Treat them as binary variables based on whether a patient has the record of a particular medication, diagnosis, or procedure; 2) Treat them as a continuous variable by counting the frequency of the occurrence of these events, see Figure 5.3 for details.

Here we provide a concrete example of variable encoding from an EHR project, the vasopressor treatment evaluation and mortality prediction for subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) patients. Subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) is an uncommon and life-threatening stroke that accounts for approximately 5% to 10% of all strokes in the United States. A particularly serious and common complication following SAH is delayed cerebral ischemia, and the development of delayed cerebral ischemia results in even higher mortality and disability among SAH patients. When delayed cerebral ischemia is clinically suspected, induced hypertension is often recommended to maintain cerebral perfusion unless the blood pressure is already elevated (Connolly Jr et al. 2012). While treatment with vasopressors is often initiated to maintain cerebral perfusion, it is not known which vasopressor is most effective in improving outcomes. In this project, we evaluated the initial use of different vasopressors among patients with nontraumatic subarachnoid hemorrhage using mortality as the endpoint. A mortality prediction model was developed based on the available variables and potential predictors in the EHR database for this patient population. We will use this project as an example to illustrate the data processing and preparation principles and methods in this chapter.

In this SAH study, we used generic names of medications and the medication variables were treated as binary predictors, i.e., “1” if a patient has ever received the medication and “0” otherwise. The diagnoses, lab tests, and clinical events were also treated as binary variables. At the same time, some of these variables such as lab tests and vital signs in the clinical event table can also be treated as categorical or continuous variables. For example, glucose measurements or blood pressures have numerical values and can be treated as continuous variables.

5.2.2 Feature Extraction: Derived Variables

In EHR data, some variables, such as lab test results, clinical events and vital signs, were measured repeatedly in irregular time intervals and some others were only measured once for a patient. Some vital signs such as blood pressure or heart rate are measured or monitored intensively. In some cases, we need to combine those repeatedly measured variables with the ones with single measurement for a patient for predictions and other statistical models. It is necessary to either summarize each of these variables (i.e., variables with repeated measurement into summary statistics) or use some feature extraction or feature engineering approaches to extract features from it so that “tidy data” can be created for downstream analysis.

First, we may extract simple and meaningful features from the repeated measurements for a variable for an individual. These features can be simple summary statistics such as mean, median, minimum, maximum, range, quantiles, standard deviation or variance of the repeated measurements for each variable. Some other simple features may be related to the measurement time, such as the first measurement, last measurement, or the measurement immediately following a treatment. Each of these simple features reflects partial information of multiple measurements of a variable and are interpretable. If we can collect all the possible features, hopefully all the information of a variable with repeated measurements can be included in the analysis model and the information loss is minimized. As an example, Table 5.1 provides a list of features that were extracted from a lab test variable in an EHR database. Figure 5.4 shows the feature extraction results for a lab test variable with repeated measurements.

Table 5.1 Examples for Derived Features for a Lab Test With Repeated Measurements

Feature Name Description
Lab_FirstTime_1 The first time of lab 1 test for a patient
Lab_LastTime_1 The last time of lab 1 test for a patient
Lab_TimeDuration_1 Lab_last_time_1 - Lab_first_time_1(unit: day)
Lab_Counts_1 The total counts of lab 1 test for a patient
Lab_Frequency_1 LabTime_Duration_1/ Lab_counts_1 (How many days it takes to do a test? Unit: day/test)
Lab_FirstTime_to_T0_1 Lab_first_time_1 – T0
Lab_LastTime_to_Te_1 Te - Lab_last_time_1
Lab_Mean_1 The mean value of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Median_1 The median value of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Min_1 The minimum value of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Min_time_1 The time label for Lab_min_1
Lab_Max_1 The maximum value of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Max_time_1 The time label for lab_max_1
Lab_Var_1 The variance of lab 1 for a patient (Nan if there is no variance)
Lab_Mode_1 The mode of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_1Quartile_1 The 1st quartile of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_3Quartile_1 The 3nd quartile of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_IQR_1 The IQR of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_QCD_1 The quartile coefficient of dispersion of lab 1 for a patient, which measures dispersion
Lab_STdev_1 The standard deviation of lab 1 values for a patient
Lab_CV_1 The coefficient of variation of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Skew_1 The skewness of lab 1 for a patient
Lab_Kurt_1 The kurtosis of lab 1 values for a patient
Lab_Peaks_1 The count of peaks of the distribution of lab 1 values for a patient

In this example, we further demonstrate how to extract features for lab test variables with repeated measurements in an EHR database. First, we must get a complete list of all the lab test identification numbers (IDs) and their total counts (total number of unique lab tests) for a targeted population. For our example from the subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) study described above, we provide our R code and results below, which shows that the total counts of 6 lab tests are 3358, 4231, 6994, 1811, 559, and 329 respectively.

Second, we need to determine the number of tests for each lab test variable for each patient so that we can summarize these multiple test results into features. As the R code shown below, for lab k (k&#160;=&#160;1 to 10), we obtain the list of patient IDs and the number of measurements that patient had for this lab test. Table TestTimes_Patient in the example code contains the patient IDs and the counts of this lab test for all patients. Then we generate several features using summary statistics (Table 5.1) for each lab test variable with repeated measurements for each patient. See the R code below for details. If needed, you may add more summary statistics or other features in a similar manner.

After the features are generated for all variables and all patients, some of the variables and their features may be missing for some patients due to the missing data problem. We need to label the missing data appropriately so that the missing data approaches (to be discussed below and in Chapter 6) can be used to handle this issue in the next step. Since we want to distinguish different missing types in our dataset, we labeled the lab test results, including missing data types, into 5 categories (see the R code below and results in Table 5.2).

Table 5.2 Labels for Missingness

Label Description
LMV There is at least one measured value
Lab0 There is no any lab data uploaded for this patient
NM Not measured
MM Measured with missing values
FM Measured but no feature derived

Here we only discussed the derived variables and features extracted from a single variable. Some features can be derived from two or more variables. For example, body mass index (BMI) is a well-known derived variable from body weight and height. Similar features can be derived from multiple variables. Once the features are generated for all variables and all patients, these features can be renamed as new variables or potential predictors for downstream data analysis.

5.2.3 Dimension Reduction

Dimension reduction approaches can be used to generate features although most of the features generated by these methods may not be easily interpretable. In particular, it may not be easy to use for intervention or prevention purposes in clinical and population health practice. However, the dimension reduction methods are still popular to extract features from high-dimensional data for prediction and clustering purposes. It is also useful to reduce the number of predictors to improve prediction accuracy and interpretability.

Variable Grouping or Clustering

The curse of dimensionality is one of the EHR data problems that we need to address. This is because many of the EHR variables such as disease diagnoses, medications, lab tests, clinical events, vital signs, and procedures are high-dimensional. If we can group or cluster these variables into higher-level groups, the dimension can be significantly reduced.

For example, for the disease diagnosis, the ICD-9-CM system consists of more than ten thousand codes. Based on the hierarchical structure of the ICD-9-CM coding system, we can use the higher-level ICD-9-CM with only three digits (<1000 disease groups). Besides, the phenome-wide association studies (PheWAS) grouped the ICD-9 codes into <2000 disease groups (phecodes) and the Agency for Healthcare Research and Quality Clinical Classification Software for ICD-9-CM (CCS) grouped all ICD-9-CM codes into <300 categories. Wei et al conducted a study to evaluate the ICD-9-CM, CCS, and phecode coding systems for phenome wide association studies using the electronic health record (EHR) data (Wei et al. 2017). Their results suggested that, although all ICD-9-CM, CCS and phecodes can be used for PheWas studies, the phecodes system produces superior analysis results (Wei et al. 2017). In addition, phecodes are more consistent with the disease categories that are generally used in clinical practice. The latest version of the phecodes groups 15,558 ICD-9-CM codes (including both three- and five-digit codes) into 1,866 disease categories (Denny et al. 2013). Similarly, we can group other types of EHR variables such as medications, lab tests and procedures into higher-level categories so that the dimension of the variables can be reduced. In addition, some categorical variables may have too many categories that can also be grouped or combined into bigger categories so that the sample size for each category is not too small for downstream analysis. For example, race has more than 9 categories and small categories have very few subjects. In this case, we may combine some of these small categories in order to obtain a sufficient sample size for further analysis. However, we have to consider the balance between the resolution of the analysis results and the sample size when we try to reduce the dimension by combining the variables and categories into larger categories. This needs to be considered on a case-by-case basis.

Principle Component Analysis (PCA)

Principal component analysis (PCA) is a multivariate technique that analyzes a data matrix, denoted by X, in which observations are described by several inter‐correlated quantitative dependent variables. Its goal is to reduce the dimensionality of a dataset while maintaining the variations in the data. It uses an orthogonal transformation to convert the correlated variables in the dataset to a new set of uncorrelated and ordered variables called principal components (PCs). Mathematically, PCA is an orthogonal linear transformation that rotates the original data to a new coordinate system such that the X lies along the direction of the greatest variation of the data, the second greatest variance lies on the second coordinate, and so on (Abdi and Williams 2010).

Suppose we have a data matrix X&#160;=&#160;(X1,&#160;…,&#160;Xp) with measurements of p random variables, the first several PCs are the linear combinations of the original variables that explain the majority of the variance of the data. We can obtain the PCs from the ordered eigenvalues of the correlation matrix for the standardized X matrix. The first PC is obtained from the eigenvector corresponding to the largest eigenvalue. The p PCs can be written as,

$\begin{matrix} {Y_{1} = e_{1}^{\prime}X = e_{11}X_{1}\ + \ \cdots\ + \ e_{1p}X_{p}} \\ {Y_{2} = e_{2}^{\prime}X = e_{21}X_{1}\ + \ \cdots\ + \ e_{2p}X_{p}} \\ \vdots \\ {Y_{p} = e_{1}^{\prime}X = e_{p1}X_{1}\ + \ \cdots\ + \ e_{pp}X_{p}} \\ \end{matrix}$

For the purpose of dimension deduction, we may keep the first q PCs (q&#160;<&#160;p) so that the majority of the information or variation in X can still be explained with these q components or PCs. We can calculate the percentage of variance explained for the ith PC by the formula:$PC = \frac{\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{k = 1}^{p}\lambda_{k}}$

In many cases, around 80% of the total variance for large p can be attributed to the first few PCs, which helps us to reduce the dimension of our dataset by using these components to replace the original data without incurring much loss of information (Jolliffe 2011). However, if we use the PCs as predictors in a prediction model, it may not be easy to explain the results and the PC predictors from a clinical perspective, which is a drawback in using the PCA for dimension reduction. Here we only discussed the PCA approach under the linear model and Gaussian assumption. For other dimension reduction methods, see the book by Li (Li 2018).

Embedding and Deep Learning

EHR data has high dimensional and sparse medical codes, such as medications, diagnoses and procedures. Efficiently reducing the data dimension may improve the performance of the data analysis and predictions. Embedding is a dimension reduction method which transforms the binary and categorical variables into continuous feature vectors. With binary or categorical variables, the basic encoding method is the one-hot encoding or dummy encoding: each variable is represented as a vector with one “1” and multiple “0”. However, there are some drawbacks for one-hot encoding. In high dimensional settings, it gets over-fitting easily, resulting in poor performance for the generalization of prediction models. In Figure 5.5, we illustrate the difference between one-hot encoding and embedding vectors. With 6 medical codes, one hot encoding requires a vector with a length of 6 to represent each code, whereas, the embedding vector only needs 3 dimensions. In real-world applications, one can choose the dimension of the embedding vector as needed in order to achieve better performance.

Some efforts were made on the developments of embedding methods to improve the prediction accuracy using EHR data. Some methods have largely borrowed ideas from Natural Language Processing (NLP) techniques word embedding, like Continuous Bag of Words Model (CBOW) and Skip-gram (Mikolov et al. 2013). CBOW is the model with a goal to predict the current word based on surrounding words. On the contrary, Skip-gram is used to predict the surrounding words given the current word. Choi et al proposed Med2vec which can be used to learn distributed representations for both medical codes and visits from a large EHR dataset (Choi, Bahadori, Searles, et al. 2016).

5.2.4 Missing Data Imputation

It is important to deal with the missing data in the data processing step. The EHR database is usually designed for practical operation or billing purposes, instead of data analysis or research purposes, and the missing data issues are highly prevalent for EHR data. The likelihood-based approach via the expectation-maximization (EM) method (Dempster, Laird, and Rubin 1977) and Bayesian approach via MCMC techniques (Andrieu et al. 2003) have been proposed to deal with the missing data directly under the variety of regression models, but these methods are computationally expensive for big EHR data analysis. An alternative strategy is to use the “missing data imputation” approaches to generate a “complete data set” which can be used for data analyses in the next step. Missing data imputation methods include the Multiple Imputation by Chained Equation (MICE) (Azur et al. 2011), MissForest (Stekhoven and Bühlmann 2012) and Matrix/Tensor Completion (MC/TC) (Phien et al. 2016) methods such as spectral regularization algorithms (Mazumder, Hastie, and Tibshirani 2010), and Structural Matrix Completion (SMC) (Zhang et al. 2016) among others. The performance and efficiency of these methods depend on many factors including the type of missing (MCAR, MAR, MNAR), correlation structures of the data, missing percentage and the type of variables. We will discuss the details of these methods in the next chapter.


5.3 Data Preparation

5.3.1 Define the Endpoint or Outcome

For a specific project, it is crucial to determine the endpoint or outcome variable. For example, if the goal of a project is to predict mortality of patients under some conditions or treatments, “mortality” needs to be defined based on the discharge status of a patient at the end of an encounter. It is possible that the mortality or death may not be clearly labeled in the encounter discharge disposition. For example, in the SAH project, a total of 24 types of patient discharge disposition were found in the cohort of SAH patients who received at least one of the three vasopressors (phenylephrine, norepinephrine, and dopamine), see Table 5.3 below. We defined the mortality of a patient if any one of the following 5 categories of discharge disposition was assigned (Williams et al. 2020):

${Death}{indicators}\begin{cases} {1.} & {Expired} \\ {2.} & {{Hospice}/{medical}{facility}.} \\ {3.} & {{Expired}{in} a{medical}{facility}.{Medicaid}{only}{hospice}} \\ {4.} & {{Expired}{at}{home}.\mspace{1mu}{Medicaid}{only},{hospice}} \\ {5.} & {{Hospice}/{home}} \\ \end{cases}$

Table 5.3 The List of Patient Discharge Disposition Descriptions and Corresponding Number of Patients in the Cohort of SAH Patients Who Received at Least One of Three Vasopressors

4 Discharged/transferred to SNF 366

6 Discharged/transferred to home with home health service 115
7 Discharged/transferred to another short-term hospital 110
8 Discharged/transferred to another type of inpatient care institution 59
