Table Version Overall Missing Rate
Cut24hrs 0.78
Cut48hrs 0.75
Cut72hrs 0.76
First24hrs 0.78
First48hrs 0.74
First72hrs 0.75

With this dataset, we could confidently assume that some variables could, at least, partially explain the missing mechanism of data in other columns, so, in our study, we made the assumption that the missing mechanism was MAR. Many others have also made the same assumptions on the basis of sample size [21].

6.4.2 Missing Imputation in EHR Datasets

In this section, we will show our results from using one of the six table versions, Last24hrs, to explore the effect of choosing the best missing rate thresholds and then use four of the aforementioned imputation methods to find the “best way” to deal with missing data in real EHR data.

Figure 6.3 visualizes the missing condition in Last24hrs dataset and changes in missing for different thresholds from 1 to 0.8. The original dataset (top panel) contains 2153 subjects and 195 variables including lab results and clinical event records and several demographic information variables. In each panel, rows and columns represent subjects and variables respectively, cells in black represent missing, and cells in grey represent non missing data?. For example, the second figure excludes all variables with a missing rate equal to 1 (variables with test records for some patients but without any numerical results), and the third figure excludes all variables with a missing rate of at least 0.95.

As missing rate threshold decreases the number of variables remaining (Table 6.3) and consequently the missing rate decrease, presented by diminishing black cells.

Table 6.3 Number of Column Numbers after Dropping Variables Based on Thresholds

Threshold Before Remove Remove Missing Rate = 1 Remove Missing Rate&#160;>&#160;= 0.95 0.94 0.92 0.9 0.8
# of variables left 195 138 105 99 90 88 74
Missing rate after remove 0.78 0.65 0.55 0.53 0.49 0.47 0.40

Table 6.3 shows the numbers of columns remain and the missing rate after dropping variables based on the missing rate.

Figure 6.4 presents the distribution of missing rate under each threshold value, it seems there is a large number of variables with missing rates than 95%. After dropping more variables using smaller thresholds, we found the distribution of missing rates for the remaining variables was closer to be random, which means the missing rates uniformly distributed between 0 to 1. The missing rates and distribution above, emphasize the intensity of the missing problem in EHR datasets, even in a relatively small dataset. These types of severe missing problems are not common in traditional datasets such as clinical trials.

After revealing the missing situation and severity of the dataset, we now apply the selected imputation methods to our case study.

  • I. MICE Imputation

In our study, we used the MICE imputation method, however it failed to return a complete dataset partly because of the extremely high percentage of missing in some variables. A possible explanation for this may be the small number of the available responses (subjects) are not enough to estimate all the model parameters. For example, Aerosol O2, had an initial missing rate of 99%, and MICE failed just based on 1% available data, shown in Figure 6.5.

  • II. MissForest Imputation

Unlike MICE, MissForest always returns a complete dataset, this is because it does not need any model training or parameter estimation.

  • III. Mean and Median Imputation

We consider mean and median imputation among the two naïve methods which both give complete datasets after imputation. We use these two as reference methods to compare AUC values.

6.4.3 Evaluating the Performance of Imputation Methods and Thresholds

In-hospital mortality, which is a binary variable, is the response of interest in this study for which we use logistic regression with LASSO regularization applied to the imputed dataset under different thresholds and imputation methods. The performance of these models is evaluated by AUC.

Table 6.4 shows the results of AUC in different models: the AUC changes from 85% (for MICE applied before removing any variable) to 89% (Under MICE with threshold 0.8) showing a clear increase in average AUC (overall four methods) as the threshold decreases (Figure 6.6). This suggests that removing those variables not only retains any useful information for predicting mortality index, but it also reduces the noise in the data significantly. Now the question is that, can we find a cutoff threshold value after which any decrease would reduce useful information and AUC?

Table 6.4 AUC Values in Each Model

Mean Median MICE MissForest
Remove Missing Rate =1 0.8779 0.866 0.8513 0.8678
Remove Missing Rate > = 0.95 0.8779 0.866 0.8734 0.8764
Remove Missing Rate > = 0.94 0.8843 0.8683 0.8773 0.8708
Remove Missing Rate > = 0.92 0.8801 0.8678 0.8744 0.8657
Remove Missing Rate > = 0.9 0.8801 0.8678 0.8679 0.8644
Remove Missing Rate > = 0.8 0.8813 0.8678 0.8886 0.8657

Figure 6.7 also presents the overall changes in AUC among the four methods and different values of the threshold. The solid line indicates mean imputation method, the dotted line indicates median imputation method, the dashed line indicates MICE imputation and the dotdash line indicates MissForest imputation method.

For MICE imputation, there is a clear decrease in the AUC value with the increase of the threshold. The reason is that, when the threshold value is close to 1, there will be many columns with high missing rates, for which MICE would face problems with fitting the regression models for predicting the missing values. For example, for a variable with sample size 2000 but has only 5 observed values, MICE would fail to estimate many coefficients for a multivariate regression model. MissForest imputation shows a robust performance among these four methods, and the AUC value is slightly increasing with increasing the threshold, which means we can keep more columns.

For the threshold, we can find small peaks in every method at around 0.9 to 0.95, this peak gives us results after we consider the trade-off between throwing high-missing-rate variables and keeping more information, only then can we get a satisfying result from the threshold.


6.5 Discussion and Conclusion

Although dealing with missing data in statistical analyses (with regular datasets) is not a new challenge, studies and developing methodologies to address missing issues in EHR datasets are gaining more attention recently. This is partly because of the nature of the EHR datasets, e.g., they are extremely big with ultrahigh dimensionality, and their missing rate is extremely high; hence, most of the existing methodologies fail when applied to EHR datasets.

In this chapter, we illustrated the fundamental missing issues in EHR datasets and reviewed the existing methodologies in the literature for dealing with the missing data problem. Because of the size of most EHR datasets and their extreme high missing rates, sophisticated likelihood-based methods are quite inefficient or even impossible to implement. Instead, we proposed using common imputation methods. Of course, these methods require some underlying assumptions, such as MAR and strong correlation structure, which need to be carefully considered in each case. Since EHR datasets have a massive number of variables, it might be reasonable to assume an MAR mechanism so we could periodically find a few observed variables to explain the missing mechanism. In addition, we included examples of missing imputation on EHR datasets with truly large dimensions and extremely high missing rates and assessed the efficacy and precision of these methods.

We believe that there is still a big gap in the literature and massive opportunity for developing new methodologies specifically to address the missing data problem in EHR datasets. Combinations of statistical and machine learning (neural networks) approaches, coupled with clinical knowledge are one of the ways to begin these investigations.

7

Causal Inference and Analysis for EHR Data
               

Stacia M. DeSantis, Momiao Xiong, Jose-Miguel Yamal, Gen Zhu, Duo Yu, Xueying Wang, Chenguang Zhang and Vi K. Ly


7.1 Introduction

7.1.1 Why Causal Inference

EHRs are a rich source of demographic and clinical information, including sociodemographics, medical visit times, diagnoses, medications, procedures, vital signs, and lab tests for a large population of patients. They can be an excellent data source from which to implement comparative effectiveness analyses to evaluate known treatments or discover new treatments. However, unbiased estimates of effect can be difficult to obtain from observational data like EHRs. The gold standard for unbiased estimation of treatment effects is the randomized clinical trial (RCT) (Concato, Shah, and Horwitz 2000), wherein treatment is randomly assigned to patients in the study. Effective randomizations alleviate the issue of bias due to confounding in the treatment effect estimation, as baseline confounders should be balanced across randomized groups. However, RCTs are expensive, time-consuming, and often infeasible or even unethical, depending on the clinical setting. An alternative to RCTs is to apply state-of-the-art causal inference techniques to EHR-derived studies.

EHR data analytics have unique challenges. Creating and integrating variables from EHR data to facilitate unbiased inference about the effects of treatments or exposures on health outcomes, remains both a challenge and a priority. In this chapter, we will first introduce several commonly used methods: propensity score method, instrumental variables estimator, and mediation analysis. Then, we will introduce deep learning methods such as generative adversarial networks for causal inference. Following that, we present a targeted minimum loss-based estimation, a modern causal inference method which is especially applicable for big data.

7.1.2 Overview of Causal Inference Methods: Rubin Causal Model (RCM)

A mainstream general framework for treatment effect estimation from observational data is the potential outcome framework where every individual has a set of “potential outcomes” corresponding to all possible treatment levels (Rubin 1978, Ray and Szabó 2019). RCM is also called the Neyman-Rubin counterfactual framework of causality, which was originally created by Neyman (Neyman 1923) and Rubin (Rubin 1974) for studying observational data. The main idea behind the Rubin causal model is the concept of potential outcomes. A counterfactual is defined as the potential outcome that would have happened in the absence of the cause (Shadish, Cook, and Campbell 2002). An individual observes only one of the potential outcomes that correspond to the assigned treatment, which is called the factual outcome. The other potential outcomes are missing and unobserved. The unobserved potential outcomes are called counterfactual outcomes or simply counterfactuals. For example, when analyzing the effect of vasopressor on death for SAH patients, we want to know the difference in the potential outcome of a patient if he/she received the treatment (vasopressor) versus the potential outcome if he/she did not receive the treatment. A causal effect is defined as the difference between the two potential outcomes. The key assumption of the counterfactual framework is that each individual in the population of interest has a potential outcome under each treatment state. In reality, one can only observe one of the potential outcomes in one treatment state, as one patient can be assigned either in a treatment group or a control group. Within this framework, the estimation problem inherently becomes a missing data problem, whereby estimating treatment effects requires properly imputing the counterfactuals (Ding and Li 2018).

A plethora of statistical methods have been developed for causal inference under this counterfactual paradigm. One commonly used approach to estimating counterfactuals is simply matching patients on underlying risk factors, which is a systematic way to approximately impute the counterfactuals by borrowing information from the matched subjects (Paul Rosenbaum and Rubin 1983, Luo and Zhu 2019). Matching methods that have been developed including Mahalanobis metric matching, propensity score matching (Rosenbaum and Rubin 1985), full matching (Rosenbaum 1991), genetic matching (Diamond and Sekhon 2013), and deep instrumental variables networks (Hartford et al. 2017).

7.1.3 Basic Framework in Causality: Potential Outcome Framework

In EHR databases, comparisons of treatments are often more complex than a simple two-group comparison. Consider, for example, when comparing drugs administered, there may be multiple drugs given, with varying doses, and various types of outcomes to evaluate the effectiveness or safety of those treatments. We introduce a general framework that allows for the consideration of multiple treatments, discrete and continuous treatments, and binary and continuous outcomes (Ge et al. 2020). Consider K treatments. Let Wi = [Wi1,….,WiK]T be the treatment assignment vector for the ith individual, where Wik = 1 indicates that the ith individual receives the kth treatment and Wik = 0 indicates that the ith individual does not receive the kth treatment. We assume that there is precisely one non-zero component of the vector Wi and its index is denoted by η, i.e., Wiη = 1. Thus, we have $\sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W_{i}^{k} = 1}$. Define the treatment vector Ti = [Ti1,…,TiK]T, where Tik is the kth treatment variable which the ith individual receives. It can be binary, categorical, or continuous. Only, Tiη is given, other components of the treatment vector Ti can be arbitrary. Let ${\overline{Y}}_{i} = \left\lbrack {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{1},\ldots,{\overline{Y}}_{i}^{K}\rbrack^{T} \right.$ be the vector of potential outcomes for the ith individual, where ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}$ is the potential outcome if the ith individual had received the kth treatment. We only observe one component of the potential outcome vector ${\overline{Y}}_{i}$ that corresponds to the assigned treatment Wiη, which is called the factual outcome and is denoted by ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$, where f = η. Other unobserved potential outcomes are called counterfactual outcomes, or simply counterfactuals, and denoted by ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}}$. The factual outcome ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$ can be expressed as ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f} = \sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W_{i}^{k}{\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}}$.

For example, if $W_{i} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \\ \end{bmatrix}$ , then η = 2 and ${\overline{Y}}_{i} = \begin{bmatrix} {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}} \\ {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f} \\ {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}} \\ \end{bmatrix}$. Each individual is associated with K potential outcomes ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}$ , but only one of them can be observed as ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$. We assume that a vector of p covariates Xi = [xi1,…,xip]T is associated with the ith individual, including the patient demographics and clinical variables. Suppose that n individuals are sampled.

We make the following assumptions (Rubin 1980, Rosenbaum and Rubin 1983).

ASSUMPTION 1: For all covariates X and all treatment k, k = 1, …, K, we have

0 < P(Wik=1|X=x) < 1.(7.1)

This assumption ensures that at every point in the feature space, there is a non-zero probability of being given treatment Wik for every i.

ASSUMPTION 2: (Unconfoundedness). Conditional on the covariates X, the potential outcomes $\overline{Y}$ and treatments W are independent:

$\overline{Y}⫫W|X.$(7.2)

ASSUMPTION 3: (The standard stable unit treatment value assumption (SUTVA)). There is no interference between units and no different versions of treatment.

Average and Individual Treatment Effects

The most widely used measure of the treatment effect for multiple treatments is the pair-wise treatment effect. The individual effect ξjk(i) between the pairwise treatments: Tij and Tik is defined as $\xi_{\mathit{jk}}^{(i)} = {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{j} - {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}$, the average pairwise treatment effect (ATE) is defined as τjk = E[ξjk(i)]. The average pairwise treatmento effect τjk|Tj on the patients treated with Tj is defined as $\tau_{\mathit{jk}|T_{j}} = E{\lbrack{\xi_{\mathit{jk}}^{(i)}\mspace{-4mu}|\mspace{-4mu} T_{j}}\rbrack}$. The focus of this chapter is on the conditional distribution of treatment effect, given the feature vector X. Assume that n individuals are sampled. For the ith individual, Tη treatment (Wiη=1) is assigned. Let Xi and ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\eta} = {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$ be the observed feature vector and the observed potential outcome of the ith individual. Therefore, the observed dataset is given by $D = \left( {X_{i},T_{i},{W_{i},\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\eta},i = 1,\ldots,n} \right)$. The factual and counterfactual outcomes of the ith individual is denoted by ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$ and ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}}$, respectively. The observed outcome ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{obs}}$ of the ith individual is equal to ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f} = {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\eta} = \sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W_{i}^{k}{\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}}$ and the other potential outcomes (counterfactuals) ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}} = {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{mis}} = \left( {1 - W_{i}^{k}} \right){\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k},k = 1,\ldots,K$ are missing.


7.2 Propensity Scoring

7.2.1 Brief Introduction

Propensity scoring methods are among the most commonly used causal inference methods. Propensity scoring seeks to eliminate the bias that results from the analysis of non-randomized observational studies by creating comparison groups that are similar to observed baseline confounders, but differ according to the treatment or exposure received (Yanovitzky, Zanutto, and Hornik 2005). It involves two steps: Step 1) fitting a propensity model to estimate the probability of being treated in order to obtain a balancing score, and Step 2) using the balancing score to adjust for bias to obtain causal inference. Adjustment methods include stratification, matching, and inverse probability weighting (IPW) in the outcome model, each with well-studied benefits and limitations (Austin and Stuart 2015).

Treatments derived from EHR-derived comparative effectiveness studies may be binary, ordinal, or nominal (i.e., mutually exclusive). Adaption of propensity scoring for EHR-derived studies requires an explanation of existing methods for multiple treatments (Imai and Van Dyk 2004, Brown et al. 2020, Fong, Hazlettand, and Imai 2018), high-dimensional confounders, and machine learning adaptations that have shown promise in the available literature (Mccaffrey et al. 2013, Burgette, Griffin, and Mccaffrey 2020, Ju et al. 2019, Brown et al. 2020).

7.2.2 Propensity Scoring for Binary Treatments

For binary treatments, a propensity (or balancing) score may be estimated from a logistic, probit (Rosenbaum and Rubin 1983) or more recently, machine learning algorithms such as random forests, neural nets, and generalized gradient boosting methods (GBM) (McCaffrey, Ridgeway, and Morral 2004, Setoguchi et al. 2008, Mccaffrey et al. 2013). The resulting propensity score is then used to adjust for covariate imbalance (Imbens 2000). This enables estimation in Step 2, of the two causal estimands of interest, presented here for the K multiple treatments case.

The average treatment effect in the treated (ATT), ATTk|k, k′′ = E(Yi(k)) − Yi(k′)|Yi=k], is the expected outcome between treatment k vs k′ for people treated with k.McCaffrey et al. (2013) Unlike ATE, the ATT distinguishes who within the population is the reference (k). There are (K/2) ATEs and K! ATTs.

7.2.3 Propensity Scoring for Multiple Treatments

In a study of treatment effects on a disease outcome, multiple treatments would often be of interest, thus applying multiple treatments propensity scoring approaches would be the norm for EHR data. Multiple treatments propensity scoring is more complex than the binary case described above. In fact, in contrast to the binary setting, there is no proposed universal scalar balancing score that captures the complex relation of counterfactuals to balance confounders across all K treatments, which is required to produce ATE/ATTs defined above. That is because any multiple treatments propensity model will produce a vector (rather than a scalar) of “generalized propensity scores” (GPS), i.e., ${\widehat{\mathbf{P}}}_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{GPS}} = {({{\widehat{p}}_{i,1},\ldots,{\widehat{p}}_{i,K}})}$, which may result from a multinomial regression or machine learning model, e.g., $\mathit{\log}{\lbrack{P\frac{Z_{i} = \mathit{k|}X_{i} = x_{i}}{Z_{i} = k^{\prime}|X_{i} = x_{i}}}\rbrack}$ = θk + xi′βk, where the left side is a regression of treatment assignment, k, on covariate vector, X, θk is a constant, and βk is a vector of K − 1 regression coefficients (Joffe and Rosenbaum 1999, Rassen et al. 2013, Tu, Jiao, and Koh 2013, Seya and Yoshida 2017, Lopez and Gutman 2017, Imbens 2000, Imai and Van Dyk 2004). Each GPS element is the conditional probability of receiving treatment k. Although how to reduce the GPS vector to a scalar in Step 2 remains ad hoc and understudied, recent work has used distance metrics and clustering tools to stratify or match it on the GPS vector. For example, once the GPS is obtained in Step 1, one can minimize Mahalonabis multivariate distance, (Imbens 2000, Imai and Van Dyk 2004, Rassen et al. 2013, Seya and Yoshida 2017) conduct “within-trio” distance matching (for K = 3 only), Rassen et al. (2013) or use clustering techniques to group those with similar GPS vectors (Tu, Jiao, and Koh 2013, Lopez and Gutman 2017) . These adjustment methods are basically extensions of the binary setting and are generally limited in the number of treatments and type of adjustment they allow (e.g., usually, matching on K = 3 treatments limited to an ATT outcome analysis, is supported). Reproducible research has also shown that distance-based approaches are not appropriate for higher-dimensional covariate vectors (Gu and Rosenbaum 1993, Stuart 2010), which are common in EHR-based studies. One must be careful in selecting which approach to apply when estimating ATEs or ATTs—existing distance/clustering methods of adjustment typically estimate average potential outcomes separately for each treatment level rather than constructing balanced strata or matched pairs (Yang et al. 2016). In fact, several approaches are tailored to estimating ATTs (versus ATEs), which may or may not be the estimand of interest for a given comparative effectiveness study derived from EHRs (since ATT is only generalizable to the subjects in a treated reference group) (Lopez and Gutman 2017). Finally, some methods are computationally impractical, (Huang et al. 2005, Tu, Jiao, and Koh 2013), only applicable to certain outcome types, may not produce good balancing scores, and/or do not apply when K > 3 (Harder, Stuart, and Anthony 2010, Fong, Hazlettand, and Imai 2018). Propensity scoring methods that readily lend themselves to “big data” causal analysis from EHR-derived studies—i.e., studies that simultaneously examine multiple treatments with complex covariate spaces—should be further studied. While research has been and is continually being developed, there continues to be a gap in propensity scoring methodology for multiple treatments in the context of big data sets, where both multiple treatments and high-dimensional covariates are simultaneously of interest.

7.2.4 Propensity Scoring for Ordinal Treatments

Ordinal propensity scoring is a special instance of multiple-treatment propensity scoring, described above, which may arise in EHR data. For example, one may be interested in the effect of the dose of a second-generation serotonin selective reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) on remission of depression symptoms or a dose of a statin on prevention of heart failure, other cardiovascular outcomes, or death. Clearly, methods for ordinal propensity scoring would differ from the multiple treatment case; for example, they might exploit the natural underlying treatment structure such as the dose-response relationship between treatment and covariates. In ordinal propensity scoring, challenges occur both in Step 1.) choosing an appropriate propensity model and Step 2.) adjusting for the balancing/propensity score to obtain ATEs. While GPS methodologies from multinomial treatments are applicable here (Imbens 2000, Imai and Van Dyk 2004). One could gain efficiency in causal estimates by exploiting the ordinal nature of the treatment/covariate relationship. Proportional odds (PO) regression model, an important extension of binary logistic regression for ordinal outcomes, is the most obvious way to do this—it can be used to produce a scalar balancing score, i.e., the linear predictor, $\mathbf{X}_{i}\widehat{\mathbf{\beta}}$, for a given covariate vector, Xi, on which patients can be matched or stratified (B. Lu et al. 2001, Zanutto, Lu, and Hornik 2005, Greene et al. 2017). In Step 2, one can apply non-bipartite matching based on the value of this balancing score, and the value of the observed (actual) treatment level (B. Lu et al. 2001). This Mahalonobis-type metric facilitates matching patients who received different levels of ordinal treatment. If the PO assumption were correct for every variable in the propensity model such that a “good” balancing score could be produced, then Xiβ̂ would be an excellent dimension reducing the scalar balancing score. However given the noisy big data challenges pertinent to contemporary statistical analysis, strict parametric propensity models are less useful (McCaffrey, Ridgeway, and Morral 2004, Mccaffrey et al. 2013). Instead, machine learning propensity models are useful alternatives, (Burgette, Griffin, and Mccaffrey 2020, Ju et al. 2019, Wyss et al. 2018, Mccaffrey et al. 2013, Greene et al. 2020) as they accommodate high-dimensional covariate spaces, multi-order terms, and some incorporate implicit interactions in Step 1. Methods for propensity score adjustment in Step 2 must, therefore, be adapted for seamless use with machine learning propensity models fitted in Step 1 to facilitate big data causal analysis. Methods for adjustment for ordinal treatments in the context of many and complex covariate spaces are still under development.

7.2.5 Propensity Score Estimation for Complex Data Sets

Studies derived from EHR data are likely to result in “complex” confounder-treatment associations e.g., a high-dimensional covariate space, interactions, higher-order, and nonlinear terms are likely to simultaneously operate on the treatment assignment as well as the outcome. The class of non-parametric machine learning methods of propensity score estimation, including ensemble methods, random forests, GBM, recursive partitioning, and neural nets (Setoguchi et al. 2008, F. Li, Morgan, and Zaslavsky 2018, McCaffrey, Ridgeway, and Morral 2004, Mccaffrey et al. 2013, Ju et al. 2019) are all useful for the analysis of EHR data and should be considered for application in high-dimensional confounder settings. Specifically, GBM has very recently been adapted to the multiple treatments setting in order to capture complex treatment/covariate associations than cannot be captured by parametric models, and a newly published software package implements GBM propensity scoring in the freeware R (Burgette, Griffin, and Mccaffrey 2020, Brown et al. 2020). The software also produces the requisite balance diagnostics required to assess the utility of the propensity score in balancing treated and control patients on confounders. Which machine learning method (e.g., super learners, random forests, or neural nets) is used in Step 1 is not usually of primary importance because the model selection and overparameterization are not as much an issue in propensity scoring as they are in typical association studies. Thus, it is generally well-accepted that these methods, which automatically incorporate variable selection, interactions, the decision to include higher-order terms and optimize covariate balance across k, probably produce at least equivalent propensity scores with respect to resultant covariate balance in high-dimensional settings such as EHR-derived studies, versus their parametric counterparts (Imai and Ratkovic 2013, Mccaffrey et al. 2013, F. Li, Morgan, and Zaslavsky 2018, Burgette, Griffin, and Mccaffrey 2020).

Machine learning propensity models have typically been coupled with IPW in Step 2, where the ATE weight is the “inverse probability of the treatment the person actually received” (Mccaffrey et al. 2013, Austin and Stuart 2015, Ju et al. 2019), since IPW is most sensitive to misspecification of the propensity model. However, some research has shown that it can lead to biased estimates of the ATEs in the high-dimensional confounder settings especially since the propensity model (in addition to the outcome model) is likely to be misspecified (Rubin 2004; Stuart 2010, Imai and Ratkovic 2013, Greene et al. 2020, Brown et al. 2020). Alternatively, matching and stratification are less sensitive to misspecification, and the former may be a more useful adjustment method when there are a large number of control treatments available in the database, as is typically true of EHR-derived studies (Brown et al. 2020). Both matching and stratification, as opposed to weighting, are also easier methods for testing covariate balance even though methods for matching and stratification are yet to be developed.

In the EHR setting with complex covariate space, both propensity and outcome model misspecification are obviously most likely to be issues. It has been well-studied (Feng et al. 2012, Greene et al. 2020, Fong, Hazlettand, and Imai 2018, Imai and Ratkovic 2013) that when propensity and outcome models are both misspecified, biases in causal effects are large, even when applying what appears to be the latest state-of-the-art methods. Thus, the application of robust machine learning propensity models combined with appropriate adjustment procedures may help avoid biased effect sizes for big data analytics. To this end, ongoing research adapting machine learning methods for use with IPW, matching, and stratification (rather than solely IPW) may offer more robust, efficient, and versatile methods for big data causal analysis than are currently available.

7.2.6 Illustration Example: Subarachnoid Hemorrhage (SAH) Project

We present a study derived from the Cerner Health Facts database that determined the association between vasopressors and mortality in patients with non-traumatic and traumatic aneurysmal subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH). The study population has been previously described (Williams et al. 2020). To summarize, the Cerner Health Facts EHR database was queried from years 2000 to 2015 to select adult patients (over age 17) with a new diagnosis of aneurysmal SAH based on ICD-9 code 430. Only patients who received infusions of dopamine, phenylephrine, or norepinephrine were included in the study population (Williams et al. 2020). Among the 4,850 patients that met the above study inclusion criteria, 40 patients presented with multiple first vasopressor treatments and were thus excluded from the cohort. Furthermore, patients whose diagnosis included a traumatic cause of SAH (based on ICD 9 codes: 800.2x, 800.7x, 801.2x, 801.7x, 803.2x, 803.7x, 804.2x, 804.7x, 852.x) or with unknown mortality status were also excluded from the study population leaving 2,634 patients in the final cohort.

The propensity score analysis presented here includes 2,417 patients with complete data for demographic variables (age, gender, race, and marital status) as well as 170 pretreatment medication variables. Of the patients included in the analysis, 492, 1,253, and 672 were administered dopamine, phenylephrine, and norepinephrine, respectively. All demographic and pretreatment variables were entered into a GBM model. More details on variable selection, as well as precise variables included in the propensity model, can be found in previous studies (Williams et al. 2020, Brown et al. 2020).

As all patients were eligible for all three treatments, ATE is the estimand of interest. First, a visual representation of the covariate balance achieved after applying IPW is depicted in Figure 7.1. Standardized mean difference (SMD) is a measure to compare the similarity of subjects in the matched sample (Austin 2011). In each comparison group, e.g., dopamine vs phenylephrine, SMD can be calculated for each covariate. The left plot shows maximum pairwise SMD for each potential pretreatment confounder. Similarly, the right plot shows the average pairwise SMD, which is calculated by averaging the SMD for each potential pretreatment confounder across treatment pairs. Literature suggests that values of average SMD less than 0.2 indicate small levels of covariate imbalance (Mccaffrey et al. 2013). Based on this cutoff, the balance was reasonably achieved for most covariates (Figure  7.1).

Around 40% of the nontraumatic patients had at least one GCS record and GCS was not well-balanced using IPW. Therefore, we conducted propensity score matching instead and found GCS scores and demographic factors were well balanced between each pair of treatments.
