7.3 Mediation Analysis

7.3.1 Introduction to Mediation Analysis

There are many ways to hypothesize the causal relationship between outcome and variables. For example, in the aforementioned example of the SAH study, we may hypothesize that the association between vasopressors and mortality are influenced by a mediator. The concept of mediation has been used in social science and psychology literature for many decades (e.g., Rucker et al. 2011). Mediation analysis attempts to characterize how the exposure or treatment affects an intermediate variable, and how the affected intermediate variable influences the outcome. This provides insight into biological mechanisms or pathways by which an exposure or treatment affects an outcome. In our SAH example, it is possible the vasopressor influences a patient outcome through a series of processes involving different intermediate stages. One could build and test such a mediation model, but this is beyond the scope of this chapter.

Like the methods above, meditation methods can be illustrated via a so-called “counterfactual” framework for causal inference, which is now widely being used as a tool in formal methodological work in causal inference, statistics, economics, etc. The framework serves as a formal and technical notation to describe the causation. In this section, we will not use the formal notation in the “counterfactual” framework to introduce the mediation analysis. Instead, we will describe the regression-based mediation intuitively, without resorting to the technical notation of the counterfactual ideology. For the interested reader, several texts give more details of the counterfactual framework.

7.3.2 The Product Method

The product method was greatly influenced by the work of Baron and Kenny (Baron and Kenny 1986). The causal diagram (Figure 7.2) used in (Baron and Kenny 1986) is a minimalist figure that illustrates the relationship between the cause, mediator, and outcome, in which A denotes an exposure variable, M denotes the mediator, and Y denotes the outcome variable. Let assume other baseline variables as C. Consider the case when the outcome and the mediator are continuous. The following regression models could help explain the effect of “with-the-mediator” and “without-the-mediator”.

E(M|A=a,C=c) = β0 + β1a + β2′c(7.3)

E(Y|A=a,M=m,C=c) = θ0 + θ1a + θ2m + θ4′c(7.4)

θ1 can be used to access the direct effect which is a measure to see the influence of the cause C on the outcome that is not through the mediator. But here the effect of the mediator and other covariates should be controlled by directly pooling them together into one regression model. It can be understood as the treatment effect on the outcome at a specific level of the mediator. Or one can treat the estimated effect θ1 to the outcome as the effect that is directly relative to the intermediate of interest. There will likely be other intermediates or mechanisms that account for other aspects of the effect of the cause on the outcome (T. VanderWeele 2015). The effect of cause on the outcome through the intermediate is the indirect effect. By this definition, β1θ2 is the estimator to access the indirect effect, which is the coefficient of the exposure the model 7.3 times the coefficient of the mediator in model 7.4.

7.3.3 The Difference Method

The difference method has been widely applied in epidemiology and the biomedical sciences. We will continue to use two regression models for an explanation. Consider the following regression models:

E(Y|A=a,C=c) = ϕ0 + ϕ1a + ϕ4′c(7.5)

E(Y|A=a,M=m,C=c) = θ0 + θ1a + θ2m + θ4′c(7.6)

If the exposure coefficient of model 7.5, ϕ1, without the mediator, drops significantly when comparing it with the exposure coefficient of the model 7.4 with the mediator, θ1, it may indicate the effect of mediation because it seems the mediator M has some impact on the effect of the exposure on the outcome (T. J. VanderWeele 2016). The difference ϕ1 − θ1 is commonly referred to as a mediated or indirect effect. Similar to the product method, the exposure coefficient in the model 7.6, θ1, is interpreted as the direct effect.

For the two methods, when the outcome and mediator are continuous and we employ the linear regression model by ordinary least squares, the estimated direct effect and indirect effect are the same. But note that for other scenarios wherein the model is fitted by logistic regression, the product method and the difference method will generate different estimates for the effects.

7.3.4 Other Considerations

To ensure the conclusion drawn from the mediation analysis is reliable, four strong assumptions must be satisfied. Basically, all these assumptions are made for the confounding influence. First, it is essential to justify that the confounding factors have been adjusted in the process of mediation analysis. For the details of the assumptions, the reader can refer to (T. VanderWeele 2015, T. J. VanderWeele 2016). Since the assumptions are strong and can be easily disregarded in practice, sensitivity analysis is necessary to examine the robustness of the conclusion in a situation where some of the assumptions are not met. Commonly used sensitivity analysis techniques for mediation can be found in the related literature (Imai, Keele, and Tingley 2010, Hafeman 2011, T. J. VanderWeele 2016).

When considering the interaction between the exposure and a mediator, the estimated direct effect and the estimated indirect effect formulae will change, and also the interpretation will be different. Fortunately, many studies have been done to account for the interaction in mediation analysis. For the interested reader, (T. VanderWeele 2015, T. J. VanderWeele 2016) would be good materials.

In this section, we only talk about mediation analysis in the context of ordinary regression models. For other statistical models, such as the logistic regression model and survival models, the idea of the indirect effect and the direct effect can also be generalizable to these situations. Readers could review the paper (Vanderweele and Vansteelandt 2010) for further explanation.


7.4 Instrumental Variables Networks for Treatment Effect Estimation in the Presence of Unmeasured Confounders

7.4.1 Instrumental Variables Frameworks

Instrumental variables (IV) framework is a popular class of methods for the estimation of treatment effects in the presence of unmeasured confounders (Usaid Awan et al. 2020). Consider a trio for treatment effect estimation (Figure 7.3). The trio intends to estimate a causal effect of a treatment T on an outcome Y. However, both treatment T and outcome Y can be affected by an observed confounder U. To remove the effect of confounder U, we use an instrumental variable Z. The only observed variables are T, Y and Z. The following three assumptions are essential to the success of the IV approach.

  1. The instrument variable Z is associated with the treatment T.

  2. The instrument variable Z is independent of the outcome Y given the treatment T and all confounders U, i.e, Y ⫫ Z |(T,U).

  3. The instrument variable Z is independent of factors (measured and unmeasured) that confound the treatment-outcome relationship, i.e, Z ⫫ U.

Any violation of the three assumptions will lead to the failure of the instrument approach. The variation autoencoder (VAE) and principal component methods for dimension reduction can be used for finding instrument variables. However, the instrumental variables are problem-dependent. For example, they are commonly used to estimate exposure-outcome relationships in EHR studies where a proxy that serves as a semi-randomized variable (such as hospital volume) can be discerned from the records. The selection of appropriate instrument variables is key for the success of the treatment effect estimation in the presence of unmeasured confounders and additional details are beyond the scope of this chapter.

7.4.2 Two-Stage Least Square Methods with Linear Models

The methods for IV analysis include two-stage least squares (2SLS) (Angrist, Graddy, and Imbens 2000), generalized method of moments (Hansen 1982, Bennett, Kallus, and Tobias 2019), the ratio of coefficients method (Burgess, Small, and Thompson 2017), and likelihood-based methods (Hayashi 2000). In this chapter, we focus on the most popular 2SLS method for IV analysis. The 2SLS method consists of two regression stages: the first-stage regression of the treatment variable on the IVs, and the second-stage regression of the potential outcome on the fitted values of the treatment from the first stage.

Simple Linear Models

Consider the causal model in Figure 7.3.

T = βT|GG + βT|UU(7.7)

Y = βY|TT + βY|UU,(7.8)

where T represents a treatment, Y is the outcome, U is the confounding variable and an instrument variable G. We assume that the instrument variable G is independent of the confounding variable U and is associated with the treatment T. The goal of the IV approach is the estimation of a causal effect of the treatment T on the outcome Y.

Covariance Analysis

Without loss of generality, we can assume that Var(G) = 1 and Var(T) = 1. By the assumption that the unconfounding variable U and the instrument variable G are independent, we conclude cov(G,U) = 0. (Figure 7.3)

Taking covariance with G on both sides of equation (7.7) yields

cov(T,G) = βT|G.(7.9)

Similarly, taking covariance with G on both sides of equation (7.8), we obtain

cov(Y,G) = βY|Tcov(T,G), which implies that

$\beta_{Y|T} = \frac{\mathit{cov}\left( {Y,G} \right)}{\mathit{cov}\left( {T,G} \right)}.$(7.10)

Define the regression of Y on G:

Y = βY|GG.(7.11)

Taking covariance with G, we obtain

cov(Y,G) = βY|G.(7.12)

Substituting equations (7.9) and (7.12) into equation (7.10) leads to

$\beta_{Y|T} = \frac{\beta_{Y|G}}{\beta_{T|G}}.$

The causal effect of the treatment T on outcome Y is estimated by ${\widehat{\beta}}_{\mathit{Y|T}} = \frac{\beta_{\mathit{Y|G}}}{\beta_{\mathit{T|G}}}$.

Generalized Least Square Estimator

Structural equations for modeling IV model in equations (7.7) and (7.8) are

 − Y + βY|TT + βY|UU + ε1 = 0,(7.13)

 − T + βT|GG + βT|UU + ε2 = 0(7.14)

where ε1, ε2 are uncorrelated with U. Let

βY|UU + ε1 = e1,(7.15)

βT|UU + ε2 = e2.(7.16)

Substituting equations (7.14) and (7.16) into equations (7.13) and (7.14), we obtain

 − Y + βY|TT + e1 = 0,(7.17)

 − T + βT|GG + e2 = 0,(7.18)

where y, T are endogenous variables (its value is determined or influenced by one or more of the independent variables) and denoted by W = [yT], G is an exogenous variable (a variable that is not affected by other variables) and e1, e2 are uncorrelated with G.

Let $\Gamma = \begin{bmatrix} {- 1} & 0 \\ \beta_{Y|T} & {- 1} \\ \end{bmatrix}$, $B = {\lbrack\begin{matrix} 0 & \beta_{T|G} \\ \end{matrix}\rbrack},E = \begin{bmatrix} e_{1} & e_{2} \\ \end{bmatrix}$, Then $\Gamma^{- 1} = \begin{bmatrix} {- 1} & {- \beta_{Y|T}} \\ 0 & {- 1} \\ \end{bmatrix}$.

Then, Equations (7.17) and (7.18) can be rewritten in a matrix form as

WΓ + GB + E = 0.(7.19)

Multiplying by Γ − 1 on both sides of Equation (7.19), we obtain W + GBΓ − 1 + EΓ − 1 = 0, which can be reduced to

W =  − GBΓ − 1 − EΓ − 1 = GΠ + V(7.20)

where $\Pi = - B\Gamma^{- 1},\Pi = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & \beta_{T|G} \\ \end{bmatrix}$ and V =  − EΓ − 1.

Using least square methods to solve the optimization problem (7.20), we obtain

Π = (GTG) − 1GTW.(7.21)

Using the relation ΠΓ =  − B, we obtain (GTG) − 1GTWΓ =  − B, which implies GTWΓ =  − GTGB or

$G^{T}\begin{bmatrix} Y & T \\ \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix} {- 1} & 0 \\ \beta_{Y|T} & {- 1} \\ \end{bmatrix} = {- G}^{T}\begin{bmatrix} 0 & {G\beta_{T|G}} \\ \end{bmatrix}.$(7.22)

Expanding Equation (7.22), we obtain $G^{T}{\lbrack\begin{matrix} {- Y + T\beta_{\mathit{Y|T}}} & {- T} \\ \end{matrix}\rbrack} = G^{T}{\lbrack\begin{matrix} 0 & {G\beta_{\mathit{T|G}}} \\ \end{matrix}\rbrack}$. Therefore, we have

GTY = GTTβY|T,(7.23)

GTT = GTGβT|G.(7.24)

Equations (7.23) and (7.24) can be rewritten as

$G^{T}\begin{bmatrix} Y \\ T \\ \end{bmatrix} = G^{T}T\begin{bmatrix} \beta_{Y|T} \\ \beta_{X|G} \\ \end{bmatrix}.$(7.25)

Equations (7.17) and (7.18) can be rewritten as

$\begin{bmatrix} Y \\ T \\ \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} T & 0 \\ 0 & G \\ \end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix} \beta_{Y|T} \\ \beta_{T|G} \\ \end{bmatrix} + \begin{bmatrix} e_{1} \\ e_{2} \\ \end{bmatrix} = Z\delta + e,$(7.26)

where $Z = \begin{bmatrix} T & 0 \\ 0 & G \\ \end{bmatrix}$ , $\delta = \begin{bmatrix} \beta_{Y|T} \\ \beta_{T|G} \\ \end{bmatrix}$ and $e = \begin{bmatrix} e_{1} \\ e_{2} \\ \end{bmatrix}$.

Multiplying both sides of Equation (7.26) by GT, we obtain

GTWT = GTZδ + GTe.(7.27)

Note that

Σ = cov(GTe,GTe) = GTΛG,(7.28)

where$\Lambda = \begin{bmatrix} \left( \beta_{Y|U})^{2}\sigma_{U}^{2} + \sigma_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{2} \right. & {\beta_{Y|U}\beta_{T|U}\sigma_{U}^{2}} \\ {\beta_{Y|U}\beta_{T|U}\sigma_{U}^{2}} & {\left( \beta \right._{T|U})^{2}\sigma_{U}^{2} + \sigma_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{2}} \\ \end{bmatrix}$

Using weighted least square methods, we obtain the generalized least square estimator:

δ̂ = [ZTG(GTΛG) − 1GTZ] − 1ZTG(GTΛG) − 1GTWT.(7.29)

If we assume Λ = σ2I, then Equation (7.29) is reduced to

δ̂ = [ZTG(GTG) − 1GTZ] − 1ZTG(GTG) − 1GTWT.(7.30)

Two-Stage Least Square Method

Now we study the relationship between the generalized least square estimator and two-stage least square estimator.

Let

$\widehat{Z} = \begin{bmatrix} \widehat{T} & 0 \\ 0 & G \\ \end{bmatrix},$(7.31)

whereX̂ = G(GTG) − 1GTX.

Equation (7.31) can be reduced to

$\widehat{Z} = \begin{bmatrix} {G\left( G^{T}G)^{- 1}G^{T}X \right.} & 0 \\ 0 & {GG^{T}G)^{- 1}G^{T}G} \\ \end{bmatrix} = G\left( G \right.^{T}G)^{- 1}G^{T}Z.$(7.32)

Consider two-stage least square estimator:

Stage 1

Solving the regression

T = GβT|G + eT,(7.33)

we obtain

β̂T|G = (GTG) − 1GTT and

T̂ = G(GTG) − 1GTT.(7.34)

Stage 2

Solving the regressionWT = Ẑδ + ew,we obtain

δ̂ = (ẐTẐ) − 1ẐTWT.(7.35)

Substituting Equation (7.32) into Equation (7.35), we obtain

$\begin{array}{l} {\widehat{\delta} = {\lbrack Z^{T}G{(G^{T}G)}^{- 1}G^{T}G{(G^{T}G)}^{- 1}G^{T}Z\rbrack}^{- 1}Z^{T}G{(G^{T}G)}^{- 1}G^{T}W^{T}} \\ {\quad = {\lbrack Z^{T}G{(G^{T}G)}^{- 1}G^{T}Z\rbrack}^{- 1}Z^{T}G{(G^{T}G)}^{- 1}G^{T}W^{T},} \\ \end{array}$(7.36)

which is the same as the Equation (7.30).

Nonlinear Models for Two-Stage Least Squares Approach

Here we consider nonlinear models for the 2SLS approach to the treatment effect estimation where covariate variable X is included (Figure 7.4). Introducing the following nonlinear structural equation models with additive latent errors that include the confounders (Mohamed et al. 2019, Hartford et al. 2017).

Y = g(T,X) + e,(7.37)

where Y is the outcome variable, Tis the treatment variable, X is a vector of covariates, g(.,.) is a nonlinear function, e is the error including the unobserved confounders. We assume that E[e] = 0. We also assume that the treatment variable is endogenous and is correlated with the error, i.e., E[e|X,T] ≠ 0 and E[eT|X] ≠ 0.

Define the potential outcome prediction function (Hartford et al. 2017)

h(T,X) = g(T,X) + E[e|X].(7.38)

Recall that we assume that the treatment variable is uncorrelated with the error, i.e.,

E[e|T,X] ≠ E[e|X],(7.39)

which implies that

E[Y|T,x] = g(T,X) + E[e|T,X] ≠ g(T,X) + E[e|X] = h(T,X).(7.40)

Since E[e|X,T] ≠ 0 and is unknown, Equation (7.40) shows that E[Y|T,x] cannot be directly estimated and cannot be estimated by h(T,X). Two unknown quantities g(T,X) and E[e|T,X] require two equations to solve it. Similar to the 2SLS in the linear models, we introduce instrument variables that allow the establishing of two equations. The previous core assumptions for the IV approach need to be modified to take the covariates into consideration. The new three assumptions (Hartford et al. 2017) are

SETP 1:
(Relevance): The distribution F(T|X,Z) of the treatment T, given X and Z, is not constant in Z, i.e, F(T|X,Z) depends on Z.

STEP 2:
Z ⫫ Y|(X,T,e).

STEP 3:
(Unconfounded Instrument): Instrument variable Z is independent of the error, given the covariates X, i.e., $Z⫫\mathit{e|X}\mspace{-4mu}$ . Assumption implies

E[e|X,Z] = E[e|X].(7.41)

It follows from Equation (7.37) that

E[Y|X,Z] = E[g(T,X)|X,Z] + E[e|X,Z].(7.42)

By definition of the conditional distribution, we obtain

E[g(T,X)|X,Z] = ∫g(T,X)dF(T,X|X,Z) = ∫g(T,X)df(T|X,Z).(7.43)

By assumption 3, we obtain

$\begin{array}{l} {E\left\lbrack e \middle| X,Z \right\rbrack = \int{edF\left( e \middle| X,Z \right)}} \\ {\quad = \int{edF\left( e,T \middle| X,Z \right)}} \\ {\quad = \int{edF\left( e \middle| X,Z7 \right)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right)}} \\ {\quad = \int{edF\left( e \middle| X \right)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right).}} \\ \end{array}$(7.44)

Combining Equations (7.42)–(7.44), we obtain

$\begin{array}{l} {E\left\lbrack Y \middle| X,Z \right\rbrack = \int{\left\lbrack {g\left( {T,X} \right) + E\left\lbrack e \middle| X \right\rbrack} \right\rbrack dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right)}} \\ {\quad = \pm \int{h\left( {T,X} \right)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right)}} \\ \end{array}$(7.45)

To solve Equation (7.45) for h(T,X), we solve the following optimization problem:

$\min\limits_{h{({T,X})} \in H}{\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left( Y_{i} - \int{h\left( {T,X} \right)\mathit{dF}\left( T|X_{i},Z_{i})^{2} \right.} \right.},$(7.46)

where H is a function space.

To remove the correlation between the treatment and error (confounder), we divide the estimation into two stages. At the first stage, we estimate the conditional distribution F̂(T|X,Z) of the treatment T, given by the IV Z and covariates X. Then, after replacing F(T|X,Z) by F̂(T|X,Z), we solve the optimization problem (7.46).

Under the linear model discussed at Section 7.4.2:

Y = αT + βX + εY,(7.47)

T = τZ + γX + εT,(7.48)

where assuming that E[εY|X,Z] = 0, E[εT|X,Z] = 0.

Under the linear model, we obtain

$\begin{array}{l} {\int{\widehat{h}\left( {T,X} \right)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right) = \int\left. (g\left( {T,X} \right) + E\lbrack e \middle| X\rbrack)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right) \right.}} \\ {\quad = \int\left. (\alpha T + \beta X + E\lbrack e \middle| X\rbrack)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right) \right.} \\ {\quad = \left. \alpha E\lbrack T \middle| X,Z + E\rbrack + {\beta X}. \right.} \\ \end{array}$(7.49)

Substituting Equation (7.48) into Equation (7.49), we obtain

$\begin{array}{l} {\int{\widehat{h}\left( {T,X} \right)dF\left( T \middle| X,Z \right) = \alpha E\left\lbrack \tau Z + \gamma X + \varepsilon_{T} \middle| X,Z \right\rbrack + \beta X}} \\ {\quad = \alpha\left( {\tau Z + \gamma X} \right) + \beta X} \\ \end{array}$(7.50)

Therefore, we first use least square methods to solve regression problem (7.48), which results in the estimators τ̂ and γ̂. Then, we substitute τ̂ and γ̂ into Equation (7.50). Finally, we substitute Equation (7.50) into the objective function in the optimization problem (7.46) and use the least square methods to solve the resulting optimization problem. The solution to the optimization problem replicates the results in Section 7.4.2.

Now we introduce some principles for solving the optimization problem (7.46) with nonlinear models. Let

$L\left( \theta \right) = \sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left( Y_{i} - \int{h\left( {T,X,\theta} \right)\mathit{dF}\left( T|X_{i},Z_{i}{))}^{2} \right.} \right.,$(7.51)

where nonlinear function h(T,X,θ) can be implemented by deep neural networks and θ are parameters in the neural networks. The gradient-based algorithms are often used to solve the optimization problem (7.51). The gradient of L(θ) is

$\left. \frac{\partial L\left( \theta \right)}{\partial\theta} = 2\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left( Y_{i} - \int{h\left( {T,X,\theta} \right)\mathit{dF}\left( T \middle| {X_{i},Z_{i}} \right)} \right.\int\frac{\partial h\left( {T,X,\theta} \right)}{\partial\theta}\mathit{dF}\left( T \middle| {X_{i},Z_{i}} \right) \right).$(7.52)

It can be calculated by Monte Carlo integration. The details are beyond the scope of this chapter. We refer the readers to Mohamed et al. (2019).

The conditional distribution F(T|Xi,Zi) can be generated by CGANs (Section 7.2.3.4) where X and Z are condition variables and T is a target variable. The generated (T,X,Z) will be substituted into the Equation (7.52) to calculate the gradient $\frac{\partial L\left( \theta \right)}{\partial\theta}$. After the solution ĥ(T,X) is calculated, we can estimate the treatment effect as follows.

ITE(X) = ĥ(T1,X) − ĥ(T0,X).(7.53)


7.5 Learning Treatment Effect by Generative Adversarial Networks

7.5.1 Introduction

In this section we propose an alternative to using matching to impute counterfactuals and estimating treatment effects. Here, we introduce the generative adversarial networks (GANs) for estimating counterfactuals and treatment effects (Goodfellow et al. 2014). Unlike the classical non-neural network methods that estimate the average treatment effects from observational data, the GANs estimate the individualized treatment effects (ITEs) (Yoon, Jordon, and Schaar 2018), an important step towards “precision medicine”. A great challenge in estimating the ITE is to remove the bias of confounders on the treatment effect estimation, where confounders affect both treatment assignment and outcome. We present an extension of the GANs to the estimation of ITEs in the presence of latent confounders. The GANs and conditional GANs (CGANs) are powerful tools for imputing missing data. The next section will introduce the CGANs as a general framework for imputing the counterfactuals and estimating the ITEs.

7.5.2 CGANs as a General Framework for Estimation of Individualized Treatment Effects

A challenge in the estimation of ITEs is the unbiased estimation of counterfactuals. Counterfactuals will never be observed and cannot be tested by data because true counterfactuals are unknown. Recently proposed GANs and CGANs started a revolution in deep learning. GANs and CGANs offer a powerful tool for missing data imputation. GANs and CGANs have the potential to accurately produce the missing data distribution given partial observations and some other information in the data. Therefore, we can use GANs and CGANs to produce potential counterfactuals.

GANs and CGANs consist of two parts: the “generative” part that is called the generator and the “adversarial” part that is called the discriminator. Both generator and discriminator are implemented by neural networks. Typically, a K-dimensional noise vector that may be uniformly distributed or normally distributed is input to the generator network that transforms the noise vector to a new desired fake data instance using nonlinear transformation theory. Then the generated new fake data instance is input to the discriminator network to evaluate them for authenticity. The generator is constantly learning to produce better fake data instances while the discriminator constantly obtains both real and fake data and improves the accuracy of evaluation for authenticity.

The Architecture of CGANs for Generating Potential Outcomes

Observed factual and covariates provide basic information for the estimation of counterfactuals. Therefore, we use CGANs as a general framework for ITE estimation. The CGANs for ITE estimation consists of two blocks. The first block is to impute the counterfactuals, which is referred to as to the imputation block. The second ITE block is to estimate the distribution of the treatment effects using the predicted complete dataset produced in the imputation block. The architecture of CGANs is shown in Figure 7.5.

  • Imputation Block

    A counterfactual generator in the imputation block is a nonlinear function of the covariate vector x , treatment vector T, treatment assignment indicator vector W, observed factual outcome Yf and K dimensional random vector zG with uniform distribution zG ∼ U((−1,1)K) where ${\overline{Y}}^{f} = {\overline{Y}}^{\eta}$ . The produced potential outcomes by the generator are denoted by

    $\widetilde{Y} = G{({X,{\overline{Y}}_{f},{T \odot W,\left( {1 - W} \right) \odot z}_{G},\theta_{G}})},$(7.54)

    where output Ỹ represents a sample of G. It can take binary values, categorical values, or continuous values. 1 is a vector of 1, ⊙ denotes element-wise multiplication and θG are the parameters in the generator. The generator is implemented by a neural network. We use Y to denote the complete dataset that is obtained by replacing Ỹη with ${\overline{Y}}^{f}$.

    The distribution of Ỹ depends on the determinant of the Jacobian matrix of the nonlinear transformation function $G\left( {X,{\overline{Y}}^{f},\mathit{T},W,z_{G},\theta_{G}} \right)$ using a transformation theorem (Ross, n.d.). Changing the transformation function can change the distribution of the generated counterfactuals. Let PY|x, t, w, yf(y) be the conditional distribution of the potential outcomes, given X = x, T = t, W = w, Yf = yf. The goal of the generator is to learn the neural network G such that G(x,yf,t,w,zG,θG) ∼ PY|x, t, w, yf(y).

    Unlike the discriminator in the standard CGANs where the discriminator evaluates the input data for their authenticity (real or fake data), the counterfactual discriminator DG that maps pairs (x,y) to vectors in [0,1]k attempts to distinguish the factual component from the counterfactuals. The output of the counterfactual discriminator DG is a vector of probabilities that the component represents the factual outcome. Let DG(x,ỹ,t,w,θd)k represent the probability that the ith component of ỹ is the factual outcome, i.e., k = η, where θd denotes the parameters in the discriminator. The goal of the counterfactual discriminator is to maximize the probability DG(x,ỹ,t,w,θd)k for correctly identifying the factual component η via changing the parameters in the discriminator neural network DG.

  • Loss Function

The imputation block attempts to impute counterfactuals by extending the loss function of binary treatment in (Yoon et al. 2018) to all types of treatments: binary, categorical or continuous treatments, we define loss function V(DG,G,θd) asE(x, t, w, yf) ∼ Pdata(x, t, w, yf)EzG ∼ u(( − 1, 1)K)[WTlogDG(X,Ỹ,T,W) + (1−W)Tlog(1−DG(X,Ỹ,T,W))],

where the log is an element-wise operation.

The goal of imputation block is to maximize the counterfactual discriminator DG and then minimize the counterfactual generator G:

$\min\limits_{G}{\max\limits_{D_{G}}{V\left( {D_{G},G,\theta_{d}} \right)}}.$(7.55)

In other words, we train the counterfactual discriminator DG to maximize the probability of correctly identifying the assigned treatment Tη or Yf(Yη), and then train the counterfactual generator G to minimize the probability of correctly identifying Tη. After the imputation block is performed, the counterfactual generator G produces the complete dataset $\overline{D} = x,y$. Next, we use the imputed complete dataset $\overline{D} = X,y$ to generate a distribution of potential outcomes and to estimate the individualized treatment effects via standard CGANs which is called the ITE block.

CGANs for Estimating ITEs

  • ITE Block

    The standard CGANs consist of three parts: generator, discriminator and loss function which is summarized as follows (Yoon, Jordon, and Schaar 2018); Ge et al. 2019)

  • ITE Generator

    The ITE generator GI is a nonlinear transform function of X and ZI:

    Ŷ = GI(X,ZI,θgI),(7.56)

    where Ŷ is the generated K-dimensional vector of potential outcomes, X is a covariant vector and ZI is a K-dimensional vector of random variables and follow the uniform distribution ZI ∼ u((−1,1)K). The ITE generator attempts to find the transformation Ŷ = GI(X, ZI,θgI) such that Ŷ ∼ PY|X(y).

  • ITE Discriminator

    Following the standard CGANs, we define a discriminator DI as a nonlinear classifier with a pair (X,Y*=Y) or (X,Y*=Ŷ) as input and a scalar that outputs the probability of Y* being from the complete dataset $\overline{D}$.

  • Loss Function

    A loss function for the ITE block is defined as

    VI(DI,GI) = EX ∼ P(x)[EY* ∼ PY|X(y)[logDI(X,Y*)]+EZI ∼ u(( − 1, 1)K)[log(1−DI(X,Y*)]](7.57)

    where DI(X,Y*) is a nonlinear classifier that determines whether Y* is from the complete dataset $\overline{D}$ or from generator GI.

    The goal of the ITE block is to maximize the probability of correctly identifying that Y* is from the complete dataset $\overline{D}$ and to minimize the probability of correct classification. Mathematically, ITE attempts

    $\min\limits_{G_{I}}{\max\limits_{D_{I}}{V_{I}\left( {D_{I},G_{I}} \right)}}.$(7.58)

    The algorithms for numerically solving the optimization problems (7.4) and (7.7) consist of the following steps.

    To solve the optimization problems (7.4) and (7.7), we need to use sampling formulas to approximate the expectations. Therefore, we first discuss the implementation of the imputation block.

  • Imputation Block Optimization

    Assume that n individuals are sampled. Sampling approximation of V(DG,G) is given by

    $\widehat{V}\left( {D_{G},G} \right) \approx \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}{\left\lbrack W \right._{i}^{T}\log{\left( {D_{G}\left( X_{i},T_{i},{\widetilde{Y}}_{i},W_{i},\theta_{d} \right.} \right) + \left( 1 - W_{i})^{T}\log{\left( 1 - D_{G}\left( {X_{i},T_{i},{\widetilde{Y}}_{i},W_{i},\theta_{d}} \right) \right)\rbrack} \right.}},$(7.59)

    where Ỹ = G(X,Yf,T ⊙ W, (1−W) ⊙ ZG,θG).

    To enforce that the estimated factual outcome Ỹiηshould be as close to the observed factual outcome Yif as possible(Yoon, Jordon, and Schaar 2018), we post the following restriction:

    $l\left( G \right) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left( Y_{i}^{f} - {\widetilde{Y}}_{i}^{\eta})^{2} \right..$(7.60)

    The optimization problem (7.4) can be implemented by

    $\min\limits_{D_{G}}{- \widehat{V}\left( {D_{G},G} \right)},$(7.61)

    $\min\limits_{G}{\widehat{V}\left( {D_{G},G} \right) + \lambda l\left( G \right)}.$(7.62)

    Optimization problems (7.10) and (7.11) can be solved by backpropagation (stochastic gradient descent) algorithms. The details for the algorithms are given in supplementary note A.

  • ITE Block Optimization

ITE block intends to estimate the counterfactuals using the observed outcomes and imputed counterfactuals. Its performance metrics are defined as$L\left( G_{I} \right) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left| \left| {Y_{i} - {\widehat{Y}}_{i}} \right| \right|_{2}^{2}.$

Sampling formula for VI(DI,GI) is${\widehat{V}}_{I}\left( {D_{I},G_{I}} \right) = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left\lbrack \log{D_{I}\left( X_{i},Y_{i}^{\ast} \right) + \log\left( \left. 1 - D_{I}\left( {X_{i},Y_{i}^{\ast}} \right) \right) \right)} \right..$

The optimization problem (7.7) for ITE can be reformulated as

$\min\limits_{D_{I}}{- {\widehat{V}}_{I}\left( {D_{I},G_{I}} \right)},$(7.63)

$\min\limits_{G_{I}}{{\widehat{V}}_{I}\left( {D_{I},G_{I}} \right) + \gamma}L\left( G_{I} \right).$(7.64)

Again, stochastic gradient descent methods can be used to solve optimization problems (7.12) and (7.13). Algorithms for their numerical implementation are similar to the algorithms for the imputation block.

CGANs for Estimating ITEs in Survival Analysis

  • Background in Survival Analysis

    An essential issue for survival time analysis is how to deal with censoring time. Let ${\overline{Y}}_{i} = \left\lbrack {\overline{Y}}_{i}^{1},\ldots,{\overline{Y}}_{i}^{K}\rbrack^{T} \right.$ be the vector of potential outcomes (survival time) for the ith subject, where ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}$ is the potential outcome (survival time) if the ith subject had received the kth treatment. We only observe the component of the potential outcome vector ${\overline{Y}}_{i}$ that corresponds to the assigned treatment, which is called the factual outcome and is denoted by ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$. Other unobserved potential outcomes (survival times) are called counterfactual outcomes (survival times), or simply counterfactuals, and denoted by ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}}$. The factual outcome (survival time) ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$ can be expressed as${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f} = \sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W_{i}^{k}{\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}}.$

    Each subject is associated with K potential outcomes (survival times) ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{k}$ , but only one of them can be observed as ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$. Let Xi = [xi1,…,xip]T be a vector of covariates associated with the ith subject, including miRNA, age, sex and other geographic and tumor feature variables. Assume that n subjects are sampled. For the sake of simplicity, the subscript i for indexing the subject is omitted when the context is clear.

    Next, we consider censoring. The reasons for censoring include: 1) loss to follow-up and 2) deaths from other causes. Let Li be an indicator variable for the censored event where li = 1 denotes that the ith subject is not censored and li = 0 denotes that the ith subject is censored. Let Ci be the censoring time for the ith subject. The potential time to event (death or censored) Z = [Z1,…,ZK]Tis defined as

    $Z^{k} = \min{\left\{ {{\overline{Y}}^{k},C} \right\},k = 1,\ldots,K},$(7.65)

    and the factual (observed) time to event is

    $Z^{f} = \sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W^{k}Z^{k}}.$(7.66)

    Define the indicator variable for the kth potential survival time censoring to indicate that it is less than the censoring time as

    $\delta^{k} = I\left( {C \geq {\overline{Y}}^{k}} \right)$(7.67)

    and

    the indicator variable for the all potential survival time censoring as

    $\delta = \sum_{k = 1}^{K}{W^{k}\delta^{k}}.$(7.68)

    The data for the treatment effect estimation with censoring survival time is (Y,W,T,X,δ,Z). Assumption 2 (Z ⫫ Y|(X,T,e) needs to be extended to censoring data.

    STEP 1: The treatment assignment is independent of censored and uncensored survival time given the covariates. Formally, we assume

    $\left( \overline{Y},Z \right)⫫W|X.$(7.69)

    Similar to the assumption for the treatment assignment, we also make the following assumption about the independence of the censoring time from the survival times and covariates:

    STEP 2: $\left( \overline{Y},Z,X \right)⫫C|W.$

  • Loss Function of the CGANs for Estimating ITEs in Survival Analysis

The data in survival analysis consist of two parts: 1) noncensored data Dnc and 2) censored data Dc . In survival analysis, the produced potential outcomes (survival time) by the generator in Equation (7.3) should be changed to

$\widetilde{Y} = G{({X,{\overline{Y}}_{f},{T \odot W,\left( {1 - W} \right) \odot z}_{G},l,\theta_{G}})}.$(7.70)

The loss function for the uncensored data Dnc is given by

$\begin{array}{l} {V\left( {D_{G}, G, D_{nc}} \right) = E_{{({x,t,w,y^{f}})}\sim P_{D_{nc}}{({x,t,w,y^{f}})}}E_{z_{G}\sim u({({- 1,1)^{K}})}}\left\lbrack W \odot \log D_{G}(X,\widetilde{Y},T, W, l = 1) \right\rbrack} \\ {\left. + (1 - W) \odot \log(1 - D_{G}\left( {X,\widetilde{Y},T, W,l = 1} \right)) \right\rbrack.} \\ \end{array}$(7.71)

For the censored data Dc, we should introduce the second loss function V(Dc) to enforce the constraints: $Z^{k} = \min{\left\{ {{\overline{Y}}^{k},C} \right\},\mathit{k} = 1,\ldots,\mathit{K}}$:

$V\left( {D_{G},G,D}_{c} \right) = E_{{({x,t,w,y^{f}})}\sim P_{D_{c}(x,t,w,}y^{f})}\left\lbrack \max\left( 0, \right.C - G{({X,{\overline{Y}}_{f},{T \odot W,\left( {1 - W} \right) \odot z}_{G},l = 0,\theta_{G}})}. \right.$(7.72)

Using the Lagrangian multiplier, we can incorporate the constraints into the loss function (7.20) to obtain the total loss function of CGANs for estimating ITEs in the survival analysis:

V(DG,G,Dnc,Dc) = V(DG,G,Dnc) + λV(DG, G, Dc),(7.73)

where λ is a tuning parameter that controls the trade-off between the censored and noncensored loss function. We often set λ = 1.

To obtain the ITEs in survival analysis, we solve the following optimization problems

$\min\limits_{G}{\max\limits_{D_{G}}{V\left( {D_{G},G,D_{\mathit{nc}},D_{c}} \right)}}.$(7.74)

7.5.3 Wasserstein GANs for Estimation of Individualized Treatment Effects

It is well-known that training GAN is difficult because we often face limitations such as its convergence and model collapse problems. Wasserstein GANs (WGANs) can be used to overcome these limitations (Arjovsky and Bottou 2019). The major difference between WGANs and GANs is loss function. We only need to introduce a loss function for WGANs. The architectures of the neural networks are the same as those in Section 7.2.3. The loss function VW(DG,G,θd) of WGANs for imputation of the counterfactuals is given by

VW(DG,G,θd) = Ex, t, w, yf) ∼ Pdata(x, t, w, yf)EzG ∼ u(( − 1, 1)K)[W⊙DG(X,(X,Ỹ,T,W)− (1−W)⊙DG(X,(X,Ỹ,T,W)],(7.75)

where

Ỹ = G(X,Yf,T ⊙ W, (1−W) ⊙ ZG,θG) and DG is the set of 1-Lipschitz functions. We usually use the neural networks to implement DG, which satisfies the 1-Lipschitz condition.

To impute the counterfactuals, we solve the following optimization problem:

$\min\limits_{G}{\max\limits_{D_{G}}{V\left( {D_{G},G,\theta_{d}} \right)}}.$(7.76)

The loss function of WGANs for the ITE block is

VI(DI,GI) = EX ∼ P(x)[EY* ∼ PY|X(y)[DI(X,Y*)]−EZI ∼ u(( − 1, 1)K)[DI(X,Y*)]],(7.77)

where DI is the set of 1-Lipschitz functions, other quantities are defined as in Section 7.2.3.

7.5.4 MisCGANs for Estimation of Individualized Treatment Effects

The General Process for Incompletely Observed Data

The general process for incompletely observed data can be statistically described by two random vectors (S. C.-X. Li, Jiang, and Marlin 2019, Little and Rubin 2002). Let X ∈ Rn be a vector of the complete data and M ∈ {0, 1}n be a n-dimensional binary mask vector to indicate whether the components in the data vector are missing or not. The statistical model for describing incompletely observed data is given by

X ∼ Pθ(X) and M ∼ Pω(M|X).

To characterize the missing mechanism of the data, the complete dataset X is decomposed into the observed components Xobs of X and the missing components Xmis of X, i.e., X = [Xobs,Xmis]. Therefore, the distribution of the mask vector can be expressed as Pω(M|Xobe,Xmis). Based on the relationships between the complete data X and the mask M, the missing mechanisms can be classified into three scenarios (Little and Rubin 2002, S. C.-X. Li, Jiang, and Marlin 2019):

  1. If the mask is independent of the complete data, i.e, Pω(M|X) = Pω(M) , then missing mechanism is completely at random (MCAR);

  2. If the mask I independent of the missing data, i.e, Pω(M|X) = Pω(M|Xobs), then the missing is missing at random(MAR);

  3. If the missing depends on the complete dataset X, then there are no missing at random (NMAR).

In this chapter, we consider only MCAR. Then, the joint probability P(x,m) can be factorized into P(X,M) = P(X)P(M).

MisGAN for Counterfactual Imputation

In this section, we study how the MisGAN (S. C.-X. Li, Jiang, and Marlin 2019) is applied to the ITE estimation problem. The missing data in the ITE estimation problem is due to the counterfactuals. The complete data vector is ${\overline{Y}}_{i}$ and the partially observed data is ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{f}$. The treatment assignment vector Wi plays a mask role. In other words, if Wik = 1 then the potential outcome (factual) is observed and if Wik = 0 then a vector of counterfactuals ${\overline{Y}}_{i}^{\mathit{cf}}$ is unobserved. Similar to the paper (S. C.-X. Li, Jiang, and Marlin 2019), an incomplete dataset is denoted $D = \left( {{\overline{Y}}_{i},W_{i}} \right),i = 1,\ldots,n$.

A masking operator that fills counterfactuals with a constant value τ is defined as

$f_{\tau}\left( {\overline{Y},W} \right) = \overline{Y} \odot W + \tau\left( {1 - W} \right),$(7.78)

where ⊙ represents elements-wise multiplication.

We first generate the distribution of the mask (treatment assignment) vector W. The architecture of the MisGAN for generating a distribution of the mask is shown in Figure 7.6. We define GY as

${\overline{Y} = G}_{Y}\left( {X,{\overline{Y}}^{f},T,Z_{G}} \right).$(7.79)

The mask Ŵ (the treatment assignment) is generated by

Ŵ = GW(ε).(7.80)

Both GY and GW are implemented by neural networks.

Unlike the standard CGANs where there is only one generator-discriminator pair, the MisGANs have two generator-discriminator pairs (GW,DW) and (GY,DY) for the mask (treatment assignment indicator) and potential outcomes, respectively. Since we assume that the treatment assignment and potential outcomes are independent, we have two independent noises ZG and ε with distributions PZ and Pε. The loss functions, one for the masks and the other for the data (potential outcomes) are defined as follows.

VW(DW,GW) = E(Y, W) ∼ Pdata[DW(W)] − Eε ∼ Pε[DW(GW(ε))],(7.81)

$V_{Y}\left( {D_{Y},G_{Y},G_{W}} \right) = E_{{({Y,W})}\sim P_{\mathit{data}}}\left\lbrack D_{Y}{({f_{\tau}\left( {\overline{Y},W} \right)})} - E_{Z\sim P_{Z},\varepsilon\sim P_{\varepsilon}}\left\lbrack D_{Y}\left( f_{\tau}\left( {G_{Y}\left( {X,{\overline{Y}}^{f},T,Z_{G}} \right),G_{W}\left( \varepsilon \right)} \right) \right\rbrack,\quad \right. \right.$(7.82)

where DW and DY are both 1-Lipschitz functions.

The loss functions in (7.30) and (7.31) follow W-GANs. To find DW, DY and GY, we solve the following optimization problems:

minGYmaxDY ∈ FYVY(DY,GY,GW),(7.83)

minGWmaxDW ∈ FWVW(DW,GW) + λVY(DY,GY,GW),(7.84)

where FY, FW are the families of 1-Lipschitz functions, λ is often set to λ = 0.2.

Finally, we investigate the imputation of the counterfactuals. We will impute counterfactuals according to the conditional distribution $P\left( {\overline{Y}}^{\mathit{cf}} \middle| {\overline{Y}}^{f} \right)$ by adding an imputer Gi and discriminator Di to Figure 7.5. The formed new architecture of the MisGANs is shown in Figure 7.7. The imputer Gi is a nonlinear function of the covariates X, the potential outcomes (factual, the estimated counterfactuals in Figure 7.5), the treatment vector, the mask (treatment assignment vector) and noise and defined as

$G_{i}\left( {X,\overline{Y},T,W,Z_{\omega}} \right) = \overline{Y} \odot W + G_{\mathit{iy}}\left( {X,{\overline{Y}}^{f},T \odot W,\left( {1 - W} \right) \odot Z_{\omega}} \right) \odot \left( {1 - W} \right).$(7.85)

The loss function for imputing the counterfactuals is defined as

$V_{i}\left( {D_{i},G_{i},G_{Y}} \right) = E_{Z_{G}\sim P_{Z}}\left\lbrack {D_{i}\left( {G_{Y}\left( {X,{\overline{Y}}^{f},T,Z_{G}} \right)} \right)} \right\rbrack - E_{{({Y,W})}\sim P_{\mathit{data}},,Z_{\omega}\sim P_{\omega}}\left\lbrack {D_{i}\left( G \right.}_{i}\left( {X,\overline{Y},T,W,Z_{\omega}} \right) \right)\rbrack.$(7.86)

To estimate ITEs, we jointly solve the following optimization problems:

minGimaxDi ∈ FiVi(Di,Gi,GY),(7.87)

minGYmaxDY ∈ FYVY(DY,GY,GW) + βVi(Di,Gi,GY),(7.88)

minGWmaxDW ∈ FWVW(DW,GW) + λVY(DY,GY,GW).(7.89)

7.5.5 Optimal Treatment Selection

Sparse Techniques for Biomarker Identification

The LASSO (least absolute shrinkage and selection operator) that performs both variable selection and regularization to enhance the prediction accuracy and interpretability of the results can be used to select biomarkers for optimal treatment election (Diamond and Sekhon 2013, Ge et al. 2020) Let yti and X(i) denote the estimated treatment effect and feature vector of the ith individual, respectively. Let$Y_{T} = \begin{bmatrix} y_{t}^{(1)} \\ \vdots \\ y_{t}^{(n)} \\ \end{bmatrix},\mathit{X} = \begin{bmatrix} x_{1}^{(1)} & \cdots & x_{q}^{(1)} \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ x_{1}^{(n)} & \cdots & x_{q}^{(n)} \\ \end{bmatrix},\beta = \begin{bmatrix} \beta_{1} \\ \vdots \\ \beta_{q} \\ \end{bmatrix}.$

The outputs of the neural networks are, in general, continuous functions even if the potential outcomes are binary. Therefore, we assume that the treatment effects are continuous regardless of the data types of the potential outcomes.

The LASSO estimators for the discovery of biomarkers that predict treatment effects are${\widehat{\beta}}_{\lambda} = \underset{\beta}{argmin}{{||}Y_{T} - X\beta||_{2}^{2}} + \lambda\sum_{j = 1}^{q}{|\beta_{j}|}.$

The correspondence Xj of nonzero elements βj ≠ 0 represents the biomarkers for treatment effect prediction.

Biomarker Identification for Optimal Treatment Selection

Consider K treatments. Let Ŷi = [Ŷi(T1i),…,Ŷi(TKi)]T be the K-dimensional vector of estimated potential outcomes for the ith individual and zi = argmax1, .., KŶi(T1i), …, Ŷi(TKi) be the index of the optimal potential outcomes of the ith individual. To select biomarkers for optimal treatment selection, we define the following LASSO:${||}{\widehat{Y}}^{i}\left( T_{z_{i}}^{i} \right) - \sum_{j = 1}^{q}{x_{j}^{(i)}\beta_{j}||^{2} + \lambda\sum_{j = 1}^{q}{|\beta_{j}|}},i = 1,\ldots,n.$

Solving the above LASSO problem, we obtain a set of non-zero coefficients that are denoted as β̂l ≠ 0, l = 1, …, L. The covariates that correspond to the non-zero coefficients of the LASSO solution are chosen as biomarkers for optimal treatment selection.


7.6 Deconfounder in Estimation of Treatment Effects

7.6.1 Introduction

Dealing with confounders, which by definition affect both the treatment and outcomes, is a central problem in the estimation of treatment effects (T. VanderWeele 2015, Zhang et al. 2019, Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018) Consider an example, we assume that smoking is a treatment (T), mortality is the outcome (Y) and gender is the confounder (X) (Figure 7.8(a)). Assume that gender may affect smoking and mortality. We observe that males are more likely to smoke than females and are also more likely to die young due to other risk behaviors. A difference in mortality between smokers and non-smokers may be due to smoking, gender, or may be due to both. In this case, gender is a confounder. This example demonstrates that estimating treatment effect in the presence of confounders will be biased.

If the confounders can be measured, we can take the measured confounders to adjust for other confounders. However, many confounders cannot be measured or are simply unknown. For example, a social-economic status that affects both the treatment the patients receive and the health conditions of the patients is an unmeasured confounder. The unadjusted confounders will seriously influence the estimation of the treatment effects. To estimate the treatment effects with little to no bias in the presence of unobserved confounders poses a great challenge. The methods for de-confounding include generative implicating models (Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018), data reduction methods (Zhang et al. 2019), confounder selection (T. VanderWeele 2015a), reinforcement learning (C. Lu, Schölkopf, and Hernández-Lobato 2018), and instrumental variable methods (Usaid Awan et al. 2019). In this section, we focus on generative adversarial learning (Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018).

7.6.2 Causal Models with Latent Confounders

We introduce causal models with unobserved confounders (Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018). We assume that a latent confounder Z affects both the treatment variable T (binary treatment) and outcome variable Y. Suppose that there exists a proxy variable X that approximates the latent variable Z (Figure 7.8b). The data which we collect are denoted by D = (Xi,Ti,Yi), i = 1, …, n. The outcome Y is a function of the treatment T, the feature vector (covariates) X, the confounder Z, and denoted by

Y = G(X,T,Z).(7.90)

The joint distribution of the variables Y, Z, X, T is denoted as P(Y,Z,X,T). Since confounder Z is not observed, to determine Z, we use two approximations to factorize the joint distribution P(Y,Z,X,T) as follows:

P(Y,Z,X,T) ≈ qE(Y,Z,X,T) = Pdata(Y,X,T)qE(Z|Y,X,T),(7.91)

P(Y,Z,X,T) ≈ qP(Y,Z,X,T) = Pdata(X,T)qI(Z|X,T)qP(Y|Z,X.T).(7.92)

Equation (7.39) shows that the joint distribution P(Y,Z,X,T) can be generated by an encoder-decoder where distribution qE(Z|Y,X,T) is the distribution of the whole latent space mapped by all the original dataset (Y,X,T). Therefore, the generated latent space includes both the confounder and non-confounder parts. Equation (7.40) implies that the joint distribution P(Y,Z,X,T) can be approximately generated by prediction principle where the generated latent space includes only the confounders. To determine the confounders, we can make the two distributions qE(Y,Z,X,T) and qP(Y,Z,X,T) equal. Adversarial learning networks can be used to implement it (Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018, Dumoulin et al. 2019, Chen et al. 2019).

The ITE for an individual is defined as

ITE(X) = E[Y|X,T=1] − E[Y|X,T=0].(7.93)

Since the confounder Z affect both Y and T, using the formula for the total probability, we can express the conditional probability P(Y|X,T) as

$\begin{array}{l} {P\left( Y \middle| X, T \right) = \int{P\left( Y,Z \middle| X,T \right)dz}} \\ {\quad = \int{P\left( Z \middle| X,T \right)P\left( Y \middle| Z,X,T \right)dz}.} \\ \end{array}$(7.94)

Since we assume that Z→T, we then obtain

P(Z|X,T) = P(Z|X).(7.95)

Substituting Equation (7.43) into Equation (7.42), we obtain

P(Y|X,T) = ∫P(Z|X)P(Y|Z,X,T)dz.(7.96)

Using Equation (7.44), we can calculate ITE(X).

7.6.3 Adversarial Learning Confounders

The basic idea of the adversarial learning confounders is to match the joint distribution qE(Y,Z,X,T) with the joint distribution qP(Y,Z,X,T) via bidirectional model and adversarial learning (Lee, Mastronarde, and van der Schaar 2018, Chen et al. 2019, Dumoulin et al. 2019). The pair-domain joint distributions qE(Y,Z,X,T) and qP(Y,Z,X,T) will be produced by two generative models that are similar to autoencoders. The generative model that produces the joint distribution qE(Y,Z,X,T) is called a reconstruction network, and the generative model that produces the joint distribution qP(Y,Z,X,T) is called a prediction network. Adversarial learning inference is used to match the pair-domain joint distribution (Figure 7.9).

A reconstruction network can produce the joint distribution qE(Y,Z,X,T) that is factorized into two components: Pdata(Y,X,T) and qE(Z|Y,X,T). The sampled data (Y,X,T) from the distribution Pdata(Y,X,T) is input to the encoders to generate the latent variables Z that follows a distribution qE(Z|Y,X,T). The latent variables Z̃ is denoted by Z̃ = GE(Y,X,T,εE,θE). To ensure that the latent variables keep the original data information, we introduce the decoder GR(Z̃) to reconstruct input of the encoder GE(Y,X,T,εE,θE).

A prediction network is developed to implement the factorization of the joint distribution qP(Y,Z,X,T) in Equation (7.40). To map the data (X,T) to the latent space, another encoder (inference network) is introduced. The output Z of the inference network is a nonlinear function of X, T and εI with the transformed distribution qI(Z|X,T) and is denoted by Z = GI(X,T,εI,θI). The joint distribution qP(Y|Z,X.T) is generated by the prediction decoder GP. The prediction decoder inputs the data (X,T) sampled from the real data distribution Pdata(X,T) and the latent variables Z ∼ qI(Z|X,T) and predicts the outcome variable Ỹ ∼ qP(Y|Z,X.T), denoted by Ỹ = GP(Z,X,T,θP). Therefore, the joint distribution qP(Y,Z,X,T) in the prediction network is equal toqP(Y,Z,X,T) = Pdata(X,T)qI(Z|X,T)qP(Y|Z,X.T).

7.6.4 Loss Function and Optimization for Estimating ITEs in the Presence of Confounders

To match joint distributions, similar to GANs, we introduce the discriminator and a loss function, which play an adversarial game between the two distributions. When the game reaches equilibrium, the two distributions qE(Y,Z,X,T) and qP(Y,Z,X,T) are matched. Let D(Z,X,T,Y) be the probability that the tuple (Z,X,T,Y) is drawn from the joint distribution qE(Y,Z,X,T) and 1 − D(Z,X,T,Y) be the probability that the tuple (Z,X,T,Y) is drawn from the joint distribution qP(Y,Z,X,T). The discriminator attempts to distinguish two tuples $\left( {\widetilde{Z,}X,T,Y} \right)$ and (Z,X,T,Ỹ) which are from qE(Y,Z,X,T) and qP(Y,Z,X,T), respectively. The loss function is defined as

V(θD,θE,θI,θP) = EqE(Y, Z, X, T)[logD(Z̃,X,T,Y,θD)] + EqP(Z, X, T, Ỹ)[log(1−D(Z,X,T,Ỹ,θD))].(7.97)

To match two joint distributions, we solve the following optimization problem:

min(θE, θI, θP)maxθDV(θD,θE,θI,θP).(7.98)

To ensure that the encoded latent variables Z contain the original data information, we define the following loss function for the reconstruction decoder:

$\left. V_{R}\left( {X,T,Y,\overline{X},\overline{T},\overline{Y}} \right) = \middle| \middle| X - \overline{X} \middle| |^{2} - T^{T}\log\overline{T} - {(1 - T)}^{T}\log\left( {1 - \overline{T}} \right) - Y^{T}\log\overline{Y}\quad - {(1 - Y)}^{T}\log\left( {1 - \overline{Y}} \right). \right.$(7.99)


7.7 Targeted Maximum Likelihood Estimation

An alternative method to estimate the ATE is the targeted maximum likelihood estimation (TMLE). TMLE is doubly robust than other methods, based on maximum likelihood estimation and has a “targeting” step that optimizes the bias-variance tradeoff for the target parameter (Laan and Rubin 2006). Although TMLE is not widely used in causal inference studies, it has desirable statistical properties and has a lot of potential in EHR causal inference.

Consider a binary treatment and let Y1 denote the outcomes with exposed factors, and Y0 denote outcomes without exposed factors. To remove confounders, G-computation and propensity score methods use conditional probability to address confounding. G-computation relies on the conditional expectation of outcome given exposure and covariates, E[Y|A,X]. Propensity score methods estimate the conditional expectation of the exposure given the observed confounders X, P(A=1|X). TMLE includes both of them and will provide unbiased estimation if either E[Y|A,X] or P(A=1|X) is consistently estimated (doubly robust). If not, the final ATE can still be unbiased if the exposure mechanism can be consistently estimated. TMLE is an asymptotically efficient substitution estimator when both the outcome and exposure mechanisms are consistently estimated, and it is more robust to outliers and sparsity. Finally, many kinds of algorithms and machine learning methods can be incorporated in TMLE, and these methods can help with minimizing bias in comparison with the use of misspecified regressions.

Figure 7.10 shows how the TMLE works. First, calculate the conditional expectation of the outcome given the exposure and covariates, E[Y|A,X]. This first estimation is used to represent the estimation of the outcome on both exposure levels. Then, the next step is the “targeting” step, which involves the estimation of exposure mechanism, P(A=1|X). This estimation is used to improve the estimation before the predefined working regression model. Finally, use the improved expectation value E[Y|A,X] to generate updated pairs of potential outcomes. The new estimation of ATE value will the average difference between the exposure pairs across individuals.

In summary, the TMLE is a doubly robust estimator of the ATE, and the doubly robust prosperity ensures the unbiasedness of the estimator. TMLE unifies maximum likelihood estimation and estimating function based estimation and combines them to get an improved result. Finally, TMLE can be implemented using machine learning algorithms, which can make this method be used on complex datasets like EHR data.

TMLE method can be used with the “tmle” package (Gruber and van der Laan 2012) in R (R Foundation for Statistical Computing, Vienna, Austria).


7.8 Supplementary Note A

7.8.1 Wasserstein GAN

A1 Different Distances

There are two approaches to learning generative models. One approach is to directly learn the probability density function pθ via maximum likelihood function. The second approach is to learn a function that transforms the existing distribution z into the density function pθ via pθ = Gθ(z). To elaborate, we first study the maximum likelihood estimator.

A1.1 Maximum Likelihood Estimation

Assume that real data samples {x1, …, xn} with density function pr(x) are given. We define a parametric family of density pθ(x). The maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) is to maximize the likelihood of the data:

$\mathit{\max}_{\theta}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left. \log{p_{\theta}\left( x_{i} \right.} \right),$(A1)

which implies

$\lim\limits_{n\rightarrow\infty}{\max\limits_{\theta}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\left. \log{p_{\theta}\left( x_{i} \right.} \right) = \max\limits_{\theta}\int{p_{r}\left( x \right)\log{p_{\theta}\left( x \right)\mathit{dx}}}}}.$(A2)

Recall that the KL distance between two distributions pr(x) and pθ(x) is defined as

$\mathit{KL}\left( p_{r}|\left( p_{\theta} \right) = \int{p_{r}\left( x \right)\log\frac{p_{r}\left( x \right)}{p_{\theta}\left( x \right)}}\mathit{dx}. \right.$(A2)

Therefore, Equation (A2) can be transformed to the following optimization problem:

$\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\max_{\theta}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}{\log p_{\theta}(x_{i}) = \max\limits_{\theta}\int{p_{r}(x)\log p_{\theta}\left( x \right)dx}} = - \min\limits_{\theta}\int{p_{r}\left( x \right)\log p_{\theta}\left( x \right)dx}\quad = \min\limits_{\theta}\int{p_{r}\left( x \right)\log p_{r}\left( x \right)dx - \int{p_{r}\left( x \right)\log p_{\theta}\left( x \right)dx}}\quad = \min\limits_{\theta}\int{p_{r}(x)\log\frac{p_{r}(x)}{p_{\theta}(x)}dx}\quad = \underset{\theta}{\left. \min KL(p_{r} \middle| \middle| p_{\theta}) \right.}.$(A3)

Equation (A3) shows that the MLE is equivalent to the minimization of KL distance. The major limitation of the MLE of the generative models is that when two distributions pθ and pr are not completely overlapped, for example, pθ(x0) = 0 where pr(x0) > 0, then KL distance goes to  + ∞. The general solution is to develop a nonlinear function map gθ : Z→χ that transforms a random variable Z with a known distribution p(z) (typically, uniform or normal distribution) to a certain distribution pθ. By varying θ, we can make the distribution pθ close to the real data distribution pr. To measure the degree of closeness, we need to develop various distance measures between two distributions pθ and pr.

A1.2 Total Variation (TV) Distance

Assume that pr and pθ are two probability distributions defined on the space Ω. Then, total variation distance between two distributions is defined as

$\delta\left( {P_{r},P_{\theta}} \right) = \sup\limits_{A \in \digamma}{|p_{r}\left( A \right) - p_{\theta}\left( A \right)|},$(A4)

where ϝ ∈ Ω is a subset of Ω.

Example 1.1

Consider probability distributions defined over probability space R2. Let Z ∼ U[0,1] be the uniform distribution on the unit interval. Let p0 be the distribution defined on (0,Z) and pθ be the distribution defined on (θ,Z), where θ is a single real parameter. DefineA = {(0,z), z ∈ [0,1]}.

Then, p0(A) = z and pθ(A) = 0 when θ ≠ 0. Thus, when θ ≠ 0, we have

|pr(A) − pθ(A)| = z, which impliessupA ∈ ϝ|pr(A) − pθ(A)| = 1.

When, θ = 0, we obtain p0(A) = pθ(A). Therefore,$\delta\left( {p_{0},p_{\theta}} \right) = \left\{ \begin{matrix} 1 & {\theta \neq 0} \\ 0 & {\theta = 0} \\ \end{matrix} \right..$

A1.3 The Kullback-Leibler (KL) Divergence

The Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between two distribution is defined as

$\mathit{KL}\left( p_{r}|\left( p_{\theta} \right) = \int p_{r}\log\frac{p_{r}}{p_{\theta}}\mathit{dx}. \right.$(A5)

Example 1.1 (continued)

Since pθ(A) = 0, ∀θ ≠ 0, therefore, we obtain$\mathit{KL}\left( p_{r}|\left( p_{\theta} \right) = \left\{ \begin{matrix} {+ \infty} & {\theta \neq 0} \\ 0 & {\theta = 0} \\ \end{matrix} \right.. \right.$

A1.4 The Jenson-Shannon (JS) Divergence

Let $p_{m} = \frac{p_{r} + p_{\theta}}{2}$. The Jenson-Shannon (JS) divergence is defined as

JS(pr|(pθ)=KL(pr|(pm)+KL(pθ||pm).(A6)

Example 1.1 (continued)

When θ ≠ 0 where p0 ≠ 0, which implies pθ = 0 . Thus, we have $p_{m} = \frac{p_{r}}{2}$. Similarly, When θ ≠ 0 where pθ≠0, which implies pr = 0. Thus, we have $p_{m} = \frac{p_{\theta}}{2}$. Therefore, we obtain$\mathit{JS}\left( {p_{0},p_{\theta}} \right) = \left\{ \begin{matrix} {\log 2} & {\theta \neq 0} \\ 0 & {\theta = 0} \\ \end{matrix} \right..$

A1.5 Earth Mover (EM) or Wasserstein Distance

When we consider discrete probability distributions, the Wasserstein distance is also illustratively referred to as the earth mover’s distance (EMD). Consider two discrete distributions pr and pθ, each has possible l states x or y. The distribution pr(x) is defined as the probability mass which each point has, and the distribution pθ(y) can be similarly defined. Our goal is to move mass around to change the distribution pr(x)into pθ(y). The joint distribution r(x,y) can be viewed as a transportation plan that moves r(x,y) mass from x to y. The distance from x to y is denoted as ||x−y||. The cost moving r(x,y) mass from x to y is equal to r(x,y)||x−y||. The moving plan r(x,y) should satisfy the following constraints:pr(x) = ∑yr(x,y)  and  pθ(y) = ∑xr(x,y).

Let Π(pr,pθ) be the set of all possible joint distributions with marginal distributions pr(x) and pθ(y). Then, the EMD is then defined as the minimum cost required to move r(x,y) mass from x to y:

W(pr,pθ) = inf r ∈ Π(pr, pθ)∑x∑yr(x,y)||x−y|| = inf r ∈ Π(pr, pθ)E(x, y) ∼ r[||x−y||].(A7)

If the variables are continuous, Equation (A7) can be extended to

W(pr,pθ) = inf r ∈ Π(pr, pθ)∫x∫yr(x,y)||x−y||dxdy.(A8)

Example 1.1 (continued)

Consider two distributions p0(z) and pθ(z) where Z ∼ U(0,1). Let x = [0,z] and y = [θ,z]. It is clear that ||x−y|| = |θ| if we move along the line parallel to the x axis.

A2 Wasserstein GAN

Using the Kantorovich-Rubinstein Duality theory, we can show (Rachev 1990)

W(pr,pθ) = sup||f||L ≤ 1Ex ∼ prf(x) − Ex ∼ pθf(x),(A9)

where f is 1- Lipschitz function.

The optimization problem (A9) is a constrained optimization. Solving the optimization problem under Lipschitz constraints with constant 1 is intractable. To alleviate the problem, we release the Lipschitz constraints with constant 1 to Lipschitz constraints with constant K, i.e.,∥f(xi)−f(xj)∥≤K∥xi−xj∥.

Let $g\left( x \right) = \frac{f\left( x \right)}{K}$. Then, we have ∥g(xi)−g(xj)∥≤∥xi−xj∥, i.e, g(x) is a Lipschitz function with constant 1.

The supremum over K-Lipschitz constraints is still hard to implement, but it allows for easier approximation. Consider a parametrized function family {fw}w ∈ W where W is the set of all possible weights. These functions are also K-Lipschitz functions. Then, we havesup||f||L ≤ KEx ∼ prf(x) − Ex ∼ pθf(x) = Ksup ||g||L ≤ 1Ex ∼ prg(x) − Ex ∼ pθg(x) = KW(pr,pθ),which impliesmaxw ∈ WEx ∼ prfw(x) − Ex ∼ pθfw(gθ(z))≤sup||f||L ≤ KEx ∼ prf(x) − Ex ∼ pθf(x) = KW(pr,pθ).

Therefore, the optimization problem (A9) can be approximated by

maxw ∈ WEx ∼ prfw(x) − Ex ∼ pθfw(x).(A10)

Let

x = gθ(z).(A11)

Substituting Equation (A11) into Equation (A10), we obtain

W(pr,pθ) ≥ maxw ∈ WEx ∼ prfw(x) − Ez ∼ p(z)fw(gθ(z)).(A12)

Both functions fw(x) and gθ(z) can be modeled by neural networks and parameters w and θ are the weights in the neural networks. Our goal is to find the generator gθ(z), which makes the Wasserstein distance between the distribution pr of the real data and distribution pθ of the generator as small as possible, i.e.,

minθW(pr,pθ).(A13)

The Wasserstein distance is calculated by its lower boundary in Equation (A12). In summary, to find the generator gθ(z), we solve the following optimization problem:

minθ maxw ∈ WEx ∼ prfw(x) − Ez ∼ p(z)fw(gθ(z)).(A14)

Numerically, the optimization problem (A13) can be solved by Algorithm 2 (Arjovsky and Bottou 2019).

A3 Algorithm (WGAN)

Input: α, the learning rate; c, the clipping parameter; m, the batch size; nd, the number of iterations of the discriminator neural network update per generator iteration. w0, initial weight parameters of the network for function fw; θ0, initial generator’s parameters.

While θ has not converged do

for t = 0, …, nd do

Sample {x(i)}i = 1m ∼ pr a batch from the real data.

Sample {z(i)}i = 1m ∼ p(z), a batch from the noises.

$\left. g_{w}\leftarrow\nabla_{w}\left\lbrack {\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i = 1}^{m}{f_{w}\left( x^{(i)} \right) - \frac{1}{m}}\sum_{i = 1}^{m}{f_{w}\left( g_{\theta}\left( z^{(i)} \right) \right)}} \right\rbrack \right.$

w←w+αRMSProp(w,gw)

w←clip(w,−c,c)

end for

Sample {z(i)}i = 1m ∼ p(z), a batch from the noises.

$\left. g_{\theta}\leftarrow - \nabla_{\theta}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i = 1}^{m}{f_{w}\left( g_{\theta}\left( z^{(i)} \right) \right)} \right.$

θ←θ−αRMSPtop(θ,gθ)

end while.
