8

EHR Data Exploration, Analysis and Predictions: Statistical Models and Methods
               

Gen Frances Stacia M. Vahed ZhuBritoDeSantisMaroufy


8.1 Introduction

8.1.1 Statistical Challenges for EHR Data

Unlike traditional datasets attained from experimental studies and clinical trials, EHR datasets are big, messy, sparse, ultrahigh dimensional, and with high missing rates. These issues are often challenging to deal with during statistical data analysis and predictions. However, if the dataset is cleaned and processed, standard statistical methods may still be appropriate and possible to implement. In addition, because of ultrahigh dimensionality and potentially large sample sizes of EHR datasets, complicated and sophisticated statistical approaches are almost computationally impossible to implement. Therefore, in this chapter, we will focus on standard and computationally feasible statistical models for EHR data analysis such as general linear models, survival models, and linear mixed effect models. We hope to convince readers that these models, partnered with novel and efficient variable selection and dimensionality reduction methods, can effectively and precisely analyze EHR data.

8.1.2 Overview of Existing Methods

After data are extracted, cleaned, and processed, data exploration and visualization, by running exploratory data analysis (EDA), is suggested in selecting the most suitable statistical model for inference or prediction. In this section, we briefly introduce and review the common techniques and methods used to analyze EHR data.

Several statistical models have been used in EHR data analysis, but generalized linear models (GLM) have been proven to be more efficient and effective as they were also the most common algorithms to develop prediction models (Goldstein et al. 2017). GLMs are the foundation for many other advanced models and researchers have developed systemic theories and software toolkits making application GLMs fast and straightforward. There are many applications of GLMs to EHR data in recent literature. For example, by developing a novel hypothesis test of the high dimensional linear model, Cai, Cai, and Guo (2019) attempted to predict individualized treatment effects based on a given patient’s profile. Wu et al. (2018) implemented logistic regression and other machine learning methods to explore the risk factors associated with major depression disorder (MDD) recurrence. Backenroth et al. (2017) used logistic regression to determine the medications that are associated with hypertension.

On the other hand, although GLMs are commonly used to model EHR data, they are insufficient and often lacking when they encounter time-to-event questions. An example of these types of analyses can be predicting time to in-hospital death for patients with the subarachnoid hemorrhage diagnosis, using longitudinal clinical records such as lab results and clinical events measurements. Survival regression models are often better-suited choices to model these types of datasets. (see Wu et al. 2019, Vinzamuri and Reddy 2013, Ranganath et al. 2015, Hagar et al. 2014). Of course, several important components of survival analysis need to be taken into account, such as the time to an event, study origin, and type of censoring. Furthermore, standard survival models cannot be directly applied to analyze EHR data due to the following limitations (Ranganath et al. 2016, Vinzamuri and Reddy 2013, Hagar et al. 2014):

  • Missingness of the covariates

  • Difficulty in determining study origin

  • Relationship between the covariates and the time to event is assumed to be linear

  • Difficulty in solving structured sparsity in high dimensional EHR data

  • Complexity of censoring

To overcome these limitations, several studies developed new survival models. For example, both Dempsey and McCullagh (2018) and Ranganath et al. (2016) have considered aligning the patients from the event of interest backward, rather than measuring time forward from an artificial starting time. Ranganath et al. (2016) also used latent process-deep exponential families to handle the missing data. Vinzamuri and Reddy (2013) proposed a new regularization linear model to solve structured sparsity in high dimensional EHR data.

Another characteristic of EHR data is its diversity of data formats. It contains both cross-sectional and longitudinal data. For traditional predictive modeling, ignoring this temporal information would result in biased models (Singh et al. 2015). Thus, it is essential to take advantage of existing statistical approaches capable of handling longitudinal data to model the temporal pattern of EHR data. In statistics, Analysis of Variance (ANOVA), Generalized Estimating Equations (GEE), and the mixed-effect models are often used to handle this problem (H. Goldstein et al. 1995). However, mixed effect models usually outperform ANOVA or GEE when handling complex missing data, irregularly timed data, and modeling the correlation of repeated measures (Garcia and Marder 2017). Additionally, mixed-effect models are the most frequently used technique in medical research in dealing with longitudinal data. Therefore, mixed-effect models will be our focus for addressing longitudinal data. Nevertheless, literature regarding the application of mixed-effect models to EHR data is sparse. To alleviate the high computation cost, a simulation study (Rhodes et al. 2018) provides different techniques to improve the efficiency of running mixed effect models on EHR data. Joint models of time-to-event and longitudinal data have also been applied to EHR data (Gasparini et al. 2020, Paige et al. 2018, Maziarz et al. 2017).

One question remains in dealing with longitudinal EHR data: how should we use the timestamps (event and record times)? Hripcsak, Albers, and Perotte (2011) illustrated that converting the clock time into sequence time is a good way to improve prediction performance. To characterize and take advantage of the time variable in EHR, many pre-processing and modeling approaches have been proposed, including time series construction, temporal parameterization, intrasubject normalization, differentiation of the temporal variables, as well as the introduction of explanatory variables, and regression models (Levine, Albers, and Hripcsak 2018). In this chapter, we will mainly focus on the regression models. In literature, many time series methods have been applied to clinical temporal data. Levine, Albers, and Hripcsak (2016; 2018) studied how lagged linear regression can be used to detect the physiological effects of drugs from lab tests. Cheng et al. 2020, Pimentel et al. (2013) used Gaussian process models to reflect the temporal pattern of some clinical events or lab measurements. In the next section, lagged regression models and the Gaussian process will be reviewed.

Curse of dimensionality is another big challenge in using EHR data. Datasets including diagnoses, clinical events, labs, and medications easily pass thousands of variables. This is especially a concern with regard to predictive modeling and causal inference analysis. One strategy used to overcome the computation cost caused by high dimensionality involves determining which significant factors are associated with the outcome of interest (response). However, traditional variable selection methods do not work well for ultrahigh dimensional problems. Fan and Lv (2007) proposed a two-scale variable selection framework, Sure Independence Screening (SIS), which consists of large-scale screening followed by moderate-scale common variable selection methods. Here, common variable selection methods refer to regression models with different types of penalties on the coefficients. Least Absolute Shrinkage and Operation Selector (LASSO) (Tibshirani 1996) and Ridge regression (Hoerl and Kennard 1970) are the most popular penalized regression models used in the EHR prediction analysis to handle “big data” (Zhang and Hong 2017; Wu et al. 2017; Stein et al. 2019). Deciding how to eliminate the influence of confounding variables becomes a critical issue for causal inference in EHR. For example, to evaluate the treatment effects, Shortreed and Ertefaie (2017) proposed to combine variable selection methods with propensity score matching method to solve the high dimensionality issue.

Besides high dimensionality, EHR datasets often have large sample sizes as well. This then requires extra computation capacity and time to fit the models. Sophisticated statistical models such as joint molding, non-conjugate Bayesian approaches, and statistical models requiring numerical likelihood maximization are often too complicated and numerically slow. Moreover, most of these models are numerically impossible to implement on the big clinical data queried for EHR databases (Chen and Xie 2014). Divide and conquer approaches may provide a solution to apply these models to big EHR datasets. In fact, many novel methodologies have been proposed to deal with the scale problem. These include “divide and conquer” techniques and subsampling approaches (Rhodes et al. 2018). However, limited literature exists for handling the EHR data size problem, even though weighted regression and meta-analysis techniques were recently proposed and applied to EHR data (Rhodes et al. 2018).

Moreover, once the model fitting process is complete, an evaluation of whether the results are influenced by the uncertainty of the data or the calibration parameters should be considered. Thus, sensitivity analysis and model validation are necessary to examine the fitted model. In this chapter, we discuss some common techniques including the area under the curve (AUC) of the receiver operator characteristic curve (ROC) to give the readers a better understanding of the way they are used in analyzing EHR data.


8.2 Data Exploration and Visualization

Exploratory data analysis (EDA) is a preliminary analysis of the data that often fosters our understanding of the data and complements the use of hypothesis tests and model building in subsequent analysis. In general, it emphasizes the graphic representation of data and some simple tests can be utilized to uncover the pattern hidden in the data. EDA can trace back to the work of Tukey (1977). Other classical works on EDA are (Mosteller and Tukey 1977, Velleman and Hoaglin 1981, Behrens 1997).

Most EDA techniques take advantage of graphs, including histograms, density plots, box plots, stem-and-leaf plots, and dot plots. In the modern era, with the rapid development of computers, researchers can explore data using different visualization tools. Now EDA is no longer restricted to conventional methods, rather, it has become a philosophy for data analysis. As Grolemund (2016) pointed out, EDA is an iterative cycle and it consists of generating questions, visualizing and transforming the data, and further refining questions. A wealth of software can help with EDA, such as Tableau, Infogram, Datawrapper, ggplot2 in R, matplotlib in Python, etc.

For EHR data analysis, the summary statistics report mentioned in Chapter 3 is the basic output of an EDA process. The report is EHR specific, which summarizes important and useful information from the extracted data. Although analysts can derive many other summaries from the data, those items in the report are believed to be more informative and meaningful, since they are refined based on our EDA experiments. For example, when investigating the most significant medications for SAH patients, we found that solely ranking the patient count by medications is not enough, because the medication frequency for SAH patients may follow the same medication frequency pattern for the general population, as shown in Table 8.1.

Table 8.1 Top Medications Based on Frequencies in SAH Patients and Cerner General Population

Top Medications in Cerner General Population Top Medications in SAH Patients
Lvp Solution Lvp Solution
Ondansetron Ondansetron
Acetaminophen Sodium Chloride
Sodium Chloride Acetaminophen
Morphine Potassium Chloride
Fentanyl Fentanyl
Lidocaine Labetalol
Midazolam Morphine
Hydromorphone Heparin
Acetaminophen/Hydrocodone Midazolam

Therefore, how to adjust the influence of the general population is also an interesting topic. Fortunately, many statistical tests can be used to generate significant medications for SAH patients. The p-value or risk ratio of each medication can serve as an index to rank the importance of the medications for SAH patients. Methodologies concerning the statistical tests will be discussed in Section 8.3.


8.3 Statistical Models for EHR Data

8.3.1 Contingency Tables

A contingency table (also known as a cross-tabulation or crosstab) summarizes the frequency of subjects classified by categorical variables. For example, Table 8.2 lists the two-by-two table of the number of subjects with/without SAH record versus dopamine/Non_doapmine treatment. Here, Non-SAH patients represent the patients with at least one diagnosis or one medication record but without SAH diagnosis from the Cerner database. The contingency table reveals the relationship between different categorical variables and is an effective function to conduct preliminary statistical analyses for many problems.

Table 8.2 Cross Counts of Dopamine by SAH Patients and Non-SAH Patients

Dopamine Non-dopamine
SAH Patients 991 27304
Non-SAH Patients 210547 17448416

We briefly explain the mechanism of statistical tests on contingency tables. Suppose X and Y are categorical variables, each with two categories, and each row(column) of the table represents one category of X (Y). Then the intersection of each row and each column is called a cell. Therefore, the total four cells (X1, Y1), (X1, Y2), (X2, Y2) and (X2, Y2) in Table 8.3 present all the possible combinations between the two variables X and Y.

Table 8.3 A Contingency Table

Y1 Y2
X1 n11 n12
X2 n21 n22

Assume that a randomly chosen subject from the population falls in one cell of the contingency table with probability πij = P(X=i,Y=j). Denote the marginal distributions P(X=i) and P(Y=j) by {πi+} and {π + j}, respectively, where πi+ = πi1 + πi2 and π + j = π1j + π2j. Similarly, denote nij and pij as

the observed cell count and proportion from the samples, respectively. Then nij and pij follow

$p_{\mathit{ij}} = \frac{n_{\mathit{ij}}}{n},$

where n = ∑I, jnij is the total sample size. For annotation, let ni+ = ni1 + ni2 and n + j = n1j + n2j. The variable X can be called the exposure, and the variable Y can be called the response or the outcome.

For the cohort study, researchers are often interested to know whether the exposure is associated with the outcome. For instance, based on Table 8.2, researchers want to know whether the prescribing dopamine for SAH patients is more likely than that for non-SAH patients is worth investigating. Here, the disease SAH is the exposure and the response is the administration of dopamine. In statistics, we tend to formulate this problem by comparing whether the distribution of Y given X1is the same as the distribution of Y given X2. This is also known as an independence test problem between two variables.

For two-by-two contingency tables, the distribution of Y given X1 follows a binomial distribution. For a subject in row 1, let π1 denote the probability of a success (say Y1 represents the success), then (1-π1) would be the probability of a failure. Likewise, let π2 denote the probability of a success for a subject in row 2. To answer the statistical independence question, the difference of proportions π1 − π2 is our target to test. We can use the Wald test to obtain the P-value, and confidence interval based on the sample difference p1 and p2 and its estimated standard error, where $p_{i} = \frac{n_{i1}}{n_{i +}}$. Readers can find more details of the Wald test in (Agresti 1996).

8.3.2 Chi-Square Test

An alternative test to the Wald test is the Chi-square test. If we are interested in assessing whether the probability of each cell is equal to the hypothesized values {πij} (we call it the null hypothesis H0), a straightforward way is to compare the observed cell counts {nij} and expected frequencies {μij = nπij} where n is the total sample size.

Karl Pearson fulfilled this idea by proposing the famous Chi-square statistic:

$X^{2} = \frac{\sum\left( {n_{\mathit{ij}} - \mu_{\mathit{ij}}} \right)^{2}}{\mu_{\mathit{ij}}}.$

It can be seen that greater differences {nij − μij} lead to larger X2 values; and stronger evidence against H0. The P-value can be obtained by calculating P(X2>observedX2). In order to apply the Chi-square test, the values of {μij} must be known. However, in reality, this may not always be the case. In two-way contingency tables, πij can be simplified through πij = πi+π + j under the null hypothesis of statistical independence. We can estimate the unknown marginal probabilities πi+ and π + j with sample marginal proportions, $\frac{n_{i +}}{n}$ and $\frac{n_{+ j}}{n}$, respectively. Therefore, the estimated expected frequencies are

${\widehat{\mu}}_{\mathit{ij}} = n{\widehat{\pi}}_{\mathit{ij}} = n\frac{n_{i +}}{n}\frac{n_{+ j}}{n} = \frac{\left( {n_{i1} + n_{i2}} \right)\left( {n_{1j} + n_{2j}} \right)}{n}.$

After plugging μ̂ij into the Chi-square statistic, the P-value can be obtained because the Chi-square statistic X2 approximately follows the Chi-square distribution. For every medication, this P-value can be used to indicate the importance of the medication.

Note that Chi-square tests of independence have limitations. The Chi-square approximation is often not precise when the expected frequencies of some cells are less than 5. The hypergeometric test (Fisher’s exact test) can be resorted to address this condition.

8.3.3 Hypergeometric Test

The chi-square test uses the laws of large numbers in the form of the central limit theorem. When the underlying assumptions of the chi-square test do not hold, the hypergeometric test (Fisher Exact test) can be used. Given the total sample size n, marginal row sizes n1+ and n2+, and the cell count n11, the whole table can be determined, which means all the other cell counts can be calculated. The probability of the cell counts has the hypergeometric distribution. The probability of the table is expressed in terms of n11 only, which is

$P\left( n_{11} \right) = \frac{\begin{pmatrix} n_{1 +} \\ n_{11} \\ \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix} n_{2 +} \\ {n_{+ 1} - n}_{11} \\ \end{pmatrix}}{\begin{pmatrix} n \\ n_{+ 1} \\ \end{pmatrix}}.$

where $\begin{pmatrix} a \\ b \\ \end{pmatrix}$ is the binomial coefficient. The P-value is the sum of P(n*), where n11 ≤ n* ≤ min (n + 1,n1+).

The P-value can be utilized as an indicator to rank the medications or diagnosis in EHR cohort studies.

For example, the top medications in SAH patients based on P-value in the hypergeometric test are listed in Table 8.4. The ranks are given by the significant degree of the P-value.

Table 8.4 The Adjusted Ranks of Medications Using the Hypergeometric Test

Medication p_value
Nicardipine 0
Mannitol 0
Glucagon 0
Enalapril 0
Alteplase 0
Polyethylene Glycol 3350 0
Vasopressin 0
Esomeprazole 0
Thrombin Topical 0
Codeine 0
APAP/Butalbital/Caffeine 0
Calcium Gluconate 0
Magnesium Oxide 0
Nystatin 0
Thiamine 0
Papaverine 0
Aminocaproic Acid 0
Balsam Peru/Castor Oil/Trypsin Topical 0
Sorbitol 0
Nafcillin 0
Atropine Ophthalmic 0
Valproic Acid 0
Amantadine 0
Lacosamide 0
Prothrombin Complex 0
Minoxidil 0
Factor IX Complex 0
Citric Acid/Potassium Bicarbonate 0
Coagulation Factor VIIa 0
Vitamin E Topical 0
Amobarbital 0
Urea 2.12E-11

However, because the number of non-SAH patients is large, compared with SAH patients, the P-values of many medications are 0. We can then take advantage of relative risks to readjust the ranks, which will be explained in Section 8.3.4.

In Section 8.3.1, we mentioned the Wald test can be used to test the difference between the proportions π1 and π2. Relative risk is another useful indicator to evaluate the difference, though no common statistical tests are available to test the significance of it. For a two-by-two table, relative risk (RR) is the ratio

$\mathit{RR} = \frac{\pi_{1}}{\pi_{2}}.$

where RR is 1, which means π1 is equal to π2, we can infer that the response is independent of the exposure. The further RR is away from 1, the larger the difference is between proportions π1 and π2. Hence, it gives us more evidence suggesting the success probabilities within the two rows are more different. In practice, we often use sample proportions p1 and p2 to estimate the RR and obtain the sample relative risk $\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}$. For example, in Table 8.2, the sample risk

ratio is 2.94. The sample proportion of dopamine in SAH patients is 194% higher than that in non-SAH patients. Based on the data, it indicates that dopamine may be a medicine that is particularly beneficial to SAH patients. Clinically, it is also true that dopamine is a commonly used vasopressor to increase blood pressure to prevent delayed cerebral ischemia (DCI) in SAH patients. Likewise, we can use the RR to rank all other medications.


8.4 GLM

When determining which statistical models to use for EHR data, an ordinary linear regression (OLR) is a good starting place. It is simple and easy to implement; we can gain valuable information from the data, and provide a baseline result for other advanced models. Next, we will give a brief introduction to the OLR and GLMs.

In OLR, the relationship between the response variable yi and covariates xi is assumed as

E(yi|xi) = xiTβ.

A GLM is a generalization of an ordinary linear model, and it introduces the link function f(x) to extend the relationship as

f(E(yi|xi)) = xiTβ.

Commonly used link functions include the identity, log, and logit functions. If we consider the identity function as the link function, the GLM is just an OLR. If the link function is the logit function,

$\log\left( \frac{x}{1 - x} \right),$

the GLM then becomes the familiar logistic regression model,

$\begin{matrix} {\log\left( \frac{\pi\left( \mathbf{x}_{i} \right)}{\left. 1 - {\pi\left( \mathbf{x} \right.}_{i} \right)} \right) = \mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta},} \\ \end{matrix}$

where π(xi) = E(yi). Logistic regression is a useful tool to model data in which the response variable is binary. Because the expectation of the binary random variable following the Bernoulli distribution is equal to the probability of “success”, π(xi) can be interpreted as the probability of “success” at the value of xi for response variable yi. Equation (8.1) implies that

$\pi\left( \mathbf{x}_{i} \right) = {\frac{\exp\left( {\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta}} \right)}{1 + \exp\left( {\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta}} \right)}.}$

Then the odds of “success” are

$\begin{matrix} {\frac{\pi\left( \mathbf{x}_{i} \right)}{1 - \pi\left( \mathbf{x}_{i} \right)} = \exp\left( {\mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta}} \right)} \\ \end{matrix}$

Hence odds ratio (OR) of “success” equals exp (βp), which means that the odds of “success” when Xpincreases by one unit is exp (βp) times the odds at Xp when adjusting for other covariates (staying unchanged). The odds ratio indicates the influence of one covariate on the odds given to the other covariates in the model. For most predictive modeling in EHR studies, logistic regression is more commonly used.

A generalization of logistic regression is multinomial logistic regression for categorical outcomes with more than two categories. These models can be used for both nominal and ordinal responses. Let us assume the number of categories is J. Let {π1,…,πJ} denote the probabilities of response yi falling into categories {1,…,J}, where ∑jπj = 1, and i denotes the ith subject. The most common model for nominal response is the baseline-category logits model, in which the last category J is selected as the baseline, and J − 1 logit models are built by pairing each response category with the reference category. Each logit model is the linear relationship between the $\log⁡\left( \frac{\pi_{j}}{\pi_{J}} \right)$ and predictors xi. Mathematically, it is

$\log\left( \frac{\pi_{j}}{\pi_{J}} \right) = \alpha_{j} + \mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta}_{j},j = 1,\ldots,J - 1.$

If J is 2, then the multinomial logistic model will be reduced to a binary logistic model as described earlier.

For ordinal responses, we need models to reflect the ordinal nature of the response, hence, cumulative logit models are commonly used for this purpose. Assume the J categories are ordered from 1 to J. Similarly, let {π1,…,πJ} denote the probabilities of response yi falling into categories {1,…,J}, where ∑jπj = 1 and i denotes the ith subject. This model is established on the logits of the cumulative probabilities of the form (yi ≤ j) = π1 + … + πj . It has the following form

${logit}{({P{({y_{i} \leq j})}})} = \log\left( \frac{P\left( {y_{i} \leq j} \right)}{1 - p\left( {y_{i} \leq j} \right)} \right) = \alpha_{j} + \mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta}_{j},j = 1,\ldots,J - 1$

where xi represents the predictors of ith subject.

Besides binary and multinomial logistic regression, log-linear regression (Poisson regression) is another important type of generalized linear model. For more information, Agresti (1996) provides a detailed description of logistic regression.


8.5 Survival Model

Survival analysis can be performed through a wide range of models, many of which focus on the estimation of hazard rate. The most popular survival model is the Cox proportional hazard regression model. Here we will briefly present some basic concepts of survival regression models and introduce the Cox model.

Suppose we are interested in one event, for example, death, recurrence of a disease, the development of one chronic disease. Let T be the time until the event of interest occurs. Two important functions characterize the distribution of t, survival function S(t) and hazard function h(t). The survival function is the probability that one subject is still alive at a specific time point t; the hazard function is the chance the subject experiences the event at the very short instant after time point t.

Mathematically, the survival function is defined as

S(t) = P(T>t) = 1 − F(t),

where F(t) is the cumulative distribution function of T.

The hazard rate is defined by

$h\left( t \right) = \lim\limits_{\Delta t\rightarrow 0}{\frac{P\left( {t \leq T \leq t + \Delta t} \middle| {T \geq t} \right)}{\Delta t}.}$

Clearly h(t)Δt indicates the probability of the occurrence of an event in an infinitesimal time period.

In reality, we often want to relate the time-to-event to covariates x. In EHR, covariates x can include some demographic variables or other recorded variables such as lab tests. The Cox model provides a way to link the hazard function and covariates together. Specifically, its form is,

h(t|x) = h0(t)exp (β′x)

where h0(t) is the hazard at baseline.


8.6 Mixed-Effect Models

When the responses are not independent or we have longitudinal covariates, GLMs fail to fit the data and learn all the structure and information in the data. Here, we introduce mixed-effect models to handle longitudinal data commonly seen in EHR data. We briefly illustrate these models through an example related to blood pressure measurements as a clinical event record.

Assume we want to model blood pressure trends with a few explanatory variables for a specific group of m patients. Let yij, j = 1, 2, …n represent the repeated measurement of blood pressure of ith patient, where j stands for the measurement times. For simplicity, we assume the total measurement times for all patients are the same, namely n. Then the relationship between the response and the explanatory variables is modeled as

yij = xijTβ + zijTbi + ϵij,

where xijT and βdenote the fixed-effects covariates and fixed-effects coefficients respectively, zijT and bi denote the fixed-effects covariates and random-effects vector respectively, bi follows N(0,D) and ϵij denote the measurement error of the jth measurement of the ith patient. It can be seen that the mixed-effect model is an extension of the OLR model. We can interpret the fixed-effect component as the population mean and the random-effect component as the variation from the population mean for a specific subject. In our example, population mean could be general blood pressure patterns, such as one smooth curve centered at 90 and variation could be subject-specific fluctuation around the mean curve. By introducing the random effect, the correlation between repeated measurements of blood pressure becomes evident and is described. Readers may refer to (Wu and Zhang 2006, Diggle et al. 2002), for more details regarding the mixed-effect model.


8.7 Time Series Analysis

8.7.1 AR, MA and ARMA Model

One way of directly modeling the temporal pattern in EHR data is to use common time series models. Time series models are specific types of models for fitting correlated responses, in which the correlation structure deals with the repeated longitudinal measurements. For example, it is reasonable to assume the current value of one lab test, yt, is related to previous measurements, yt − 1, yt − 2, …, yt − p, which are called lagged values (within a certain time duration). Standard time series models are Autoregression (AR) and Moving Average (MA). AR reflects a linear relationship among the measurements of the response at different times, and has the following form,

yt = ϕ0 + ϕ1yt − 1 + ϕ2yt − 2 + … + ϕpyt − p + at

where at is the error term, and p is the order of lag of yt.

MA models relate yt to a white noise series {at}, which means a series of identical and independent distributed random variables with finite means and variance. An MA(q) model is

yt = co + at − θ1at − 1 − … − θqat − q,

where q is the lag order of at.

In some scenarios, the AR or MA models may need high-order lag terms with many parameters to adequately describe the dynamic structure of the data (Tsay 2010). The autoregressive moving-average (ARMA) models are then introduced to solve this difficulty. Basically, an ARMA model incorporates both AR terms and MA terms so that fewer parameters will be used in the model. A general ARMA(p, q) model follows

$y_{t} = \phi_{0} + \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{\phi_{i}y_{t - i} + a_{t} - \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{q}{\theta_{i}a_{t - i}}},$

where {at} is a white noise series, and p and q are the lag orders.

Adopting the idea of the above models with lagged terms, Levine et al. (2018) used lagged linear regression to model the relationship between drugs and lab tests. Denote yt as a laboratory value at sequence time t, and xt − τ represents the drug value at time t − τ. The multivariate lagged drug model can be described as

$y_{t} = c + \sum\limits_{\tau = 1}^{L}{\beta_{\tau}x_{t - \tau} + \epsilon}$

where ϵ is the error term. Variants of this model are discussed in (Levine, Albers, and Hripcsak 2018).

8.7.2 Gaussian Process

Often, because of temporal sparsity and high missing rates of medical data, conventional time series models, such as hidden Markov models and ARMA models are limited to apply. However, Gaussian processes are widely used to tackle irregular temporal observations (Cheng et al. 2017). We briefly introduce Gaussian processes.

A Gaussian process is a collection of random variables, any finite number of which have a joint Gaussian distribution. For the multivariate Gaussian distribution, it can be specified by its mean vector and covariance matrix. Likewise, a Gaussian process which can be represented by the mean function m(x) and the covariance function k(x,x′) as

m(x) = E[f(x)]

k(x,x′) = E[(f(x)−m(x))(f(x′)−m(x′)).k

The Gaussian process then can be written as

f(x) ∼ 𝒢𝒫(m(x),k(x,x′)).

The difference between a Gaussian process and a multivariate normal vector can be understood this way: the distribution of a Gaussian process is the joint distribution of infinitely many random variables, while the distribution of a multivariate normal vector is the joint distribution of the finite vector of random variables.

There are different forms of the covariance function, to illustrate, the squared exponential covariance function specifies the covariance of any pairs of random variables as

$\mathit{cov}\left( {f\left( x_{p} \right),f\left( x_{q} \right)} \right) = k\left( {x_{p},x_{q}} \right) = \mathit{\exp}\left( {- \frac{1}{2}\left| {x_{p} - x_{q}} \right|} \right).$

So the covariance will vary monotonously with the distance between the two time points xpandxq, More details regarding Gaussian processes can be found in (Rasmussen and Williams 2005).

Gaussian processes can be applied to times series variables in EHR. For example, Pimentel et al. (2013) used Gaussian processes to obtain the mean trajectories of systolic blood pressure and temperature for each patient. The mean trajectories from all patients were clustered to detect the abnormal physiological pattern of systolic blood pressure or temperature.


8.8 Variable Selection Methods

The two main purposes for fitting a statistical model are interpretation and prediction. When building a predictive model, the bigger and more flexible the model is, the better and more precise the predicted values will be. However, bigger models and more flexibility compromises interpretation. Therefore, for fitting many of the models introduced in earlier sections, we need tools and methods to select the variables carefully, so that we avoid over-fitting. One particular circumstance where interpretation might turn out wrong or counter-intuitive is when many correlated variables are included in a regression model, known as multicollinearity.

Generally, variable selection methods fall into two classes. One class can be summarized as the variable subset selection with linear regression using ordinary least squares (OLS). In this approach, statistical hypothesis testing for model coefficients is used as a common criterion for selecting the final variables into the model. Approaches such as stepwise and purposeful variable selection are examples of this class. Although OLS can produce unbiased prediction results, it suffers from the high variance of the estimation and unsatisfactory interpretation, especially in high dimension problems. Penalization techniques have been suggested to improve the traditional OLS-based variable selection methods. There are many penalization-based methods, among which the most commonly used methods are LASSO and its extensions, smoothly clipped absolute deviation (SCAD), minimax convex penalty (MCP), etc. In this section, we briefly introduce these methods.

8.8.1 Stepwise Variable Selection

Stepwise variable selection can be classified as either forward stepwise selection or backward stepwise selection. Both methods follow the same strategy of variable selection: add or remove variables within iterative steps. The difference between them is the direction of adjusting the model. Forward stepwise selection starts with the intercept and building up the model, while backward stepwise selection begins with the full model and reduces redundant variables.

The algorithm for forward stepwise selection includes the following 4 steps.

STEP 1. Begin with the null model, contains no predictors and only the intercept (in this case, the mean of the outcome).

STEP 2. Consider all models that augment the previous model with one additional predictor that has not yet been selected.

STEP 3. Choose the model that shows an improvement of the previous model, typically determined by the residual sum of squares (RSS).

STEP 4. Repeat Step 2 through 3 until some stopping rule is achieved. The rule can be the number of variables required in the final model, for example.

Backward stepwise selection starts with a full model including all predictors. All models containing all but one of the predictors are created. The model having the smallest RSS is chosen which contains one less variable. The process is repeated until reducing any more variables from the model causes a statistically significant increase in RSS.

8.8.2 Purposeful Variable Selection

Both stepwise methods work solely based on statistical significance criteria, but are completely ignorant of the practical significance of covariates for regressing a response of interest. Therefore, sometimes variables with a clear clinical relationship with the response are omitted from the model. To overcome this issue, the purposeful variable selection procedure was suggested by (Hosmer and Lemeshow 2000). This methodology uses significance and change-in-estimate criteria for variable selection and allows the analyst to make variable selection decisions at each step of the modeling process.

STEP 1. Perform the univariate analysis to identify potential covariates. The variables that have the P-value < 0.25, as well as variables that are clinically significant, are included for further multivariable analysis in step 2.

STEP 2. A multivariate model is fitted with all variables selected from step 1. Further, the variables with a P-value >0.05 are eliminated from the multivariate model and a new smaller model is fitted. These two models are then compared using a partial likelihood ratio test.

STEP 3. Change-in-estimate criteria are used to identify the confounding variables. Thus, the larger model and the smaller model are compared to identify a change in the magnitude of any other coefficient estimates by 20%, in such cases each dropped variable is added back to the model. This process is repeated several times until all important variables are included in the model.

STEP 4. Each variable not selected in step 1 is added into the model from step 3. All the significant variables identified in step 4 are retained in the model.

STEP 5. The linearity assumption of continuous variables is checked, and appropriate correction is made.

STEP 6. Interaction effects are studied, and important interaction terms are included in the model.

STEP 7. The goodness of fit is performed on the final model.

8.8.3 Sis

All the models described in the earlier sections (except for shrinkage and penalized likelihood methods) assume that the number of observations is much bigger than the dimensionality (number of the covariates) of the model (n ≫ p).

However, in EHR datasets, with thousands of variables and clinical biomarkers available, this assumption is often ignored. This curse of dimensionality causes problems such as collinearity among the predictors, noise accumulation, and computation cost (Fan and Lv 2007). To solve this problem, one way is to use shrinkage methods such as LASSO and its variations. Another method for dealing with this issue is SIS (Fan and Lv 2007) which was claimed to be suitable for dealing with ultrahigh dimensional problems. For a linear regression model

y = Xβ + ϵ

suppose y = (y1,…,yn) is the response vector with dimension n, X = (x(1),…,x(p)) is the n × p design matrix and x(i) is the ith covariate among the total p covariates, β = (β1, …, βp)T is the p-dimensional regression coefficient vector, and ϵ = (ϵ1,…,ϵp)T is the n-dimensional error vector. SIS is performed in two different steps. In the first step, it assumes there are a given number of covariates that influence the outcome while the rest are considered as noise. Let ℳ* represent those significant variables. Then based on the magnitude of the marginal correlation $\hat{\textit{corr}}\left( {\mathbf{x}_{(i)},\mathbf{y}} \right)$ of x(i) and y, SIS chooses the top d covariates to build the set $\widehat{\mathcal{M}}$; in other words,

$\widehat{\mathcal{M}} = {\{{1 \leq i \leq p:{|{\hat{\mathit{corr}}\left( {\mathbf{x}_{(i)},\mathbf{y}} \right)}|}{is\ among\ the\ top\ d\ largest\ ones}}\}},$

where $\hat{\mathit{corr}}$ stands for the sample Pearson correlation. Next, common regularization variable selection methods including Ridge regression, LASSO, and its various extensions can be utilized to further screen the reduced set $\widehat{\mathcal{M}}$.

The key idea of SIS is to regress the response to each covariate. However, it may cause some issues if the assumptions of SIS are not satisfied. For example, some unimportant predictors that are highly correlated with the important predictors can have higher priority to be selected by SIS than other important predictors that are relatively weakly related to the response (Fan and Lv 2007). The iterative SIS (ISIS) is proposed to overcome the weak points of SIS. This contains two steps. In the first step, use an SIS based model selection method such as the SIS-LASSO to select a subset of variables C1. In the second step, it will treat the residuals from the step 1 as the new response and apply the same SIS based method in the step 1 to the remaining variables (x(1),…,x(p)) ∖ C1 which results in a subset of variables C2. Implementing the previous two steps repeatedly until the union of the disjoint subsets C1, …Cl has a size less than n. Readers can view (Fan and Lv 2007) for more details.

8.8.4 Penalty-Based Methods

Penalty-based variable selection methods can be generalized into the following optimization problem:

$\min\limits_{\mathbf{\beta}}\left\| {\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{\beta}} \right\|_{2}^{2} + \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{P_{\lambda}\left( \beta_{i} \right).}$

where ∥•∥2 denotes the L-2 norm, Pλ(βi) is the penalty function imposed on the coefficient βi. Different forms of the penalty function lead to two main types of penalty-based methods, norm-based regularization methods and concave function-based regularization methods.

Norm-based regularization methods include both LASSO, which uses an L1 penalty |βi|, and ridged regression which uses an L2 penalty ∥βi∥22. If Pλ(βi) are some specific concave functions, then the optimization problem can fall into one of the following common variable selection methods: smoothly clipped absolute deviation (SCAD) (Fan and Li 2001), and minimax convex penalty (MCP) (C.-H. Zhang 2010). Specifically, for SCAD,

$P_{\lambda}\left( \beta \right) = \left\{ {\begin{matrix} {\lambda,} & {\mathit{if}\left| \beta \right| \leq \lambda} \\ {\frac{a\lambda - \left| \beta \right|}{a - 1},} & {\mathit{if}\lambda < \left| \beta \right| < a\lambda} \\ {0,} & {\mathit{if}\left| \beta \right| \geq a\lambda} \\ \end{matrix}.} \right.$

For MCP,

$P_{\lambda}\left( \beta \right) = \left\{ \begin{matrix} {\lambda\left| \beta \right| - \frac{x^{2}}{2\gamma},} & {\mathit{if}\left| \beta \right| \leq \gamma\lambda} \\ {0.5\gamma\lambda^{2},} & {\mathit{if}\left| \beta \right| > \gamma\lambda} \\ \end{matrix} \right.$

Among the regularization methods, LASSO is the most commonly used in various fields of science, including statistics, machine learning, econometrics, engineering, etc. One important advantage of LASSO that distinguishes it from Ridge regression is that LASSO has the advantage of shrinking some coefficients to exactly zero when the tuning parameter λ is sufficiently large, thus performing variable selection and reducing dimensionality. This is due to the form of the penalty function in Equation (8.3). Specifically, for LASSO, Equation (8.3) reduces to

$\min\limits_{\mathbf{\beta}}\left\| {\mathbf{y} - \mathbf{X}\mathbf{\beta}} \right\|_{2}^{2} + \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{\lambda\left| \beta_{i} \right|.}$

|βi| will introduce the so-called “soft shrinkage” while ridge regression will shrink the coefficients proportionally (Hastie, Tibshirani, and Friedman 2009). Many extensions of LASSO have been proposed. For example, the Grouped LASSO is one method that addresses the problem where the covariates belong to predefined groups (Yuan and Lin 2006). The Adaptive LASSO adopts a weighted penalty of the form $\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{\omega_{j}\left| \beta_{i} \right|}$ which has oracle properties, namely consistent estimates of the coefficients and correct recovery of the true model (Zou 2006). The Elastic Net method was motivated by the desire to shrink correlated variables to zero and to achieve this goal, it introduces a flexible penalty function $\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{({\alpha\left| \beta_{i} \right| + \left( {1 - \alpha} \right)\beta_{i}^{2}})}$ (Zou 2006).

LASSO can also be extended to GLMs, generalized estimating equations, the Cox model, etc. Since many studies use a logistic model with an L1 penalty to do statistical analysis of EHR data, we present the extension of LASSO to the logistic model. In linear regression models, LASSO adds an L1 penalty to the least square function which is a type of loss function. Hence for logistic regression (8.1), once we determine the loss function, combining the L1 regularization with it leads to logistic regression with a LASSO penalty. The loss function for logistic regression is the negative log-likelihood function. The regularization mechanism for logistic regression (8.1) can be described as

$\min\limits_{\mathbf{\beta}}{n^{- 1}\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{n}\left( {\left. - y_{i}\left( \mathbf{x} \right._{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta} \right) + \log⁡\left( 1 + \exp⁡\left( \mathbf{x}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{\beta} \right) \right)} \right)} + \sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}{\lambda\left| \beta_{i} \right|.}$

If we change the penalty to $\sum\limits_{i = 1}^{p}\lambda\left\| \beta_{i} \right\|_{2}^{2}$, the regularization gives ridge regression. This L2 regularization is also widely used in EHR data analysis. Similarly, the Grouped LASSO, the Adaptive LASSO, and the Elastic Net can also be generalized to GLMs. One major price paid for gaining the benefits of using regularization methods is compromising unbiased coefficient estimates for smaller variance. LASSO, unlike GLMs, does not guarantee unbiased estimates for coefficients however, the latter variations of LASSO such as adaptive LASSO address this issue. Readers may refer to (Hastie, Tibshirani, and Friedman 2009) for a further understanding of the LASSO.


8.9 Divide-and-Conquer Method

All the methods described above are straightforward to apply to big EHR data if we are using well-developed computational packages. However, more complicated approaches such as non-linear models, and joint modes are computationally infeasible for big data. One way to overcome the computational issue is to pair statistical methods with parallel computational approaches and divide and concur methods. In the latter method, the big data is divided, often randomly, to smaller datasets, then the result of any statistical method on all of the methods will be pooled together.


8.10 Validation

Combining the initial variable screening Iterative SIS with any of the proposed methods above (e.g., stepwise, LASSO, and adaptive LASSO) grants us with multiple models for prediction and identifying risk factors, and each of these models exploits a different mathematical and computational criterion for variable selection. However, it is important to compare and evaluate these different models and methods, so that a final predictive model can be selected and used in practice. In particular, for continuous outcomes, the mean squared error (MSE) is a common measure to evaluate the prediction performance. The formula of MSE is

$\frac{1}{\mathbf{n}}\sum\limits_{\mathbf{i} = 1}^{\mathbf{n}}{\left( {\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{i}} - \widehat{\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{i}}}} \right)^{2},}$

where Yi and $\widehat{\mathbf{Y}_{\mathbf{i}}}$ are observed and predicted responses respectively for the n observations. For binary outcomes, the (AUC) of the (ROC) curve is often used to compare the prediction accuracy of different models, which will be introduced in Section 8.10.1.

Many metrics are used to evaluate the performance of different models. For binary classifications, the AUC is the most commonly used measure. Take the logistic regression as an example: after we fit the model, the estimated coefficients of the model can be plugged into the formula of π(xi) (2) to obtain the estimated probability of “success” π̂(xi) for a new observation xi. Based on π̂(xi), we can classify subject i into Group 1 (namely, ŷi = 1) or Group 0 (namely, ŷi=0). For this classification, a threshold value πT is assumed and if π̂(xi) is larger or equal to πT, ŷi = 1; Otherwise, ŷi = 0. For a specific cutoff πT, we can calculate sensitivity and specificity. Sensitivity is defined as P(ŷ=1|y=1), and specificity is defined as P(ŷ=0|y=0). For real data analysis, the proportion of captured positive samples by the positive predictions over the total positive samples is used to replace the true sensitivity probability, which sometimes is also called the true positive rate. The proportion of captured negative samples by the negative predictions over the total negative samples is then to estimate the true specificity probability, which is sometimes called the true negative rate. Here positive samples refer to subjects in Group 1 and negative samples refer to subjects in Group 0. So any value of πT threshold, both sensitivity and 1- specificity can be obtained. For a series of thresholds, we can plot a scatter curve using sensitivity and 1- specificity as the coordinates, as shown in Figure 8.1 which is generated from the SAH study in which we used the patient information to predict the mortality status at discharge. The plotted curve is named the ROC. The area under the ROC is the AUC. For a given spe cificity, higher sensitivity means better predictive power. Hence, the bigger area under the ROC indicates the better predictive performance of the model.


8.11 Results and Examples

Here we use our SAH study as illustrations for logistic and multinomial logistic regression, stepwise, and LASSO. In one project of this study, we examined the first vasopressor administered to 4810 eligible SAH patients (Williams et al. 2020). Using an intention-to-treat approach, patients were grouped into specific treatment groups (either dopamine, phenylephrine or norepinephrine) based on the initial vasopressor received, regardless of whether they subsequently received a different vasopressor. The predictors include age, race, marital status, gender, 750 medications (generic names) administered before the vasopressor treatment, Charlson comorbidity index (CCI), and total IV-fluid administered throughout the encounter. The outcome is in-hospital mortality, which was defined as in-hospital death or discharge to hospice care.

Due to a large number of predictors, we implemented LASSO and stepwise separately to reduce the dimension of covariates and then the logistic regression. See Table 8.5 for the results.

Table 8.5 OR between Different Vasopressors Using Dimension Reduction Methods and Logistic Regression Jointly

Methods

OR

Dopamine vs. Phenylephrine

Dopamine vs. Norepinephrine

LASSO + Logistic Regression

2.00 (<0.001)

0.86 (0.16)

Stepwise + Logistic Regression

1.98 (<0.001)

0.86 (0.14)

In another project of our SAH study, we aimed to predict the risk of mortality for non-traumatic SAH patients using information from the first 24&#160;hours after hospital admissions. The information contains baseline demographic variables, time-stamped medication records, and procedures in the model development process. Finally, a total of 816 patients are included and they are split into train data set and test data set. L1-regularized logistic regression (LASSO) was chosen to predict the risk of mortality due to its property of interpretability. We achieved AUC at 0.84. Altogether, 17 out of 113 variables were included in the prediction model with only the first 24&#160;hours EHR data, 2 increased the risk of mortality, and 15 protected from death, see Figure 8.2.


8.12 Discussions and Conclusions

In this chapter, we reviewed common statistical methods that can be applied to EHR data and discussed the challenges and irregularities in EHR data which make the application of more sophisticated models subtle. For all the models, variable selection and validation methods were included in this chapter, and there are fast and efficient computational packages available, which made EHR analysis possible even for scientists without professional training in statistics. The examples provided in this section are the brief results of two papers based on EHR datasets extracted from Cerner. One investigating the effect of vasopressor on in-hospital mortality of SAH patients, and the other predicting in-hospital death using patients’ clinical records, validating our proposal that common statistical models are often sufficient for EHR data analysis.

Nevertheless, there are still big opportunities to develop new methodologies, because EHR datasets have many unique potentials and unique challenges required to be addressed.

9

Neural Network and Deep Learning Methods for EHR Data
               

Duo Ashraf Xi YuYaseenLuo

This chapter reviews the recent and emerging literature on deep learning methods in EHR data analysis. It covers a select list of methods related to structural data and discusses their advantages and disadvantages in various application scenarios and research goals. Two examples and scripts are employed to illustrate our applications of deep learning techniques for analyzing EHR data.


9.1 Introduction

Deep learning (DL) is a class of machine learning modeling techniques that employs several hierarchical layers of artificial neurons forming a network, often referred to as an artificial neural network (ANN), to learn the complex “rules” between inputs and outputs. The number of layers can range from a few to several folds larger, resulting in a deeply connected neural network. Thus, such models are referred to as deep learning. The history of deep learning can be found in many resources online or in journal articles. Examples of recent reviews include publications by leading researchers in this field (LeCun, Bengio, and Hinton 2015, Hinton 2018, Esteva et al. 2019). This chapter will first present a light overview of DL before shifting the focus to the applications of DL in some aspects of EHR data analysis related to our own applications. Please see (Shickel et al. 2018, Miotto et al. 2018) for more detailed and comprehensive reviews of DL in EHR analysis.

The types of DL model structures have evolved rapidly in the past few years. Though there are an exploding number of types being added to literature, the basic underlying model structure can be characterized in Figure 9.1 as follows.

As shown in the figure example, we use four input variables (shown in light gray), say x1 to x4, to predict a single output variable y (gray). The prediction is achieved in two steps. First, four input variables are mapped to a hidden layer of five hidden variables (dark gray), say z1 to z5, using the following linear function:

z = σ(wzx+θz)

where x and z are vectors, wz is the weight matrix, and θz is a vector of intercepts.

σ(⋅) represents an activation function that can take various forms, including linear, sigmoid, or hyperbolic tangent (tanh) functions. The sigmoid function is defined as

$\sigma(x) = sig(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{- x}}.$

and the hyperbolic tangent function is defined as

$\sigma(x) = \tanh(x) = \frac{e^{2x} - 1}{e^{2x} + 1}.$

Finally, in the last layer, these hidden variables are mapped to a single output variable y using a similar form as

y = σ(wyz + θy).

There is no restriction on choosing the same activation function across layers, though typically they are chosen to be the same, except in the last layer where the activation function is chosen to be compatible with the outcome. A multilayer perceptron (MLP) is a class of feedforward artificial neural network (ANN) which utilizes a supervised learning technique called backpropagation for training. Its multiple layers and non-linear activation distinguish MLP from a linear perceptron. An MLP usually contains more hidden layers than one single hidden layer shown in Figure 9.1. An advantage of choosing non-linear activation functions is to allow MLP to capture non-linear relationships between the input and output. More choices of activation functions will be discussed later. An example on how to implement MLPs will also be presented later.

In general, DL approaches can be divided into three categories depending on their goals:

 

  1. supervised learning aims to find patterns in a set of predictors that forecast outcome variables accurately. Examples include recognizing numbers/texts from images, predicting patient diagnoses, and treatment outcomes. DL techniques in this category achieve the same goal as other classical machine learning techniques, such as logistic regression and random forests.

  2. reinforcement learning employs computer-based agents to carry out certain tasks in repeated trials with rewards and penalties until computer algorithms perform the tasks well. A prominent example is probably AlphaGo, a DL based algorithm that has beaten top human GO masters (Silver et al. 2016).

  3. unsupervised learning aims to search for important patterns in a set of variables without a direct goal to forecast outcomes stored in other variables. The “learned” patterns can also be fed into supervised learning algorithms. This is considered an area that still needs more future development.

 

In the analysis of EHR data, the first category is probably the most developed as in many other DL supervised learning applications, and our example project later will focus on this category. We will also review the advantages and disadvantages of various DL techniques in the next section.

There are several challenges in EHR data analysis that pose, sometimes, lighter burdens on DL techniques than classical machine learning methods.

First, EHR data typically consist of structured (e.g., demographics, diagnosis codes, medications, lab tests) and unstructured data (e.g., doctor’s notes, images), and sometimes the second type can account for the majority volume in terms of storage size. Classical machine learning methods can be effective in modeling the first type of data because they can be effectively computed based on matrix/vector inputs with relatively smaller dimensions. For EHR data with images and texts, DL methods have been well developed with promising results. For example, DL was applied to classifying clinical notes (Weng et al. 2017) and analyzing medical images (Lundervold and Lundervold 2019, Suzuki 2017, Lee et al. 2017).

Second, EHR data contain a huge collection of variables captured by hospital EMR systems. The number of variables to be analyzed could be further increased if considered in conjunction with time variables or medical encounters. Such variables are heterogeneous in nature, including binary, categorical and continuous variables in demographics or encounter tables, diagnosis tables, medication tables, and lab test tables. Classical approaches would require domain experts and statisticians to work together to design and extract important features based on raw EHR data, for example, blood pressure increases or decreases on sequential visits. This step is sometimes called feature engineering in classical machine learning domains. However, due to the complexity of EHR data, this task can be daunting and time-consuming, not to mention that experts can overlook many complex patterns buried in tons of medical encounters. On the other hand, DL can learn useful features from data directly, sometimes surpassing existing features using state-of-the-art machine learning prediction algorithms (LeCun, Bengio, and Hinton 2015).

Third, the EHR data collected from an individual patient are sparse in time and variables. For example, a patient can have no medical encounters for months and then several encounters in a week. Taking the diagnosis outcome as another example, one patient can have only a few ICD diagnosis codes out of thousands in one encounter. These characteristics make EHR unique from traditional study designs where limited pre-defined variables are measured at roughly regular time intervals (at least for predictors). One existing DL technique to handle this challenge is to embed these sparse coded data in a common space that yields promising results (Rajkomar et al. 2018).

Fourth, it is conceivable that there might be complex temporal dependence in medical encounters, because earlier life health (even in childhood) can have long-lasting effects on later life health (Bijker and Agyemang 2016). With this in mind, constructing classical machine learning algorithms with a long-term dependency could be challenging and hard to tune, while several DL techniques have already been developed for modeling long or short-term temporal dependencies (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997, Chung et al. 2014).

Last, EHR is primarily designed for billing and management purposes, and sometimes the detailed medical information (e.g., blood testing results) can be missing or error-prone. DL methods, specially designed with robust modeling techniques (e.g., dropouts), can be less sensitive to these issues than classical methods. One positive implication of deploying DL is that researchers may not need to spend a lot of time cleaning and correcting EHR data, and such efforts can represent the overwhelming majority of the efforts in EHR research.

In the meantime, there are also several limitations of DL methods. One big issue is its limited interpretability. Despite its success in superior prediction performance, DL models may still lack the power to unravel the mechanisms. Some progress has been made to address this challenge, for example (Choi et al. 2016). Another issue is related to model training, wherein one needs to be careful about overfitting and proper choices of model structures and hyper-parameters. Sometimes, good choices come from empirical trial-and-error techniques and experiences. To deal with overfitting, one may also need to resort to training on fairly large data with carefully crafted models.

The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows. We will review existing DL methods for EHR data analysis and describe our DL applications in two examples. Due to the focus of our applications, we will limit our review to those structured data and leave out other applications on non-structured data (e.g., clinical notes) and laboratory measures (e.g., CT, MRI). We will end the chapter with discussions and future directions.


9.2 Deep Learning Methods for EHR Data

DL techniques can be characterized according to their network structures. We will briefly explain a few common DL network structures and present some successful applications.

The basic type of DL model is the multiple layer perceptron (MLP) (Hastie, Tibshirani, and Friedman 2001), which was popular in the 1980s. As the name suggests, it consists of multiple layers of artificial neurons such that the input passes forward through the layers. MLP is usually used for predictions, see (Shickel et al. 2018) for MLP applications in EHR.

The first modern DL method is probably deep convolutional networks (CNN) (LeCun, Bengio, and Hinton 2015). CNN usually employs convolutional layers on input data such that the correlations between neighboring inputs are accounted for in the model, and the data dimension is further reduced by pooling layers such that the output is robust against location shifts in inputs. Applications of CNN include predictions of disease risks (Goldstein, Navar, and Pencina 2016), diagnosis (Haenssle et al. 2018), and readmissions (Nguyen et al. 2017). A CNN model can also be applied in disease prediction by semantic understanding of EHR texts, Figure 9.2.

To deal with temporal inputs, recurrent neural network (RNN) was developed to learn from sequential inputs, usually collected at different time points. To adapt to possible long or short temporal dependence of inputs, long short-term memory (LSTM) (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997) and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) (Chung et al. 2014) were developed to allow automatic “memory”-like capability of linking the past and current inputs together (Figure 9.3). Example applications of RNN include predicting disease onsets (Rasmy et al. 2018, Maragatham and Devi 2019) and readmissions (Rajkomar et al. 2018). Some GRUs (Che et al. 2018) also addressed missing values in EHR time series data.

Another exciting technique in DL is probably generative adversarial networks (GAN). This technique trains two competing neural networks together. One network (generator) aims to simulate as genuine as possible to real data, while the other (discriminator) aims to distinguish simulated and real data points. The applications of GAN in EHR include synthesizing data (Baowaly et al. 2019) and disease predictions (Hwang, Choi, and Yoon 2017) (Figure 9.4).

Choosing DL architectures should depend on the data and modeling goals, different architectures have their own advantages and disadvantages. CNN is helpful for handling spatial and temporal dependence of data, and thus it is a natural choice for images and texts where such dependence is strong. In general, CNN training is fast but it also is restricted to local dependence structures. RNN is good for handling long-range and short-range dependencies. It enables better modeling of long time series with complex dependencies, though computationally it is slower to converge. GAN incorporates two sub-architectures (generator and discriminator) in one framework, and thus choosing GAN may allow users to accomplish more tasks than predictions. For the sub-architectures, CNN, RNN, or others can be employed. In general, however, GAN is much harder to train, and sub-optimal constructs and implementations may lead to poor performance in simulations and predictions partly due to failed convergence. We recommend that users choose the DL architecture based on their goals, complexity of data structures (e.g., dependencies), computational infrastructure, data size, and experience with DL training.


9.3 Deep Learning Software Tools and Implementation

Many software tools for constructing and implementing DL networks are now widely available and often open-sourced. Some popular packages include TensorFlow, Torch/Pytorch, CNTK, Keras, Caffe (2), Theano, MXNet, and H2O. Usually, these packages are being continuously refined over time as they are also being used in many large high-tech companies. There are plenty of tutorials available online or in books. For example, TensorFlow provides online tutorials (https://www.tensorflow.org/tutorials) for users at different levels. These definitely make it possible to quickly develop new DL algorithms for EHR data analysis and predictions.

Based on our own experience, we find that the python packages, TensorFlow and Pytorch, are good choices for EHR data analysis. The most recent version of TensorFlow also includes Keras. Both pros and cons of these two software tools are summarized as follows:

  • TensorFlow is continuously being developed by Google with contributions from the community. It is a mature framework with much community support and can be deployed for production across different platforms (e.g., mobile, servers).

    • Pros: mature, easy-to-use (especially based on integrated Keras in TensorFlow 2), large community support, easy deployment, production-ready, visualization interface (Tensor Board)

    • Cons: less pythonic (especially using basic TensorFlow without Keras), not backward compatible (TensorFlow 2 versus 1, though conversion scripts available), hard-to-install on certain OS platforms with GPU support

  • Pytorch development is led by Facebook and with community contributions.

    • Pros: mature, easy to learn (mostly just like regular python codes and thus pythonic), easy to install, easy to debug, more detailed control

    • Cons: more coding effort (if not using Keras), varying speed depending on customizations


9.4 Application Examples

Case Study 1: Application of MLP for Mortality Prediction

We illustrate deep learning techniques using the application example of the subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) project as described in previous chapters. In this application, we aim to explore the performance of MLP for predicting mortality. Mortality here is defined as death in the next 24&#160;hours. The predictors include demographics, first vasopressor use, medication, procedure, baseline diagnosis, all aggregated from an observational time window on and before admission. In this example, the MLP model was implemented in R using the H2O package, and the model parameters were tuned using the Auto-ML platform built in H2O. The tuning parameters include activation function, number of hidden layers, number of epochs, number of neurons, learning rate, dropout rate, and so on.

Case Study 2: Application of RNN for Heart Failure Prediction for Hypertension Patients

For patients with chronic diseases, EHR data with multiple encounters and long-term follow up are available. RNN is then a good choice of ANN models to take advantage of the rich information coded in longitudinal encounters. However, we should point out that it is not straightforward to apply classical longitudinal models (e.g., mixed-effects regression models), because the outcome variables and predictors were measured at irregular times and the time intervals can be informative of the outcome as well. In this example, we present a project of predicting heart failure (HF) with recurrent neural network (RNN) model.

Experimental Setting

The aim of this study is to combine dynamic variables (variables from diagnosis, medication, procedure, lab test procedure and surgical procedure tables) and static predictors (e.g., age, gender, race, number of visits, time span and visit density) to predict HF onset for hypertension patients. The inclusion criteria of patients in this study are: patients who were diagnosed as unspecified essential hypertension, i.e., at least one of the following ICD-9 codes: “401”, “401.0”, “401.1”, “401.9”. To save computation time, we randomly sampled 40,000 hypertension patients for modeling and prediction of HF onset in this illustration example.

Definition of Heart Failure Cases: The criteria for HF onset was adopted from previous HF prediction studies (Gurwitz et al. 2013, Ng et al. 2016, Choi et al.). To confirm an HF case of a patient, the HF-related ICD-9 codes should appear at three or more clinical visits within 12 months in the patient’s records. The date that the first HF related ICD-9 codes appeared was set to be the onset date of HF for this patient.

Definition of Heart Failure Controls: The hypertension patients who satisfied inclusion criteria, but were not classified as HF cases.

We used Python to process and prepare the EHR data for this project, and we label each patient either an HF case or a control first using the following code:

We define the observation window and prediction window as follows: Observation Window: From the first diagnosis time of unspecified essential hypertension to the time of the last visit before HF onset visit (for HF patients) or the last second visit in records (for control patients), see Figure 9.5.

Prediction Window: Time interval between the end of the observation window and next visit.

The unique identifier of patients in the EHR database, “patientSK”, was used to merge all the variables including discharge date, age, diagnosis, medication, race, heart_failure, HF_time, sex, marital_status, procedure, surgical procedure, and lab test procedure into one data frame. Each patient in this data frame has multiple rows (one row for each encounter). This data frame is referred to as long_version8. To prepare the data for predictions, we remove all the data before the first diagnosis of hypertension. The hypertension diagnosis ICD-9 codes are 401, 401.0, 401.1, 401.9.

Since we intend to use historical EHR data to predict the future HF onset in this project, the patients with only one visit of records are dropped from the cohort. To do this, we ran the following Python codes:

We also removed the data after heart failure diagnosis, which is defined as the HF cases. Python code for this is shown below:

Based on the inclusion criteria and definition of HF cases, we identified 1827 HF cases (4.57%) among 40,000 hypertension patients. Various variables and factors were extracted for different EHR data tables. We extracted discharge time, age, gender, race, and marital status from the encounter table, diagnosis codes and diagnosis identifiers from the diagnosis table, medication codes and medication identifiers from the medication table, procedure identifiers from the procedure table, lab procedure identifiers from the lab procedure table, and surgical procedure identifiers from the surgical procedure table. Table 9.1 lists the types of variables included in our model.

Table 9.1 Predictor Variables and Corresponding Data Types

Variables Type
Age Continuous, Longitudinal
Gender Binary, Cross-Sectional
Race Categorical, Cross-Sectional
Marital Status Categorical, Longitudinal
Number of Visits Integral, Cross-Sectional

Average Duration between Visits Continuous, Cross-Sectional
Diagnosis Identifiers Binary, Longitudinal
Medication Identifiers Binary, Longitudinal
Lab Test Identifiers Binary, Longitudinal
Procedure Identifiers Binary, Longitudinal
Surgical Procedure Identifiers Binary, Longitudinal

Data from different data tables were linked together using the unique patient ID, longitudinal variables were also further coded with the time of corresponding discharge date. After data cleaning and pre-processing, 29441 patients, including 953 HF cases, were included for final data analysis and modeling. The summary statistics of the prediction window were shown in Table 9.2. Some patients’ prediction window is small (only one day) and some patients’ prediction window is as large as 4453 days with a median of 63 days and a mean of 185 days. Control patients have a longer prediction window than those of HF patients.

Table 9.2 The Summary Statistics of Prediction Window (Days) for HF Cases and Controls

Statistics (Days) All Patients HF Cases HF Controls
Minimum 1 2 1
Maximum 4453 3164 4453
Median 63 41 65
Mean 185 143 186
Wald Test P-value: <0.001

RNN Prediction Results

We trained the RNN model based on the source code from Choi E et al.4 The RNN model was referred to as RETAIN which is an interpretable predictive model for healthcare application. Using the ratio of 7:1:2, we randomly divided 29441 patients into training, validation, and test data sets: there were 20609 patients (670 cases) in training data set; 2944 patients (96 cases) in the validation data set, 5888 patients (187 cases) in test data set. We set the mini-batch size as 50 and 100 respectively to train the RNN model. The prediction results from the RNN model are shown in Table 9.3.

Table 9.3 The Prediction Accuracy When the Batch Size was Set as 50 and 100

Mini-Batch Size AUC (%) TP (%) FP (%) Training Time/Epoch (Minutes)
50 64.06 64.17 40.75 13
100 60.75 73.80 53.08 13

The tutorial of RETAIN implementation can be found at https://github.com/mp2893/retain. In this experiment, we implemented RETAIN to predict HF onset for hypertension patients using EHR data. Before implementing RETAIN, we need to prepare the data in the format required by the input of source code from (Choi et al. 2016). The inputs were required to be Python cPickled list, see the code below:

The standard command for RETAIN implementation ispython retain.py <visit file> <# codes in the visit file> <label file> <output path>

In this experiment, we implemented aspython retain.py med_seq.pkl 33704 label.pkl output.txt --time_file duration_seq.pkl --age_file age_seq.pkl --interval_file interval_seq.pkl --visit_file visit_seq.pkl --simple_load --n_epoch 30 --batch_size 50 --embed_finetune 0

In this application example, it is noticed that RNN prediction model only produced moderate prediction performance. There could be several reasons, one is that the prediction window is quite large, which is not easy to predict HF. Some tuning parameters such as the mini-batch size and other hyper-parameters could be further optimized.


9.5 Discussion

Our application experiments demonstrated that DL is flexible in handling various types of EHR data for outcome predictions. This flexibility makes DL a promising and powerful tool for analyzing heterogeneous and time-coded data scattered in different EHR data tables. Such a task can be daunting for constructing traditional statistical models, especially in the early stages of EHR research with limited prior insight for guiding traditional statistical or probabilistic model building.

Despite recent rapid progress in DL application in EHR data analysis, there are still several limitations. Limited interpretability of DL models is one major concern for clinical researchers. As such, one may not translate successful DL models into a mechanistic understanding of the science behind it. This further makes DL models less comprehensible among clinicians and may deter its adoption in clinical practice. On the technical side, the reasons for DL successes and failures remain to be investigated. Though software implementation is less cumbersome, thanks to high-level DL packages, DL models are not off-the-shelf packages like classical machine learning methods where users do not need to adjust or tune the parameters and models on a case-by-case bases. Some critical tasks, such as how to choose the optimal model structures and parameters, may still rely on users’ practical experience, intuition, and some mathematical reasoning. Computing efficiency for DL models is another important issue that requires further research.
