10

EHR Data Analytics and Predictions: Machine Learning Methods
               

Yuxuan Gu, Yuefan Huang, Vi K. Ly, Ashraf Yaseen and Hongyu Miao


10.1 Machine Learning Overview

The meaning of machine learning (ML) might be different for people from different research and application fields. To simplify, here ML refers to a broad range of statistical and computational methods, theories, and tools for data exploration and analytics. In comparison with traditional statistical methods, ML approaches are primarily data-driven and may not rely on a pre-specified mechanistic model. With the goal of extracting hidden knowledge from data, usually the larger the number of samples available for learning tasks, the better the performance of ML algorithms. Thanks to its power and flexibility, ML becomes a popular choice for researchers and scientists in solving a variety of complex problems involving large datasets with a huge number of variables. In general, ML methods may be classified into several categories: supervised learning, unsupervised learning, semi-supervised learning, and reinforcement learning.

As described in Shobha and Rangaswamy (2018), supervised learning mainly adopts two types of techniques: 1) classification techniques, which are used to predict discrete responses (binary or multi-classes); e.g., the risk category of a patient to develop a certain disease or condition, and 2) regression techniques, which are mostly used to predict continuous responses; e.g., changes in a patient’s vital signs (like blood pressure or body temperature). Examples of ML algorithms commonly used for classification and/or regression include k-nearest neighbor (KNN), Naïve Bayes, linear models, bagged and boosted decision trees, random forests, support vector machine (SVM), and neural networks.

Nowadays, supervised learning has been broadly employed in the healthcare fields such as the diagnosis of diseases, disease risk prediction, or mortality/outcome prediction. A few examples include researchers using the rich information in EHR database to predict hospitalization due to heart diseases (Dai et al. 2015), forecasting the risk of type 2 diabetes (Mani et al. 2012), or predict 6-month mortality among cancer patients (Parikh et al. 2019). In the previous two chapters, we have discussed statistical methods, neural networks, and deep learning approaches for EHR data analysis and predictions. In this chapter, we focus on some select supervised learning techniques. We briefly describe these supervised learning techniques and then illustrate their usefulness via an EHR application project on mortality prediction for subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) patients. We will also compare the performance of these ML methods based on the SAH dataset.


10.2 Machine Learning Methods

Random Forest

In order to understand random forest, we need to start by defining the concept of a decision tree. Briefly, a decision tree is a decision-making tool that utilizes a branching, tree-like graph. Let S be the training set of n samples (x1, y1), …, (xn, yn), where xi ∈ X is the input instances, yi ∈ Y are the class labels associated with each xi. A decision tree is a classifier, h: X → Y, that predicts the label y of x by making a series of decisions along a tree structure. A typical decision-tree starts with the root node, the algorithm then assigns this node a label according to the majority vote among all attributes over the training set. As we move from the root node to the leaf node, a series of tests must be performed. For each test, we examine the effect of splitting a single branching node. The entropy (or gain) is used to help the algorithm find the best numerical or categorical feature to split from. The algorithm then looks at the improvement associated with the gain from each split; among all possible splits, we choose the one that maximizes the gain or we just stop if further splitting does not result in an increase in the gain. This process can also end if a pre-specified maximum depth of the tree is reached.

An example of a simple decision tree is shown in Figure 10.1. In order to check if a given patient has a certain disease or not, the decision tree first examines the lab result of that patient. If this result is in the normal range, then the tree immediately predicts that the patient does not have the disease and stops there. Otherwise, the tree moves to examine the medication that the patient is taking. If that patient takes a medication related to the disease, the tree predicts that the patient has the disease.

Many algorithms for learning decision trees have been developed, such as ID3 and C4.5 and CART algorithm. For additional reading, we refer the readers to the work by Hastie, Tibshirani, and Friedman (2009). Here we will look into ID3, which is a commonly used decision tree algorithm (Figure 10.2). Specifically, ID3 is built upon the concept of recursive calls as follows:

ID3 takes a training set S and a feature subset A as input. The algorithm calculates the Gain(S, i), which measures the probability of incorrectly classifying a randomly-chosen element in the dataset if it was randomly labeled according to the class distribution in the dataset. Then, the algorithm evaluates the gain of a split of the tree according to the ith feature and chooses the best split by maximizing the gain. In classification, we calculate the gain using the Gini impurity or entropy.

The decision tree is good for a dataset with a small number of features. However, for a dataset with a large feature space, algorithms like ID3 can return a large size and highly complex tree. Such a tree may predict a training data set well but perform poorly on a testing set, and thus incur the overfitting problem. To address this problem, the random forest (RF) algorithm was introduced by Breiman (2001). RF works by constructing an ensemble of trees. The prediction of the random forest is obtained by a majority vote over the predictions of the individual trees (Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David 2014) (Figure 10.3).

Specifically, given a training data set S = (x1, y1), …, (xm, ym), we will implement a random forest algorithm as follows: First, we sample with a replacement on the training set S to get smaller subsets; then, we train a classification tree algorithm (such as ID3) on all those extracted subsets; Eventually, after we get all the predictions from individual trees, we take the majority vote to get the final prediction. Therefore, the training algorithm for random forests applies the general technique of bagging, or by bootstrapping sampling from the training set uniformly and with the replacement, to decision trees. (James, Witten, and Tibshirani 2013) This bagging method decreases the variance of the model and does not increase the bias. Hence, it helps in preventing the problem of overfitting from decision trees as mentioned earlier.

Extremely Randomized Tree

Extremely randomized tree (ERT) has been introduced by Geurts, Ernst and Wehenkel (2006). ERT is a new tree-based algorithm that introduces randomness totally or partially on splits. In terms of computational cost, ERT can be more efficient than the random forest. When a large number of attributes are involved, the computational efficiency of ERT is even attractive and thus might be a suitable tool for EHR data analysis (for instance, one patient could have hundreds or even thousands of laboratory tests, clinical procedures, and/or medications; therefore, the data dimension is often extremely high). There are two main differences that distinguish random forest from ERT: i) for the ERT algorithm, each split is chosen at random, while each split is optimal in the random forest method. Consecutively, the trees built in ERT are more diverse. ii) each tree in ERT is built by using the entire learning sample while in the random forest, each decision tree is built by a bootstrapped learning sample.

The variance of a single tree can be high and using ensemble methods could reduce such variance. Among the ensemble methods, the amount of reduction in variance introduced by bagging is less than that of random forest and ERT because averaging highly correlated trees (as in the bagging approach) will have less variance reduction than averaging uncorrelated trees. The ERT has a tendency to strongly reduce variance at the cost of increasing bias. In practice, one needs to find the best balance between variance and bias, which is called bias and variance trade-off. Overfitting is a common problem for decision tree models, especially when a tree has many levels. To address this issue, the recommended approach is to use the bagging method described earlier in the section of random forest.

Gradient Boosting

What is boosting? Simply put, it is a committee-based procedure like bagging. In the bagging approach, we calculate the average of all the predictions from bootstrap sampling. Therefore, bagging is parallelizable. However, boosting is a sequential method and cannot be parallelized in the same way as bagging (i.e., the residuals from the previous iteration are inputs to the current iteration). It takes the outputs of many weak classification models (usually decision trees) and combines them to form a more powerful “committee”.

In gradient boosting, we build an ensemble of decision trees in a stage-wise fashion and use a functional gradient descent (FGD) procedure to minimize the loss. In FGD, we parametrize the trees and reduce the residual loss at each step by moving in the direction suggested by the gradient descent algorithm. (Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman 2009).

In the pseudo-code in Figure 10.4, it can be seen that by inserting different loss criteria L(y, f(x))L(y, f(x)), we can do a customized implementation for gradient boosting.

XgBoost

Boosting is a very popular and effective machine learning method and is usually employed as a benchmark approach. Recall that boosting is an ensemble method, but instead of training all models parallelly, it builds models in a sequential manner such that the new models are built to correct the errors made by its predecessor.

Before getting into the details, it is necessary to point out that gradient tree boosting is the same as Gradient Boosting Machine (GBM) or gradient boosted regression tree (GBRT), and XGBoost is the nickname for eXtreme Gradient Boosting. XGBoost was proposed by Tianqi Chen in a research project as part of the Distributed (Deep) Machine Learning Community (DMLC) group (Chen et al. 2015). Boosting can be very slow in training time because it needs to sequentially train and build models; hence, such methods are not easily scalable. Thus, XGBoost focuses on both execution speed and model performance. XGBoost can do parallel tree construction and out-of-core computation for big datasets that do not even fit in available memory. Technically, XGBoost is a regularized method so it can control overfitting and may have better performance. In addition, XGBoost introduces a new sparsity aware algorithm, learning from the data in the optimal way to deal with missing values and to handle different kinds of sparsity patterns in a unified manner (e.g., the sparsity due to missing values, frequent zero entries, or artifacts caused by one-hot encoding). With many attractive features introduced into regular gradient boosting, XGBoost provides a parallel tree boosting solution to many data science problems in an efficient way.

Support Vector Machine (SVM)

The SVM algorithm was introduced for the first time in the “Statistical learning theory frameworks” paper by Vapnik (1995). Cortes and Vapnik (1995) later used SVM for binary classification problems. Later, the SVM framework has been significantly extended to various problems, including regression, ranking and clustering.

In many machine learning tasks, we often face the problem of sample complexity as the dimensionality of the feature space goes higher. The SVM algorithm tackles the sample complexity problem by finding the half-space separators.

We can look at the simple example in Figure 10.5, from which we can tell that the blue and red hyperplanes together separate the example data points of two different clusters (in green and yellow colors, respectively). We call those hyperplane lines the half-space separators.

In order to pick the optimal half-space, we need to maximize the margin of two clusters. From the example above, we would prefer the blue hyperplane since the blue line has a larger margin which means better separation. The margin is defined to be the minimal distance between a point in the training set and the hyperplane.

Hard-SVM is the learning algorithm that will run through the training set to find and return a half-space with the largest margin (a hyperplane that best separates the training set into predefined classes). The pseudo-code for hard SVM is described in Figure 10.6.

Intuitively, w is the margin and b is the bias, hard-SVM searches for the w of a minimal norm among all the vectors that separate the data, for which |⟨w,xi⟩ + b | ≥ 1 for all i. From there, the output of hard-SVM is indeed the separating hyperplane with the largest margin. In other words, finding the largest margin half-space becomes the problem of finding w whose norm is minimal.

Hard-SVM formulation requires a strict condition that the training set is linearly separable, which is usually not the case for real-world data. When the training set is homogenous but not linearly separable, soft-SVM is more suitable. We can view soft-SVM as a less restrictive version of Hard-SVM that can work even if the training set is not linearly separable.

As described in Shalev-Shwartz, and Ben-David (2014), the optimization function in Equation (10.1) in Figure 10.6 enforces the hard constraint yi(⟨w,xi⟩+b) ≥ 1 for all i. To address this, soft-SVM allows this constraint to be relaxed at some data points in the training set. This can be modeled by introducing non-negative slack variables, ξiwith i = 1, …, m, which measures to what extent the constraint yi(⟨w,xi⟩+b) ≥ 1 is being violated. Then, the algorithm replaces each constraint yi(⟨w,xi⟩+b) ≥ 1 by yi(⟨w,xi⟩+b) ≥ 1 − ξi as in function (10.2) in Figure 10.7. Soft-SVM jointly minimizes the norm of w (e margin) and the average of ξi (the violations of the constraints). A parameter λ controls the tradeoff between these two terms. The pseudo-code for soft-SVM is as in Figure 10.7.

We see that a Soft-SVM algorithm uses regularized loss minimization for Equation (10.2) in Figure 10.7 so that it can penalize not only training errors. Therefore, soft-SVM is often used to deal with a homogenous half-space. For further details, please refer to Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David (2014).

For non-linear and inseparable data, we need to use the Kernel trick to help SVM’s to form nonlinear boundaries. How does the Kernel trick work? First, the original data are passed through non-linear maps to form new data in a high-dimensional space. Then, we perform a function to take a dot product of the data after doing non-linear mapping on it. This function is the kernel function (Jakkula 2011). Different SVM algorithms use different types of kernel functions such as linear, nonlinear, sigmoid, polynomial, and radial basis function (RBF). The most popular one is RBF since it localizes and limits the response along the entire x-axis. (Hill and Lewicki 2006).


10.3 Machine Learning Software Tools

There exist numerous software packages that implement the machine learning models and computing algorithms described in the previous section. While we are not able to illustrate all such packages in this chapter, we select several representative packages and demonstrate how to employ them to achieve machine learning tasks using different ML methods such as Least Squares Support Vector Machine with Radial Basis Function Kernel, Random Forest, Stochastic Gradient Boosting, Xgboost, or Extremely Randomized Tree.

H2O

H2O.ai is a visionary open-source software company committed to providing novel scalable machine learning technologies. Besides its web interface, H2O.ai also provides interfaces to R, Python, Scala, Java, JSON, and CoffeeScript/JavaScript. H2O supports commonly used machine learning algorithms, such as random forest, gradient boosting, generalized linear model, deep learning, and more. We use R interface here, which happens to be very flexible when tuning hyperparameters and defining customized constraints (e.g., training time, optimization of hyperparameter search, maximum models to be trained, and stopping metric). The available models are listed in Table 10.1.

Supervised Unsupervised

• Deep Learning (Neural Networks) • Generalized Low Rank Models (GLRM)
• Distributed Random Forest (DRF) • Isolation Forest
• Generalized Linear Model (GLM) • K-Means Clustering
• Gradient Boosting Machine (GBM) • Principal Component Analysis (PCA)
• Naïve Bayes Classifier Miscellaneous
• Stacked Ensembles • Word2vec
• Support Vector Machine (SVM) MOJO Models
• XGBoost • MOJO Models

H2O has a grid search strategy as well, and we just need to specify the search strategy as “Cartesian”. The evaluation metric we use is AUC; of course, there are other metrics available as well, for example, MSE, MAE, RMSE, and deviance.

Caret

The Caret package (Classification and Regression Training) in R is a versatile library that can handle multiple tasks. So far, 238 statistical or machine learning models are available in this package, both classification and regression models included (e.g., AdaBoost Classification Trees, k-Nearest Neighbors, etc.).

The basic process of creating predictive models is,

  • data splitting

  • pre-processing

  • feature selection

  • model tuning using resampling

  • variable importance estimation

More details on the package of Caret can be found in the book Applied Predictive Modeling (Kuhn and Johnson 2013) and a paper in the Journal of Statistical Software (Kuhn 2008).

Tpot

The Tree-Based Pipeline Optimization Tool (TPOT) is an open-source AutoML software package in Python built on the scikit-learn library. The goal of TPOT is to automate the design and implementation of machine learning pipelines using a stochastic optimization process to make machine learning more friendly and accessible to non-experts (Olson et al. 2016).

TPOT optimizes a pipeline by starting from several simple and randomly chosen pipelines (the population). For every iteration of the optimization process (a generation), TPOT makes several copies of the current best-performing pipelines in the population and then applies random changes to them (e.g., adding or removing an operation or tuning a parameter setting of one operation). At the end of every generation, the worst-performing pipelines are removed from the population, and TPOT proceeds to the next generation. After a fixed number of generations, TPOT recommends the best performing pipeline that it has created during the optimization process. TPOT is very user-friendly, even people with no data analysis experience can easily learn and use it. You only need to specify the generations and populations you want to generate.

Auto-sklearn

Auto-sklearn is also an open-source AutoML toolkit, built upon the scikit-learn library. The main goal of this package is to automatically select algorithms and tune parameters. There are 4 data preprocessing methods, 14 preprocessing methods, and 15 classification algorithms included in this package (Feurer et al. 2015).

In our application example, the algorithms we used are logistic regression, SVM, random forest, gradient boosting, and Xgboost. We used 5-fold cross-validation and the same tuning time to compare the AUC between different machine learning algorithms (see next section below). Auto-sklearn is pretty flexible in defining training time and tuning options, and the code is easy to understand, as you can tell from the following example:

This example also shows how to use 5-fold cross-validation. One more thing to mention is that you have to reload the data and use refit function before calculating AUC in order to make cross-validation work properly.


10.4 Application Example: SAH Project

In this section, we share our experience and evaluate the performance of different machine learning methods and packages based on the application example on mortality prediction for subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH) patients from an EHR (Cerner) database.

Prediction Scenarios

For the SAH project, we intend to develop a prediction model for SAH mortality for the following six cases (see Chapter 5 for more details):

  1. Use of first 24 hours EHR data after admission: 1263 patients for training, 545 for testing, 917 dummy variables (predictors)

  2. Use of first 48 hours EHR data after admission: 1665 patients for training, 689 for testing, 930 dummy variables (predictors)

  3. Use of first 72 hours EHR data after admission: 1661 patients for training, 714 for testing, 975 dummy variables (predictors)

  4. Use of EHR data up to 24 hours before discharge or death: 2773 patients for training, 1183 for testing, 1473 dummy variables (predictors)

  5. Use of EHR data up to 48 hours before discharge or death: 2486 for training, 1058 for testing, 1421 dummy variables (predictors)

  6. Use of EHR data up to 72 hours before discharge or death: 2284 for training, 987 for testing, 1375 dummy variables (predictors)

Predictors and Outcome Data

For this prediction, we included variables from medication, procedure, diagnosis, and demographics tables. In the k-fold cross-validation, we chose the specific value k to 5, then we randomly divided our training data into 5 sets, use one of the folds as the validation set and the rest four as training sets to train our model, and do this iteratively until we have used each fold as a validation set once. In this manner, we would have 5 performance measures, one for each fold, and the final performance is the mean of the 5 performance measures. In this project, we will focus on the mortality prediction: ‘death’ coded as 1 and ‘survived’ coded as 0.

Model Training

Training machine learning models can be very tricky and different hyperparameters may yield very different results. Here we list the hyperparameters that need to be fine-tuned and the tuning ranges that were used to train our prediction models (Figures 10.8–10.10).

Result of Outcome Prediction

In our example, we ran six different methods in total and chose from four software packages we mentioned at the beginning of this section. At each time, we set a specific machine learning model in one package, and we used 5-fold cross-validation and reported the area under the curve (AUC) for validation purposes. The results are shown below (Table 10.2).

Table 10.2 AUC (%) from Four Different Software Packages for Non-Trauma SAH Patients with Six Different Prediction Windows

Evaluation of Model Performance

We use the AUC to evaluate the performance of different prediction models and algorithms. The higher the AUC is, the better the model is. When AUC is equal to 0.5, it indicates that the model works just like the random guess.

Figure 10.11 gives a rough idea about how AUC for different models looks like. Ideally, if the model is considered good, then the curve has to approach the upper left corner, if the curve is around the diagonal line from lower left to upper right, then the AUC for the model is roughly 0.5 indicating the model is not performing well. For our model, the results are generally considered good. Most of the AUCs are higher than 0.8, and some of the results are even higher than 0.9. Comparing the results between the six cases, the conclusion indicates that the more data we use, the more accurate the prediction is, which is reasonable (Figure 10.12).

The comparison between packages and methods are more complicated (Figure 10.13). From what we observed in our specific problem, the performances of GBM and the Xgboost (regularized version of gradient boosting) in H2O are consistently better than those of the other three packages, except for the data version “first 24 hours” wherein the performance of SVM in Auto-sklearn is the best. Also, logistic regression does not yield the best performance. H2O also generates better results than the other three packages for random forest except for the data version “first 48”, where the caret model is slightly better than H2O. TPOT gives higher AUC than the other three packages for the logistic regression model except for the data version “cut off last 72 hours”. TPOT also gives higher AUC than the other three packages for SVM model except for the data version “first 24 hours”. From what is shown above, there is no universally best package and the performance of a package will vary by models and by data input. We have to make a tradeoff between running time and performance metrics. Our recommendation is that if you have enough time to try all the packages, then it can give you a better estimate of the best model performance. With that said, any of the packages above may generate reasonable results for your particular projects.


10.5 Conclusion and Recommendation

From what is shown above, there is no universally best package and the performance of a package will vary by models as well as by data input. We have to make a trade-off between running time and performance metrics. TPOT, H2O, and Caret give the results relatively faster, and it takes more time to train models by Auto-sklearn (the default running time is one hour). TPOT is recommended if you want to see the result quickly, and the performance is relatively good. If you have enough time to train the model, it could be better to use GBM and XGBoost in the H2O package to get the best performance. Tree-based models like extra tree and random forest do not perform well in our EHR dataset, and H2O is our recommendation if you want to use tree-based models for your specific project. For R users, H2O and Caret are well documented and easy to use. For Python users, TPOT and Auto-sklearn would be familiar to use if you have experience on scikit-learn. In general, if you have enough time to try all packages, then it can give you a better estimate of the best model performance, but any of the packages above may generate reasonable results for your particular projects.

11

Use of EHR Data for Research: Future
               

Hulin Wu


11.1 Future EHR Research

In previous chapters, we have focused on the approaches and methodologies for structured EHR data for research and scientific discoveries. However, it is more powerful if unstructured EHR data, such as clinical notes, pathological or diagnosis images, and high-frequency data from medical devices and monitoring equipment (EEG, ECG etc.), can be integrated with the structured data for analysis and prediction. In general, unstructured data are more difficult to access since it is not as easy to de-identify and share. Additionally, unstructured data are more challenging to analyze because of their complexity and large volume.

Another trend of EHR research is to integrate other types of data from different sources with the EHR data to improve disease risk predictions, disease diagnosis, and patient care quality. Other types of data may include genomics data, real-time monitoring data from wearable devices, social determinants of health (SDoH) from census, population or community studies, public or government databases, and data from other research projects including laboratory experiments, observational studies and clinical trials. Since these types of data are from different sources or studies, it is challenging to integrate or standardize them with EHR data. These other types of data are typically only available for a subset of patients in the EHR system, selection bias may also need to be considered in the analysis and integration process. Ideally, if any of the data from other sources can help with clinical decision-making for patient care improvement, those data should be used and integrated, and a clinical decision support (CDS) system should be developed to improve patient management. This will also allow for rapid translation of research results into healthcare benefits.

UK Biobank (https://www.ukbiobank.ac.uk/), is a large and openly accessible de-identified database that enables integration of health outcomes (including EHR data from primary care, hospital inpatients, death registry and cancer registry) from 500,000 volunteer participants, recruited in the UK between 2006–2010, with genomic, imaging, biological, physical activity, cognitive, behavioral and mental health, 24-hour dietary recall, and environmental data. This large database that enables the consolidation of EHR data with other data types is an excellent resource for researchers to integrate heterogeneous data for biomedical and health science research. However, this large cohort also has its limitations. In particular, the UK Biobank study is not designed to represent the general population and follow-up visits are sparse. In order to improve healthcare through research, the National Institutes of Health (NIH) of the USA recently initiated a similar research program, “The All of Us” program (https://allofus.nih.gov/) which aims to recruit one million participants and collect many of the above-mentioned data types. However, these data may not be available for analysis until after a few more years.

Patient privacy and data security present major challenges for using EHR data for research and scientific discoveries. To deal with this important issue, in addition to the development of efficient and reliable approaches and methods for data de-identification, the use of simulation technologies to generate synthetic EHR data can help ensure data privacy and protect against data security issues. While it would not be easy to justify the scientific discoveries based on synthetic EHR data, the simulation approach is useful for training, education, EHR methodology testing purposes, and to simulate and investigate the effect of new healthcare policies and intervention strategies. EHR simulation systems have been proposed and developed by collaborations between industry and academic partners. Earlier efforts for EMR simulations used real EMR data to identify patient care models or patterns and generated the patient identities and basic information so that complete EMR data could be synthesized (Moniz et al. 2009, Buczak, Babin, and Moniz 2010). Another simulated EHR data system, termed “Synthea”, was developed using public data (McLachlan, Dube, and Gallagher 2016, Walonoski et al. 2018) such as demographics data from the US Census Bureau, disease prevalence and incidence rates from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, health incidence statistics, NIH reports, Clinical Practice Guidelines (CPGs) and protocols, and medical coding dictionaries. Actual EHR data were not used for Synthea development. This system can be used to simulate the entire lifespans of synthetic patients, but it may not reflect patients’ records in a real EMR or EHR system. Recently, generative adversarial networks (GAN) have been proposed to synthesize EHR data (Choi et al. 2017, Baowaly et al. 2019); however, this model is not used to generate raw EHR data, which are irregular and noisy, instead, it is used to generate a regular data matrix that mimics the variables and factors derived from EHR databases. One obvious challenge is how to evaluate whether a simulated EHR database is good or not since there is no quantitative standard or evaluation method to define a “good” synthetic EHR dataset. To this end, different EHR simulation systems may need to be developed for different purposes. Generally, a good synthetic EHR dataset should be realistic and applicable to achieve the targeted goals, although it is difficult to evaluate the quality of the simulated EHR data.

One of the EHR data sharing approaches is to use the Observational Medical Outcomes Partnership (OMOP) Common Data Model (CDM) developed by the Observational Health Data Sciences and Informatics (OHDSI) organization (https://www.ohdsi.org/). The idea behind this approach is to transform data from different EHR databases into a common format (data model) as well as a common representation (terminologies, vocabularies, coding schemes) from which summary statistics and analyses can be derived and performed systematically using a library of standard analytic routines that are developed based on the common format. These summary statistics and analysis results from different EHR databases could then be shared without sharing the original raw data at individual patient levels, avoiding patient privacy and data security issues. However, one challenge of this approach is that it is not trivial to efficiently integrate the summary statistics and analysis results from different EHR databases and new statistical approaches may need to be developed for this purpose. One approach consists of distributed statistical inference and analysis methods (Zhang, Duchi, and Wainwright 2013, Chen and Xie 2014, Neiswanger, Wang, and Xing 2013, Lee et al. 2015, Wang and Dunson 2013, Battey et al. 2018, Jordan, Lee, and Yang 2019, Fan et al. 2019) which utilize distributed computing and divide-and-conquer strategies to handle the aforementioned Big Data analytic challenge. These methods could be further adapted to combine and integrate the analysis results from different EHR databases in order to make effective statistical inferences, which is recommended for future research.

Cutting-edge artificial intelligence (AI) technologies have recently played an important role in solving healthcare problems. For example, AI technologies have been used in supporting disease diagnosis, identifying disease risk factors, predicting treatment outcomes, recommending treatment, patient engagement, and healthcare administration (Davenport and Kalakota 2019). For some of these tasks, AI performs as well as, or even better, than humans. Most of the AI technologies that are used in healthcare are statistical methods, traditional machine learning approaches, and new deep learning techniques that have been discussed in the previous chapters of this book. These statistically-based machine learning or AI technologies use the existing EHR and other relevant healthcare data to train or fit prediction or classification models to identify the risk factors or best treatment for a disease so that the disease diagnosis can be more efficient and treatment outcomes can be improved (Rajkomar et al. 2018, Shimabukuro et al. 2017). In particular, the deep learning approach was shown to perform better than humans in identifying patterns from medical images for disease diagnosis (Fakoor et al. 2013, Vial et al. 2018). Other AI technologies that we have not discussed in this book include the traditional rule-based clinical expert systems and natural language processing (NLP) techniques. In particular, both semantic and statistical or machine learning-based (including deep learning) NLP techniques are very useful to process the unstructured EHR data such as the clinical notes, clinical practice guidelines (CPG), medical literature and documents. The NLP is still an active research field (Mehta and Devarakonda 2018). The AI technologies could also be employed to automate the EHR data extraction, cleaning, processing, preparation and analysis. In addition, the NLP techniques could be used to automatically generate the analysis report. We expect that this will greatly speed up our effort to translate the EHR Big Data into knowledge and healthcare benefits. However, due to many implementation barriers, ethical issues and regulation problems, there still is a long way to go before AI technologies can replace clinicians and human caregivers.


11.2 Post-Research Practice

The ultimate goal of biomedical and health science research is to improve the health of people. In previous chapters, we presented detailed methodologies for each of the steps of the research procedure (see Chapter 1, Section 1.2) based on the EHR data. From a research perspective, the research cycle is concluded after the results are published. However, we believe that this is not the end of EHR research since the research results must be evaluated to determine their practical benefit on patient care and improvement in health. In order to further promote the practical use of the real-world evidence based on the EHR data, we propose the following “post-research practice”:

 

  1. Design a randomized trial for further validation and confirmation if feasible: Randomized controlled trials (RCT) are still the gold standard in evaluating the effect of a treatment, medication and other interventions. If it is feasible, ideally, the results and conclusions derived from the retrospective EHR data should be validated and confirmed by a well designed, randomized and prospective clinical trial. The EHR data and data analysis results can be used to inform the design of the RCTs and some methodologies may need to be developed for this purpose. We also recognize that sometimes it is not practical or feasible to perform a clinical trial due to many reasons such as ethical issues, difficulty in patient recruitment, and implementation barriers.

  2. Design and develop user-friendly tools for implementation and practice: To allow clinicians and patient caregivers to use the research results based on the EHR data in clinical practice and to improve patient care outcomes, it is necessary to develop a user-friendly tool or clinical decision support (CDS) system to implement the analysis, modeling and prediction algorithms for clinical practice. This may also require the involvement of professional software tool developers in order to make the tools follow the industry standard.

  3. Deployment and practical use: After the software tool or clinical decision support system is developed, one may need to find a hospital or clinic to test the software tool or CDS system in a practical setting.

  4. User feedback and further evaluation: User feedback and evaluation need to be collected to further improve the design of the software tool or CDS system. Any bugs or problems can be fixed based on the user’s feedback. If necessary, a formal randomized trial may be designed and performed to evaluate the effectiveness of the CDS system in terms of clinical outcomes.

  5. Iterative improvement: The above research and post-research practice procedures may need to be iteratively updated so that continuous improvement can be done during clinical practice.

 

We expect that the results and clinical evidence derived from EHR data can be quickly translated into impactful benefits for patient care. In order to shorten the time delay from research to clinical practice, a close collaboration among researchers, clinical investigators, caregivers, industry partners, and government regulatory agencies is required.


11.3 Summary

The use of EHR data for research and scientific discoveries is still in its infancy. It is not only very challenging from a privacy perspective to make the data available for scientific research, many data integration and statistical methodology issues still need to be addressed. State-of-the-art data science approaches, computational algorithms and AI technologies are also necessary to harness and translate the EHR Big Data into accessible practical knowledge. Many investigators, including the authors of this book, are actively developing these cutting-edge methods and technologies to meet the needs and challenges in interrogating the EHR data.
